so that the artificial Neumann conditions for
q
in (
1.6.7
) do not pass to the limit. Of course, regularization does not come without its downsides, and a good amount of work is needed to deal with technical complications it introduces. We now turn to a somewhat more detailed account of how we use (
1.6.7
) to complete the linear analysis.

We study a priori estimates for the principal part and its regularization in tandem; for the former the goal is to develop the desired tame estimates, but for the latter the goal is high regularity
N-
independent estimates with only a weaker form of tameness with respect to the background tuple
(q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0})
. The starting point for the principal part is estimates for weak solutions, which are proved in the usual manner modulo some minor technical complications due to some nonlinear expressions involving members of anisotropic Sobolev spaces. See Proposition
5.2
for more details. With the weak estimates in hand, we then turn our attention to high regularity estimates for strong solutions. The idea here is to exploit the fact that we can apply tangential derivatives to the system (
1.6.6
) without changing the basic structure of the equations; this allows us to employ the weak solution estimates to get bounds on these tangential derivatives as in Theorem
6.5
. After we achieve this control of the tangential derivatives, it remains to recover control of the normal, or vertical derivatives. Here we implement a version of the classic technique of Matsumura and Nishida
[
75
]
, originally developed for fixed domains with Dirichlet boundary conditions, to reveal a subtle dissipative structure for the normal derivatives of the density. This, together with some supplementary analysis of steady transport equations (see Proposition
4.4
), provides an estimate on the high norms of the solution in terms of the data and the tangentially differentiated solution alone. This is recorded in the first conclusion of Theorem
6.15
. Synthesizing, we then derive the first conclusion of Theorem
6.16
, which is the desired a priori estimates for the principal part equations.

The strategy for the a priori estimates of the regularized problem (
1.6.7
) is similar at a bird’s eye view, although the specifics of the argument are rather distinct. We again start by proving a priori estimates for weak solutions and then study the equations satisfied by the tangentially differentiated solution: see Proposition
5.4
and the second conclusion of Theorem
6.5
, respectively. The argument used to derive estimates for the normal system, the second conclusion of Theorem
6.15
, now substantially deviates from that used for the principal part case because of the need to pass through analysis of the regularized steady transport equations. It is here that the regularization
L_{m}
serves as a liability rather than an asset. Indeed, deriving estimates with good dependence on
N
is technically delicate and requires a very careful use of the bilinear form associated to
L_{m}
and the precise Neumann boundary conditions imposed on
q
. The majority of Section
4
is devoted to this regularized transport analysis. In the end, we obtain the uniform in
N
estimates as stated in the second conclusion of Theorem
6.16
.

The existence theory for the regularized problem (
1.6.7
) for all
\tau\in[0,1]
is developed by first establishing the existence of weak solutions in Theorem
5.5
. This is proved through the method of continuity, based on the a priori estimates from Proposition
5.4
and an existence result for the problem with
\tau=0
, which itself requires a further two-parameter regularization and compactness argument to establish. The weak solutions are then promoted to strong solutions in Corollary
5.6
via elliptic regularity arguments. We emphasize that while this result shows that (
1.6.7
) induces an isomorphism, it does not establish
N-
uniform estimates. The existence theory for the principal part problem (
1.6.6
) is then established by way of the regularized existence theory, the
N-
uniform a priori estimates, and another weak limiting argument. This is our Theorem
6.17
.

For the conclusion of the linear analysis, we combine the work that allowed us to identify the principal part equations, namely the splitting of the derivative, with the previously discussed principal part analysis on estimates and existence. This is the synthesis of linear analysis result of Theorem
6.18
.

We conclude with a couple remarks on our linear strategy. First, we emphasize that our existence strategy for (
1.6.6
) intentionally bypasses direct regularity promotion of weak solutions in favor of high regularity inherited by weak limits of solutions to the regularized equations (
1.6.7
). This is advantageous since the mixed elliptic-hyperbolic nature of (
1.6.6
) creates substantial technical difficulties in attempting to implement the standard techniques for regularity promotion (e.g. finite differences or mollification and commutators). The second feature we wish to highlight is that our methods of handling the free surface unknown in the linear existence theory are significantly different and simpler than in the previous work on the incompressible problem
[
60
,
97
,
57
]
. In these works the free surface unknown is constructed in terms of the data alone via a pseudodifferential equation, the symbol for which is inverted after a careful asymptotic analysis. In our work, we entirely circumvent the use of these delicate pseudodifferential techniques in our existence theory, replacing them with regularizations, a priori estimates, weak limits, and a new spatial characterization of the anisotropic Sobolev spaces
\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Conclusion and appendices.
Once we have completed the nonlinear and linear analysis, the hypotheses of our Nash-Moser theorem are verified. We then invoke the result with a few minor additional arguments in Theorems
7.1
and
7.3
to complete the proof of well-posedness.

The remainder of the paper consists of four appendices. These mostly consist of various analytical and PDE tools that are customized or optimized for our particular needs in the paper. However, some of the results there appear to be new and may be of independent interest for use in other problems. Appendix
A
records tools related to standard Sobolev spaces. In contrast, Appendix
B
is concerned with properties of the nonstandard spaces we employ in this paper, namely the anisotropic spaces and the adapted spaces. Appendix
C
focuses on PDE tools, with an emphasis on the specified divergence problem in various contexts. Appendix
D
contains a selection of nonlinear analysis tools that are essential in our abstract tame analysis.

1.7.
Conventions of notation

We recall that we have included a
Notation Index
at the end of the paper, which catalogs the numerous operators, function spaces, and other symbols used throughout.

We write
\mathbb{N}
for the set of nonnegative integers,
\mathbb{N}^{+}=\mathbb{N}\setminus\{{0}\}
, and
\mathbb{R}^{+}=(0,\infty)
. Whenever
\alpha\lesssim\beta
appears in a result, it means there is a constant
C\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
depending only on the parameters mentioned in the formulation of the result such that
\alpha\leqslant C\beta
. To emphasize this dependence, we will sometimes write
\alpha\lesssim_{a,\dotsc,b}\beta
to indicate the parameters
a,\dotsc,b
. We will also write
\alpha\asymp\beta
to mean
\alpha\lesssim\beta
and
\beta\lesssim\alpha
. We will use the bracket notation

(1.7.1)

\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{p}\rangle=(1+x_{1}^{2}+\cdots+x_{p}^{2})^{1/2}

for
p\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
and
x_{1},\dots,x_{p}\in\mathbb{R}
. Given
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we will often abbreviate
\langle x\rangle=\langle x_{1},\dotsc,x_{d}\rangle
. If
U
and
V
are some open sets we write
U\Subset V
to mean that the closure
\overline{U}
is compact and
\overline{U}\subset V
.

We denote the gradient and its tangential counterpart by
\nabla=(\partial_{1},\dots,\partial_{n})
and
\nabla_{\|}=(\partial_{1},\dots,\partial_{n-1})
, respectively. The divergence and tangential divergence operators are written
\nabla\cdot f=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\partial_{j}(f\cdot e_{j})
and
(\nabla_{\|},0)\cdot f=\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\partial_{k}(f\cdot e_{k})
for appropriate
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-valued functions
f
. We will also use subscript ‘
\|
’ to indicate that a differential operator depends only on
\partial_{1},\dots,\partial_{n-1}
, e.g.
\Delta_{\|}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{n-1}\partial_{\ell}^{2}
.

If
\lVert\cdot\rVert
is a norm on a product of normed spaces,
X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{q}
, we will typically write
\lVert x_{1},\dots,x_{q}\rVert
in place of
\lVert(x_{1},\dots,x_{q})\rVert
, where
x_{i}\in X_{i}
for
i\in\{1,\dotsc,q\}
. Given
\Lambda\subseteq\mathbb{R}
, we say a decreasing collection of Banach spaces
\{{X_{s}}\}_{s\in\Lambda}
with norms
\{{\lVert\cdot\rVert_{X_{s}}}\}_{s\in\Lambda}
is log-convex if for all
s_{0},s_{1},s\in\Lambda
such that
s_{0}<s_{1}
and
s=(1-\sigma)s_{0}+\sigma s_{1}
for some
\sigma\in[0,1]
we have that

(1.7.2)

\lVert x\rVert_{X_{s}}\lesssim_{s_{0},s_{1},\sigma}\lVert x\rVert_{s_{0}}^{1-\sigma}\lVert x\rVert_{s_{1}}^{\sigma}\text{ for all }x\in X_{s_{1}}.

Suppose that
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is open, and
V
is a finite dimensional real vector space. For
p\in[1,\infty]
, we write
W^{k,p}(U;V)
for the usual
L^{p}
-based Sobolev space of order
k
with functions valued in
V
, and we write
H^{k}(U;V)=W^{k,2}(U;V)
. We write
C^{k}_{b}(U;V)=C^{k}(U;V)\cap W^{k,\infty}(U;V)
,
endowed with the obvious norm. The space
C^{k}_{0}(U;V)
is the closure of
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
in
C^{k}_{b}(U;V)
. We also write
C^{\infty}_{b}(U;V)=\bigcap_{k\in\mathbb{N}}C^{k}_{b}(U;V)
and
C^{\infty}_{0}(U;V)=\bigcap_{k\in\mathbb{N}}C_{0}^{k}(U;V)
. Similarly, we write
H^{\infty}(U;V)=\bigcap_{k\in\mathbb{N}}H^{k}(U;V)
and
W^{\infty,\infty}(U;V)=\bigcap_{k\in\mathbb{N}}W^{k,\infty}(U;V)
. Note that
W^{\infty,\infty}(U;V)=C^{\infty}_{b}(U;V)
.

For
S\in\{{\Sigma_{0},\Sigma}\}
, we write
\mathrm{Tr}_{S}
for the trace operator that maps appropriate functions defined on
\Omega
to functions on
S
. The following closed subspace of
H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
is frequently used:

(1.7.3)

{{}_{0}}H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})=\{{u\in H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})\;:\;\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}(u)=0}\}.

The Fourier transform, which we normalize to be unitary on
L^{2}
, is denoted by
\mathscr{F}
. We will utilize the homogeneous Sobolev space of order
-1
, which is defined as

(1.7.4)

\dot{H}^{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\{{f\in\mathscr{S}^{\ast}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})\;:\;\mathscr{F}[f]\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{{0}\};\mathbb{C})\text{ and }[{f}]_{\dot{H}^{-1}}<\infty}\}

for the seminorm
[{f}]_{\dot{H}^{-1}}=\lVert\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{{0}\}}|\cdot|^{-1}\mathscr{F}[f]\rVert_{L^{2}}
. Finally, we emphasize that we frequently identify
\Sigma
and
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
in the canonical way when working with function spaces defined on
\Sigma
.

2.
A variation on the Nash-Moser inverse function theorem

As we discussed in Section
1.6
, our strategy for proving the well-posedness of the system (
1.4.9
) is to employ a new version of the Nash-Moser inverse function theorem. The goal of this section is to prove this theorem and develop its abstract framework, which will be employed generally in all of our subsequent analysis. Before we begin, we provide a brief overview of the Nash-Moser strategy and some variants of the theorem that have been developed in the literature.

The abstract setting of the classical inverse function theorem is
C^{1}
maps
\Psi:U\to F
, where
E
and
F
are Banach spaces and
U\subseteq E
is an open set, for which
D\Psi(u)\in\mathcal{L}(E;F)
is invertible for some
u\in U
. The typical proof leverages the invertibility of
D\Psi(u)
to prove the local bijectivity of
\Psi
by way of the contraction mapping principle. In particular, surjectivity is established via a Picard iteration scheme that crucially employs the map
D\Psi(u)^{-1}
.

The Nash-Moser approach aims to handle the case when
D\Psi(u)
fails to be invertible but remains ‘nearly invertible’ in the sense that it admits a right inverse
L(u)
, defined on
F
but only mapping into some larger vector space
E_{0}\supset E
. This phenomenon is typically regarded as ‘derivative loss,’ as in practice
E_{0}
often consists of functions, and the subspace
E
consists of functions of higher regularity than those in
E_{0}
. To fully take advantage of this, the Nash-Moser strategy generalizes the setting of the standard inverse function theorem to maps between one-parameter scales of Banach spaces, say
\{E^{s}\}_{s\in S}
and
\{F^{s}\}_{s\in S}
for some
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}
, where roughly speaking one should think of the parameter
s
as measuring the regularity of the elements of the space. The derivative loss is then required to be uniform along the scale in the sense that
D\Psi
maps from
E^{s+\mu}
to
F^{s}
, for some
\mu>0
measuring the extent of the derivative loss, but its right inverse
L
(which is now required to exist in the entirety of an appropriate open set) only maps from
F^{s}
to
E^{s}
. See Figure
4
for a diagram. The profound idea of Nash
[
81
]
, which was expanded upon by Moser
[
80
]
, was to establish local surjectivity not via Picard iteration, which is not available due to the derivative loss, but instead with an iteration scheme, based on Newton’s method, that employs a family of smoothing operators that increase the regularity along the Banach scales. The extreme speed of convergence of Newton’s method is needed to make the derivative loss and smoothing operators cooperate, and in order to properly implement this idea various precise quantitative estimates are required for the map and the smoothing operators.

The above approach is extremely flexible and customizable, and has thus become more of a strategy than a specific theorem. Indeed, many variants of the Nash-Moser theorem have appeared in the literature. Most of these are really local surjectivity theorems rather than full inverse function theorems, which must establish injectivity as well as continuity or higher regularity of the induced local inverse map. We are aware of a few exceptions in the literature. Sergeraert
[
93
]
and Hamilton
[
40
]
prove smoothness of the inverse map by working in the more restrictive context of smooth tame maps between Fréchet spaces. Berti, Bolle, and Procesi
[
10
]
study an implicit function theorem with parameters, and they prove continuous differentiability of the local solution map only with respect to a finite dimensional space of parameters. Ekeland
[
30
]
and Ekeland and Séré
[
31
]
prove an inverse function theorem and deduce some suboptimal Lipschitz estimates of the inverse map.

Unfortunately, the well-posedness problem for (
1.4.9
) is not amenable to any of the above results, so we have endeavored to develop a new Nash-Moser inverse function theorem that works well for our problem, produces solutions in optimal spaces, provides a regularity promotion mechanism, and provides an optimal continuity and even some higher regularity results for the inverse map. Our approach is to synthesize ideas from Hamilton
[
40
]
with the iteration scheme of Baldi and Haus
[
5
]
, which is an improvement on the results of Hörmander
[
42
,
43
,
44
]
that employs ideas from Littlewood-Paley theory.

When compared to the standard inverse function theorem, the Nash-Moser variants have hypotheses that are much more involved, but are similar at a high level. To guarantee a local inverse for a nonlinear operator, one needs to check that: 1) the nonlinear operator is defined on tame scales of Banach spaces; 2) the operator is tamely
C^{2}
, with a fixed derivative loss; and, 3) the derivative of the operator admits a tame family of inverses in an open neighborhood of a point. As all of these hypotheses involve the adjective ‘tame,’ we devote Section
2.1
to defining tame structures and developing a calculus of tame maps. Once this is done, we use Section
2.2
to state the precise hypotheses and conclusions of our version of the Nash-Moser inverse function theorem. Afterward, in Sections
2.3
,
2.4
, and
2.5
the theorem is then proved in several parts. For the reader looking to take the inverse function theorem as a ‘black box’ and more readily proceed to the analysis of the PDE (
1.4.9
), we suggest restricting to the abstract tame structure and the statement of our Nash-Moser theorem, but initially skipping over Sections
2.3
,
2.4
, and
2.5
.

2.1.
Tame structure abstraction

In this subsection we are first concerned with tame mappings between scales of Banach spaces. Our presentation here primarily inspired by Hamilton
[
40
]
and Baldi and Haus
[
5
]
. Our initial concern is scales of Banach spaces, starting with some notation for indexing them.

Definition 2.1

(Subsets of
\mathbb{N}
)
.

Given
N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{{\infty}\}
we define
\llbracket N\rrbracket\subseteq\mathbb{N}
to be the set
\llbracket N\rrbracket=\{{n\in\mathbb{N}\;:\;n\leqslant N}\}
.

Now we define scales of Banach spaces.

Definition 2.2

(Banach scales)
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}\cup\{{\infty}\}
and let
\mathbf{E}=\{E^{s}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
be a collection of Banach spaces over a common field, either
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
.

(1)

We say that
\mathbf{E}
is a Banach scale if for each
s\in\llbracket N-1\rrbracket
we have the non-expansive inclusion
E^{s+1}\hookrightarrow E^{s}
, i.e.
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{E^{s}}\leqslant\lVert\cdot\rVert_{E^{s+1}}
.

(2)

If
\mathbf{E}
is a Banach scale, then we define the scale’s terminal space to be
E^{N}=\bigcap_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}E^{s}
and endow it with the Fréchet topology induced by the collection of norms
\{\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{E^{s}}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
. Note that if
N<\infty
, then
E^{N}
has the standard Banach topology from its norm.

(3)

If
\mathbf{E}
is a Banach scale, then we write
B_{E^{r}}(u,\delta)\subseteq E^{r}
for the
E^{r}
-open ball of radius
\delta>0
, centered at
u\in E^{r}
.

(4)

If
\mathbf{E}
is a Banach scale and
E^{N}
is dense in
E^{s}
for each
s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
, then we say that
\mathbf{E}
is terminally dense.

We note that finite scales of Banach spaces correspond to the case
N\in\mathbb{N}
in this definition. Next we record a quick remark regarding the product of Banach scales.

Remark 2.3

(Products of Banach scales)
.

Suppose that
\mathbf{E}_{i}=\{E^{s}_{i}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
is a Banach scale for
i\in\{1,\dotsc,n\}
, each over the same field. Then
\mathbf{F}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{E}_{i}=\{\prod_{i=1}^{n}E^{s}_{i}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
is a Banach scale when each
\prod_{i=1}^{n}E^{s}_{i}
is endowed with the norm

(2.1.1)

\left\lVert u_{1},\dotsc,u_{n}\right\rVert_{\prod_{i=1}^{n}E^{s}_{i}}=\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\lVert u_{i}\right\rVert_{E^{s}_{i}}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}.

We now consider tame mappings between Banach scales in our next definition.

Definition 2.4

(Tame maps)
.

Let
\mathbf{E}=\{{E^{s}}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
and
\mathbf{F}=\{{F^{s}}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
be Banach scales over the same field,
\mu,r\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
with
\mu\leqslant r
,
U\subseteq E^{r}
be an open set, and
P:U\to F^{0}
.

(1)

We say that
P
satisfies tame estimates of order
\mu
and base
r
(with respect to the Banach scales
\mathbf{E}
and
\mathbf{F}
) if for all
\llbracket N\rrbracket\ni s\geqslant r
, there exists a constant
C_{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that for all
f\in U\cap E^{s}
we have the inclusion
P(f)\in F^{s-\mu}
as well as the estimate

(2.1.2)

\lVert P(f)\rVert_{F^{s-\mu}}\leqslant C_{s}\langle\lVert f\rVert_{E^{s}}\rangle,

where
\langle\cdot\rangle
is defined by (
1.7.1
).

(2)

We say that
P
is
\mu
-tame with base
r
if for each
f\in U
, there exists an
E^{r}
-open set
V\subseteq U
such that
f\in V
and the restricted map
P\restriction V:V\to F^{0}
satisfies tame estimates of order
\mu
and base
r
.

(3)

We say that
P
is strongly
\mu
-tame with base
r
if on every
E^{r}
-open and bounded subset
V\subseteq U
, the restricted map
P\restriction V
satisfies tame estimates of order
\mu
and base
r
.

(4)

We say that
P
is (strongly)
\mu
-tamely
C^{0}
with base
r
if
P
is (strongly)
\mu
-tame with base
r
and if for every
\llbracket N\rrbracket\ni s\geqslant r
we have that
P:U\cap E^{s}\to F^{s-\mu}

is continuous as a map from
E^{s}
to
F^{s-\mu}
. The collections of such maps will be denoted by
T^{0}_{\mu,r}(U,\mathbf{E};\mathbf{F})
and
{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{0}_{\mu,r}(U,\mathbf{E};\mathbf{F})
, respectively.

(5)

For
k\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
we say that
P
is (strongly)
\mu
-tamely
C^{k}
with base
r
if for all
\llbracket N\rrbracket\ni s\geqslant r
the map
P:U\cap E^{s}\to F^{s-\mu}
is
C^{k}
and for all
j\in\{{0,1,\dots,k}\}
the
j^{\mathrm{th}}
derivative map, thought of as mapping
D^{j}P:U\times\prod_{\ell=1}^{j}E^{r}\to F^{0}
, is (strongly)
\mu
-tame with base
r
with respect to the Banach scales
\mathbf{E}^{1+j}
and
\mathbf{F}
. In other words,
D^{j}P\in T^{0}_{\mu,r}(U\times\prod_{\ell=1}^{j}E^{r},\mathbf{E}^{1+j};\mathbf{F})
or, in the strong case,
D^{j}P\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{0}_{\mu,r}(U\times\prod_{\ell=1}^{j}E^{r},\mathbf{E}^{1+j};\mathbf{F})
. The collections of such maps will be denoted by
T^{k}_{\mu,r}(U,\mathbf{E};\mathbf{F})
and
{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{k}_{\mu,r}(U,\mathbf{E};\mathbf{F})
, respectively.

(6)

We denote the collections of (strongly)
\mu
-tamely
C^{\infty}
with base
r
maps by
T^{\infty}_{\mu,r}(U,\mathbf{E};\mathbf{F})=\bigcap_{k\in\mathbb{N}}T^{k}_{\mu,r}(U,\mathbf{E};\mathbf{F})
and
{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{\mu,r}(U,\mathbf{E};\mathbf{F})=\bigcap_{k\in\mathbb{N}}{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{k}_{\mu,r}(U,\mathbf{E};\mathbf{F})
.

(7)

When
U=E^{r}
we will often use the abbreviated notation
T^{k}_{\mu,r}(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{F})
and
{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{k}_{\mu,r}(\mathbf{E};\mathbf{F})
in place of
T^{k}_{\mu,r}(E^{r},\mathbf{E};\mathbf{F})
and
{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{k}_{\mu,r}(U,\mathbf{E};\mathbf{F})
, respectively.

The following result is a useful characterization of tameness when there is multilinear structure present in the map.

Lemma 2.5

(Multilinearity and tame maps)
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
and
r,\mu\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
with
\mu\leqslant r
. Let
\mathbf{F}=\{{F^{s}}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
,
\mathbf{G}=\{{G^{s}}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
, and
\mathbf{E}_{j}=\{{E^{s}_{j}}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
, for
j\in\{{1,\dots,k}\}
, be Banach scales over the same field. Let
U\subseteq G^{r}
be an open set. Then the following are equivalent for all maps
P:U\times\prod_{j=1}^{k}E^{r}_{j}\to F^{0}
such that
P(g,\cdot)
is
k
-multilinear for all
g\in U
.

(1)

P\in T^{0}_{\mu,r}(U\times\prod_{j=1}^{k}E^{r}_{j};\mathbf{G}\times\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathbf{E}_{j};\mathbf{F})
.

(2)

The restriction of
P
to
(U\cap G^{s})\times\prod_{j=1}^{k}E^{s}_{j}
is continuous as a map into
F^{s-\mu}
for all
\llbracket N\rrbracket\ni s\geqslant r
, and for all
g\in U
there exists a
G^{r}
-open subset
V\subseteq U
such that
g\in V
and whenever
\llbracket N\rrbracket\ni s\geqslant r
,
f\in V\cap G^{s}
, and
h_{i}\in E_{i}^{s}
for
i\in\{{1,\dots,k}\}
we have the estimate

(2.1.3)

\lVert P(f,h_{1},\dots,h_{k})\rVert_{F^{s-\mu}}\lesssim\langle\lVert f\rVert_{G^{s}}\rangle\prod_{i=1}^{k}\lVert h_{i}\rVert_{E_{i}^{r}}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lVert h_{j}\rVert_{E_{j}^{s}}\prod_{i\neq j}\lVert h_{i}\rVert_{E_{i}^{r}}.

A similar equivalence holds for maps
P\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{0}_{\mu,r}(U\times\prod_{j=1}^{k}E^{r}_{j};\mathbf{G}\times\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathbf{E}_{j};\mathbf{F})
if we change the space in the first item to the space of strongly tame maps and we change the second item’s quantification of
V
to ‘for all bounded
G^{r}
-open sets
V\subseteq U
’.

Proof.

The second item implies the first by noting that if
\{{h_{i}}\}_{i=1}^{k}
lies within a bounded subset of
\prod_{i=1}^{k}E^{r}_{i}
, then we immediately obtain the required tame estimates on
P
from inequality (
2.1.3
).

Now we look to the converse, fixing
g\in U
. The first item provides an open set
\widetilde{V}\subseteq U\times\prod_{j=1}^{k}E^{r}_{j}
such that
(g,0,\dots,0)\in\widetilde{V}
and if
(f,h_{1},\dots,h_{k})\in\widetilde{V}\cap(G^{s}\times E^{s}_{1}\times\cdots\times E^{s}_{k})
for
\llbracket N\rrbracket\ni s\geqslant r
, then

(2.1.4)

\lVert P(f,h_{1},\dots,h_{k})\rVert_{F^{s-\mu}}\lesssim\langle\lVert f\rVert_{G^{s}},\lVert h_{1},\dots,h_{k}\rVert_{E^{s}_{1}\times\cdots\times E^{s}_{k}}\rangle.

Now, since
\widetilde{V}
is open, there exists
\delta\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that
V_{\delta}=\overline{B_{G^{r}}(0,\delta)}\times\prod_{j=1}^{k}\overline{B_{E^{r}_{j}}(0,\delta)}\subseteq\widetilde{V}
. Hence, if
f\in\overline{B_{G^{r}}(0,\delta)}
and
h_{1},\dots,h_{k}\in\prod_{j=1}^{k}(E^{r}_{k}\setminus\{{0}\})
are such that
(g,h_{1},\dots,h_{k})\in G^{s}\times E^{s}_{1}\times\cdots\times E^{s}_{k}
, then we have that

(2.1.5)

(f,\delta h_{1}/\lVert h_{1}\rVert_{E^{r}_{1}},\dots,\delta h_{k}/\lVert h_{k}\rVert_{E^{r}_{k}})\in V_{\delta}

and so we can invoke estimate (
2.1.4
) and multiply through by
\delta^{-k}\lVert h_{1}\rVert_{E^{r}_{1}}\cdot\dots\cdot\lVert h_{k}\rVert_{E^{r}_{k}}
to acquire the desired bound (
2.1.3
). A similar argument applies in the case of strongly tame maps.
∎

Remark 2.6
.

If
P\in T^{0}_{\mu,r}(\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathbf{E}_{j};\mathbf{F})
is a
k
-multilinear mapping, then a simple modification of the proof of Lemma
2.5
shows that
P
is actually strongly tame. Moreover, by multilinearity, we have that
P
is automatically a smooth function whose derivatives are also multilinear maps. This combines with the previous fact to show that actually
P\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{\mu,r}(\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathbf{E}_{j};\mathbf{F})
.

As a consequence of the previous lemma, we have structured estimates of the derivatives of tame maps.

Corollary 2.7

(Derivative estimates on tame maps)
.

Let
\mathbf{E}=\{{E^{s}}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
and
\mathbf{F}=\{{F^{s}}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
be Banach scales over a common field. Let
r,\mu\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
with
\mu\leqslant r
,
U\subseteq E^{r}
be an open set, and
P\in T^{k}_{\mu,r}(U,\mathbf{E};\mathbf{F})
. Then for each
f_{0}\in U
, there exists an open set
f_{0}\in V\subseteq U
with the property that for every
\llbracket N\rrbracket\ni s\geqslant r
there exists a constant
C_{s,V}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, depending on
s
and
V
, such that for all
f\in V\cap E^{s}
and all
g_{1},\dots,g_{k}\in E^{s}
we have the estimate

(2.1.6)

\lVert D^{k}P(f)[g_{1},\dots,g_{k}]\rVert_{F^{s-\mu}}\leqslant C_{s,V}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\lVert g_{\ell}\rVert_{E^{s}}\prod_{j\neq\ell}\lVert g_{j}\rVert_{E^{r}}+C_{s,V}\langle\lVert f\rVert_{E^{s}}\rangle\prod_{j=1}^{k}\lVert g_{j}\rVert_{E^{r}}.

Moreover, if
P\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{k}_{\mu,r}(U,\mathbf{E};\mathbf{F})
, then a similar assertion holds for every bounded and open subset
V\subseteq U
.

Proof.

This is a direct application of Lemma
2.5
.
∎

Our next result studies the interaction of tame maps via composition.

Lemma 2.8

(Composition of tame maps)
.

Let
\mathbf{E}=\{{E^{s}}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
,
\mathbf{F}=\{{F^{s}}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
, and
\mathbf{G}=\{{G^{s}}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
be a triple of Banach scales over a common field (
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
), and let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mu,\mu^{\prime},r,r^{\prime}\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
be such that
\mu\leqslant r
,
\mu^{\prime}\leqslant r^{\prime}
,
r^{\prime}+\mu\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
. Suppose that
U\subseteq E^{r}
,
U^{\prime}\subseteq F^{r^{\prime}}
are open sets,
P\in T^{k}_{\mu,r}(U,\mathbf{E};\mathbf{F})
, and
Q\in T^{k}_{\mu^{\prime},r^{\prime}}(U^{\prime},\mathbf{F};\mathbf{G})
. Set
V=U\cap E^{\max\{{r,r^{\prime}+\mu}\}}
. If
P(V)\subseteq U^{\prime}
, then
Q\circ P\in T^{k}_{\mu+\mu^{\prime},\max\{{r,\mu+r^{\prime}}\}}(V,\mathbf{E};\mathbf{G})
. A similar assertion holds for the composition of strongly tame maps.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Consider first the case that
k=0
. Fix
f_{0}\in V
. Since
P(f_{0})\in U^{\prime}
, we can appeal to the tameness of
Q
to obtain an open set
P(f_{0})\in W^{\prime}\subseteq U^{\prime}
such that
Q
satisfies a tame estimate of order
\mu^{\prime}
and base
r^{\prime}
in
W^{\prime}
. The map
P
is continuous and hence
P^{-1}(W^{\prime})\subseteq V
is open and contains
f_{0}
. In light of the tameness of
P
, there exists an open set
f_{0}\in W\subseteq V
in which
P
satisfies a tame estimate of order
\mu
and base
r
. Now, the open set
W\cap P^{-1}(W^{\prime})\subseteq V
contains
f_{0}
and is such that whenever
\llbracket N\rrbracket\ni s\geqslant\max\{{r,r^{\prime}+\mu}\}
and
f\in E^{s}\cap W\cap P^{-1}(W^{\prime})
we may estimate

(2.1.7)

\lVert Q\circ P(f)\rVert_{G^{s-(\mu+\mu^{\prime})}}\lesssim\langle\lVert P(f)\rVert_{F^{s-\mu}}\rangle\lesssim\langle\lVert f\rVert_{E^{s}}\rangle.

Hence,
Q\circ P
is indeed
(\mu+\mu^{\prime})
-tamely
C^{0}
with base
\max\{{r,r^{\prime}+\mu}\}
.

Now suppose that for
1\leqslant k\in\mathbb{N}
the result has been proved at the level
k-1
, and
P
and
Q
satisfy the hypotheses at level
k
. Applying the induction hypothesis handles the tameness of all derivatives up to order
k-1
, so it suffices to show that
D^{k}(Q\circ P)
is
(\mu+\mu^{\prime})
-tame with base
\max\{{r,r^{\prime}+\mu}\}
. Fix
f_{0}\in V
. For
\ell\in\{{0,1,\dots,k}\}
we have that
D^{\ell}Q
is
\mu
-tame with base
r
and hence there exists an open set
P(f_{0})\in W^{\prime}_{\ell}\subseteq U^{\prime}
such that in
W^{\prime}_{\ell}
we have that
D^{\ell}Q
satisfies an
\ell
-multilinear tame estimate of order
\mu^{\prime}
and base
r^{\prime}
(see Lemma
2.7
). The map
P
is continuous and hence
\bigcap_{\ell=1}^{k}P^{-1}(W^{\prime}_{\ell})
is an open subset of
V
containing
f_{0}
. By appealing to the tameness of
P
and Lemma
2.7
again, for
\ell\in\{{0,1,\dots,k}\}
there exists an open set
f_{0}\in W_{\ell}\subseteq V
such that in
W_{\ell}
we have that
D^{\ell}P
satisfies an
\ell
-multilinear tame estimate of order
\mu
and base
r
.

For each
R>0
, the set
O_{f_{0},R}=\bigcap_{\ell=1}^{k}(W_{\ell}\cap P^{-1}(W^{\prime}_{\ell}))\times B_{(E^{\max\{{r,r^{\prime}+\mu}\}})^{k}}(0,R)
is an open
V\times\prod_{p=1}^{k}E^{\max\{{r,r^{\prime}+\mu}\}}
containing
(f_{0},0,\dots,0)
. For
\llbracket N\rrbracket\ni s\geqslant\max\{{r,r^{\prime}+\mu}\}
and
(f,g_{1},\dots,g_{k})\in O_{f_{0},R}\cap E^{s}
, we use the Faà di Bruno theorem (see, for instance, Section 2.4A in Abraham, Marsden, and Ratiu
[
1
]
) to compute

(2.1.8)

D^{k}(Q\circ P)(f)[g_{1},\dots,g_{k}]\\
=\sum_{\sigma\in S_{k}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\sum_{j_{1}+\cdots+j_{\ell}=k}c_{\sigma,\ell,k,j_{1},\dots,j_{\ell}}D^{\ell}Q\circ P(f)\{{D^{j_{1}}P(f),\dots,D^{j_{\ell}}P(f)}\}[g_{\sigma(1)},\dots,g_{\sigma(k)}],

where
S_{k}
denotes the permutation group on
\{{1,\dots,k}\}
, and
c_{\ast}
denotes some combinatorial constant. We will estimate the norm in
G^{s-(\mu+\mu^{\prime})}
of each term in the sum above. Fix
\ell\in\{{1,\dots,k}\}
and
j_{1},\dots,j_{\ell}\in\{{1,\dots,k}\}
such that
j_{1}+\cdots+j_{\ell}=k
. Thanks to Lemma
2.7
and the fact that
\max_{1\leqslant i\leqslant k}\lVert g_{i}\rVert_{E^{r}}\leqslant R
, we are free to estimate

(2.1.9)

\begin{cases}\lVert D^{j_{1}}P(f)[g_{1},\dots,g_{j_{1}}]\rVert_{F^{s-\mu}}\\
\qquad\vdots\\
\lVert D^{j_{\ell}}P(f)[g_{j_{\ell-k+1}},\dots,g_{k}]\rVert_{F^{s-\mu}}\end{cases}\lesssim_{R}\langle\lVert f,g_{1},\dots,g_{k}\rVert_{E^{s}\times\cdots\times E^{s}}\rangle.

By the same argument,

(2.1.10)

\begin{cases}\lVert D^{j_{1}}P(f)[g_{1},\dots,g_{j_{1}}]\rVert_{F^{r^{\prime}}}\\
\qquad\vdots\\
\lVert D^{j_{\ell}}P(f)[g_{j_{\ell-k+1}},\dots,g_{k}]\rVert_{F^{r^{\prime}}}\end{cases}\lesssim_{R}\langle\lVert f,g_{1},\dots,g_{k}\rVert_{E^{\max\{{r^{\prime}+\mu}\}}\times\cdots\times E^{\{{r^{\prime}+\mu}\}}}\rangle\lesssim_{R}1.

Thus, applying the estimate from Lemma
2.7
once more yields

(2.1.11)

\lVert D^{\ell}Q\circ P(f)\{{D^{j_{1}}P(f)[g_{1},\dots,g_{j_{1}}],\dots,D^{j_{\ell}}P(f)[g_{j_{\ell}-k+1},\dots,g_{k}]}\}\rVert_{G^{s-(\mu+\mu^{\prime})}}\\
\lesssim_{R}\langle\lVert f,g_{1},\dots,g_{k}\rVert_{E^{s}\times\cdots\times E^{s}}\rangle.

Then (
2.1.8
) and (
2.1.11
) combine to give us the estimate

(2.1.12)

\lVert D^{k}(Q\circ P)(f)[g_{1},\dots g_{k}]\rVert_{G^{s-(\mu+\mu^{\prime})}}\lesssim_{R}\langle\lVert f,g_{1},\dots,g_{k}\rVert_{E^{s}\times\cdots\times E^{s}}\rangle,

which is the desired tame bound at level
k
. The result thus holds for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
by induction.
∎

A convenient application of the previous result is that tame products of tame maps result in a tame map. More precisely, we have the following.

Corollary 2.9

(Tame products of tame
C^{k}
maps)
.

Let
\ell,k\in\mathbb{N}
with
\ell\geqslant 1
. For
j\in\{{1,\dots,\ell}\}
let
\mathbf{E}_{j}=\{{E^{s}_{j}}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
and
\mathbf{F}_{j}=\{{E^{s}_{j}}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
be Banach scales over a common field, and let
\mathbf{G}=\{{G^{s}}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
be a Banach scale over the same field. Let
r_{1},\dots,r_{\ell}\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
,
\mu_{j}\in\llbracket r_{j}\rrbracket
for
j\in\{{1,\dots,\ell}\}
,
r^{\prime}\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
, and
\mu^{\prime}\in\llbracket r^{\prime}\rrbracket
. Finally, suppose that
U_{j}\subseteq E^{r_{j}}_{j}
is open,
P_{j}\in T^{k}_{\mu_{j},r_{j}}(U_{j},\mathbf{E}_{j};\mathbf{F}_{j})
, and
B\in T^{0}_{\mu^{\prime},r^{\prime}}(\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\mathbf{F}_{j};\mathbf{G})
is
k
-multilinear. If we set

(2.1.13)

\mu=\mu^{\prime}+\max\{{\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{\ell}}\}\text{ and }r=\max\{{r_{1},\dots,r_{\ell},\mu_{1}+r^{\prime},\dots,\mu_{\ell}+r^{\prime}}\}

and assume that
r\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
, then the product map satisfies the inclusion

(2.1.14)

B(P_{1},\dots,P_{\ell})\in T^{k}_{\mu,r}\Big(\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}U_{j}\cap E^{r}_{j},\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\mathbf{E}_{j};\mathbf{G}\Big).

A similar assertion holds for products of strongly tame maps.

Proof.

We first note that by Remark
2.6
,
B
in fact belongs to
{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{\mu^{\prime},r^{\prime}}(\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\mathbf{F}_{j};\mathbf{G})
. The inclusion

(2.1.15)

(P_{1},\dots,P_{\ell})\in T^{k}_{\max\{{\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{\ell}}\},\max\{{r_{1},\dots,r_{\ell}}\}}\Big(\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}U_{j}\cap E^{\max\{{r_{1},\dots,r_{\ell}}\}}_{j},\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\mathbf{E}_{j};\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\mathbf{F}_{j}\Big)

is clear, so the conclusion then follows from Lemma
2.8
.
∎

We can also combine families of tame maps by integrating over parameters. Although more general results hold, we will need only the following simple realization of this fact.

Lemma 2.10

(Integrals of one-parameter families of tame
C^{k}
maps)
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{{\infty}\}
,
\mathbf{E}=\{{E^{s}}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
and
\mathbf{F}=\{{F^{s}}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
be a pair of Banach scales over the same field, and let
\mu,r\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
satisfy
\mu\leqslant r
. Suppose that
U\subseteq E^{r}
is an open set, and for all
t\in[0,1]
let
P_{t}\in T^{k}_{\mu,r}(U,\mathbf{E};\mathbf{F})
. Suppose additionally that the defining inequalities for these tame estimates (as in the first item of Definition
2.4
for the map and its derivatives) are satisfied uniformly in
t\in[0,1]
and that for every
j\in\{{0,\dots,k}\}
,
\llbracket N\rrbracket\ni s\geqslant r
,
f\in U\cap E^{s}
, and
f_{1},\dots,f_{j}\in E^{s}
the map

(2.1.16)

[0,1]\ni t\mapsto D^{j}P_{t}(f)[f_{1},\dots,f_{j}]\in F^{s-\mu}

is continuous. Then the integral map
\int_{0}^{1}P_{t}\;\mathrm{d}t:U\to F^{0}
given by
f\mapsto\int_{0}^{1}P_{t}(f)\;\mathrm{d}t
is well-defined and satisfies
\int_{0}^{1}P_{t}\;\mathrm{d}t\in T^{k}_{\mu,r}(U,\mathbf{E};\mathbf{F})
. A similar assertion holds for integrals of strongly tame maps.

Proof.

The proof essentially amounts to checking definitions, so we will only sketch the argument. Hypothesis (
2.1.16
) ensures that the map
\int_{0}^{1}P_{t}\;\mathrm{d}t
is well-defined, while the assumed uniform tame estimates ensure that the integrands are uniformly bounded with respect to
t\in[0,1]
. The map is
C^{k}
as a map from
U\cap E^{s}
to
F^{s-\mu}
thanks to (
2.1.16
) and standard dominated convergence arguments. The fact that
\int_{0}^{1}P_{t}\;\mathrm{d}t
obeys the defining inequalities to be
\mu
-tamely
C^{k}
with base
r
follows from uniformity in
t
and the fact that the norm of the integral is at most the integral of the norm.
∎

The latter half of this subsection is concerned with various specialized classes of Banach scales. The nicest classes of these are introduced in the subsequent definition, which closely follows Baldi and Haus
[
5
]
.

Definition 2.11

(Smoothable and LP-smoothable Banach scales)
.

Let
\mathbf{E}=\{E^{s}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
be a Banach scale.

(1)

We say that
\mathbf{E}
is smoothable if for each
j\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
there exists a linear map
S_{j}:E^{0}\to E^{N}
satisfying the following smoothing conditions for every
u\in E^{0}
:

(2.1.17)

\left\lVert S_{j}u\right\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{E^{s}}\text{ for all }s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket,

(2.1.18)

\left\lVert S_{j}u\right\rVert_{E^{t}}\lesssim 2^{j(t-s)}\left\lVert S_{j}u\right\rVert_{E^{s}}\text{ for all }s,t\in\llbracket N\rrbracket\text{ with }s<t,

(2.1.19)

\left\lVert(I-S_{j})u\right\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim 2^{-j(t-s)}\left\lVert(I-S_{j})u\right\rVert_{E^{t}}\text{ for all }s,t\in\llbracket N\rrbracket\text{ with }s<t,

(2.1.20)

\left\lVert(S_{j+1}-S_{j})u\right\rVert_{E^{t}}\lesssim 2^{j(t-s)}\left\lVert(S_{j+1}-S_{j})u\right\rVert_{E^{s}}\text{ for all }s,t\in\llbracket N\rrbracket,

where the implicit constants are independent of
j
and are increasing in
s
and
t
.

(2)

We say that
\mathbf{E}
is LP-smoothable if
\mathbf{E}
is smoothable and the smoothing operators satisfy the following Littlewood-Paley condition: for
s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
and
u\in E^{s}
we have that

(2.1.21)

\lim_{j\to\infty}\lVert(I-S_{j})u\rVert_{E^{s}}=0,

and there exists a constant
A>0
, possibly depending on
s
, such that

(2.1.22)

A^{-1}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{E^{s}}\leqslant\Big(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\left\lVert\Updelta_{j}u\right\rVert_{E^{s}}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\leqslant A\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{E^{s}},

where the operators
\{\Updelta_{j}\}_{j=0}^{\infty}
are defined via
\Updelta_{j}=S_{j+1}-S_{j}
with the convention that
S_{0}=0
.

We now give some examples of LP-smoothable Banach scales. The first is trivial, but instructive.

Example 2.12

(A fixed Banach space)
.

Suppose that
X
is a Banach space and
N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. Then the scale
\{{X}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
, generated by
X
alone, is LP-smoothable in the sense of Definition
2.11
, provided we take
S_{j}=I
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
.

We also have the less trivial example of Sobolev spaces on all of Euclidean space.

Example 2.13

(Sobolev spaces on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
)
.

Let
V
be a finite dimensional real vector space and
N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. The real Banach scale of Sobolev spaces
\{{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
is LP-smoothable for the smoothing operators
\{{S_{j}}\}_{j=0}^{\infty}
given by
S_{j}=\mathds{1}_{B(0,2^{j})}(\nabla/2\pi\mathrm{i})
.

Unfortunately, the (LP-)smoothable Banach scales are insufficiently general for our purposes. As such, we introduce a broader class that captures Banach scales which are essentially closed and complemented subspaces of LP-smoothable Banach scales. This is analogous to Hamilton’s notion of a tame Fréchet space, which is given in Definition 1.3.2 of
[
40
]
.

Definition 2.14

(Tame direct summands and tame Banach scales)
.

Let
\mathbf{E}=\{E^{s}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
be a Banach scale.

(1)

Suppose that
\mathbf{F}=\{F^{s}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
is a Banach scale over the same field as
\mathbf{E}
. We say that
\mathbf{E}
is a tame direct summand of
\mathbf{F}
if there exist bounded linear maps
\lambda:E^{0}\to F^{0}
and
\rho:F^{0}\to E^{0}
such that the following hold.

(a)

\rho\lambda u=u
for all
u\in E^{0}
.

(b)

For
s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
we have that
\lambda(E^{s})\subseteq F^{s}
and
\rho(F^{s})\subseteq E^{s}
, and the induced maps
\lambda:E^{s}\to F^{s}
and
\rho:F^{s}\to E^{s}
are bounded and linear.

In this case, we say
\lambda
is the lifting map and
\rho
is the restriction map.

(2)

We say that
\mathbf{E}
is tame if there exists an LP-smoothable Banach scale
\mathbf{F}
such that
\mathbf{E}
is a tame direct summand of
\mathbf{F}
.

Remark 2.15
.

We note that if
\mathbf{E}
is a tame direct summand of
\mathbf{F}
, then basic functional analysis shows that
\lambda(E^{s})\subseteq F^{s}
is a closed and complemented subspace of
F^{s}
, and
\pi=\lambda\circ\rho:F^{s}\to F^{s}
is bounded and linear projection onto
\lambda(E^{s})
. Consequently, for each
s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
there exists a closed subspace
G^{s}\subseteq F^{s}
such that
F^{s}=\lambda(E^{s})\oplus G^{s}
. This is the motivation for calling
\mathbf{E}
a direct summand of
\mathbf{F}
. Moreover, if
\mathbf{E}
is a direct summand of
\mathbf{F}
, then for each
s\in\mathbb{N}
we have the equivalence
\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{E^{s}}\asymp\left\lVert\lambda u\right\rVert_{F^{s}}
for
u\in E^{s}
.

We have the following example of a tame Banach scale.

Example 2.16

(Sobolev spaces on domains)
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be an open set that is a Stein extension domain in the sense of Definition
A.2
, and let
\mathfrak{E}_{U}
denote the associated extension operator. Let
V
be a finite dimensional real vector space and
N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. The real Banach scale of Sobolev spaces
\{{H^{s}(U;V)}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
is tame in the sense of Definition
2.14
. Indeed, we realize this scale as a tame direct summand of
\{{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
, which is LP-smoothable by Example
2.13
, with the lifting operator
\lambda=\mathfrak{E}_{U}
and restriction operator given by standard restriction,
\rho=\mathfrak{R}_{U}
.

We now record a result on products of (LP-)smoothable and tame Banach scales, the proof of which is straightforward and thus omitted.

Lemma 2.17

(Products of Banach scales)
.

The product of a finite family of (smoothable, LP-smoothable, tame) Banach scales over a common field is again a (smoothable, LP-smoothable, tame) Banach scale.

Consider now the following useful inequalities about smoothing in tame Banach scales.

Lemma 2.18

(Smoothing in tame Banach scales)
.

Let
\mathbf{E}=\{{E^{s}}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
be a tame Banach scale in the sense of Definition
2.14
. There exist a sequence of smoothing operators
\{{T_{j}}\}_{j=0}^{\infty}\subset\mathcal{L}(E^{0};E^{N})
and, for
s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
, sequences of seminorms
\{{\mathbf{m}_{j}^{s}}\}_{j=0}^{\infty},\{{\mathbf{n}_{j}^{s}}\}_{j=0}^{\infty}
on
E^{s}
such that the following hold.

(1)

For all
g\in E^{s}
we have the estimates

(2.1.23)

\lVert(T_{j+1}-T_{j})g\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim\mathbf{m}^{s}_{j}(g)\text{ and }\lVert(I-T_{j})g\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim\mathbf{n}^{s}_{j}(g),

where the implicit constants depend only on
s
.

(2)

For
s,t\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
we have that

(2.1.24)

\begin{cases}\mathbf{m}_{j}^{s}\asymp 2^{j(s-t)}\mathbf{m}_{j}^{t}&\text{for all }s,t,\\
\mathbf{n}_{j}^{s}\lesssim 2^{j(s-t)}\mathbf{n}_{j}^{t}&\text{for all }s\leqslant t,\end{cases}

with the implicit constants depending only on
s
and
t
.

(3)

We have the equivalence

(2.1.25)

\lVert\cdot\rVert_{E^{s}}^{2}\asymp\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(\mathbf{m}_{j}^{s})^{2}

with implicit constants depending only on
s
.

(4)

For every
g\in E^{s}
we have that
\mathbf{n}_{j}^{s}(g)\lesssim\lVert g\rVert_{E^{s}}
(with implicit constants depending only on
s
), and
\mathbf{n}_{j}^{s}(g)\to 0
as
j\to\infty
.

(5)

\mathbf{E}
is terminally dense in the sense of the fourth item of Definition
2.2
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{F}
be an LP-smoothable Banach scale witnessing the definition of tameness for
\mathbf{E}
with lifting
\lambda\in\mathcal{L}(E^{0};F^{0})
, restriction
\rho\in\mathcal{L}(F^{0};E^{0})
, and smoothing operators
\{{S_{j}}\}_{j=0}^{\infty}\subseteq\mathcal{L}(F^{0};F^{N})
. We define
T_{j}=\rho\circ S_{j}\circ\lambda
and for
s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
and
g\in E^{s}
set

(2.1.26)

\mathbf{m}_{j}^{s}(g)=\lVert(S_{j+1}-S_{j})\circ\lambda g\rVert_{F^{s}}\text{ and }\mathbf{n}^{s}_{j}(g)=\lVert(I-S_{j})\circ\lambda g\rVert_{F^{s}}.

Then the first item follows from the boundedness of
\rho
. The second, third, and fourth items are immediate consequences of Definition
2.11
and Remark
2.15
. The fifth assertions follows from the first and the fourth.
∎

Next we give an interpolation result.

Lemma 2.19

(Log-convexity in tame Banach scales)
.

Let
\mathbf{E}=\{{E^{s}}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
be a tame Banach scale in the sense of Definition
2.14
over either
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
. For
r,s,t\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
with
r<s<t
we have that

(2.1.27)

\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{E^{r}}^{\frac{t-s}{t-r}}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{E^{t}}^{\frac{s-r}{t-r}}

for all
u\in E^{t}
, where the implicit constant is increasing
r,t
.

Proof.

The bound is trivial if
u=0
, so assume
u\neq 0
. Let
\mathbf{F}
be an LP-smoothable Banach scale witnessing the definition of tameness for
\mathbf{E}
, with lifting, restriction, and smoothing operators
\lambda
,
\rho
, and
\{{S_{j}}\}_{j=0}^{\infty}
, respectively. By Remark
2.15
, we have that
\lVert u\rVert_{E^{p}}\asymp\lVert\lambda u\rVert_{F^{p}}
for
p\in\{{s,r,t}\}
. Now we invoke the properties of the smoothing operators from Definition
2.11
to see that for any
j\in\mathbb{N}

(2.1.28)

\lVert\lambda u\rVert_{F^{s}}\leqslant\lVert(I-S_{j})\lambda u\rVert_{F^{s}}+\lVert S_{j}\lambda u\rVert_{F^{s}}\lesssim 2^{j(s-t)}\lVert\lambda u\rVert_{F^{t}}+2^{j(s-r)}\lVert\lambda u\rVert_{F^{r}}

and hence
\lVert u\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim 2^{j(s-t)}\lVert u\rVert_{E^{t}}+2^{j(s-r)}\lVert u\rVert_{E^{r}}
. Now, since
1\leqslant\lVert u\rVert_{E^{t}}/\lVert u\rVert_{E^{r}}
, we can choose
j=\lfloor\log(\lVert u\rVert_{E^{t}}/\lVert u\rVert_{E^{r}})/((t-r)\log 2)\rfloor\in\mathbb{N}
to obtain the desired inequality.
∎

2.2.
Mapping hypotheses and statement of the inverse function theorem

We now introduce a lengthy definition that records a number of conditions that must be placed on the nonlinear map in our version of the Nash-Moser inverse function theorem.

Definition 2.20

(Mapping hypotheses)
.

We say that a triple
(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{F},\Psi)
satisfies the RI mapping hypotheses with parameters
(\mu,r,R)\in\llbracket N\rrbracket^{3}
if
\mathbf{E}=\{E^{s}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
and
\mathbf{F}=\{F^{s}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
are Banach scales over a common field,
1\leqslant\mu\leqslant r<R<\infty
,
R+\mu\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
, and there exists
0<\delta_{r}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\Psi:B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{r})\to F^{r-\mu}
is a map satisfying the following.

(1)

\Psi(0)=0
.

(2)

\mu
-tamely
C^{2}
: For every
r-\mu\leqslant s\in\llbracket N-\mu\rrbracket
we have that
\Psi:B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{r})\cap E^{s+\mu}\to F^{s}
is
C^{2}
, and for every
u_{0}\in B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{r})\cap E^{s+\mu}
we have the tame estimate

(2.2.1)

\left\lVert D^{2}\Psi(u_{0})[v,w]\right\rVert_{F^{s}}\leqslant C_{1}(s)(\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{E^{r}}+\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{E^{r}}\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}+\left\langle\left\lVert u_{0}\right\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}\right\rangle\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{E^{r}}\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{E^{r}}).

Here the constant
C_{1}(s)
is increasing in
s
. In other words, we have the inclusion
\Psi\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{2}_{\mu,r}(B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{r}),\mathbf{E};\mathbf{F})
according to the notation from Definition
2.4
.

(3)

Derivative inversion: There exists
\delta_{R}\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying
0<\delta_{R}\leqslant\delta_{r}
such that for every
u_{0}\in B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{R})\cap E^{N}
there exists a bounded linear operator
L(u_{0}):F^{r}\to E^{r}
satisfying the following three conditions.

(a)

For every
s\in\mathbb{N}\cap[r,R]
we have that the restriction of
L(u_{0})
to
F^{s}
defines a bounded linear operator with values in
E^{s}
, i.e.
L(u_{0})\in\mathcal{L}(F^{s};E^{s})
.

(b)

D\Psi(u_{0})L(u_{0})f=f
for every
f\in F^{r}
.

(c)

We have the tame estimate

(2.2.2)

\left\lVert L(u_{0})f\right\rVert_{E^{s}}\leqslant C_{2}(s)(\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{F^{s}}+\left\langle\left\lVert u_{0}\right\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}\right\rangle\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{F^{r}})

for every
f\in F^{s}
and
r\leqslant s\leqslant R
, where again the constant
C_{2}(s)
is increasing in
s
.

Here the use of the prefix RI- is meant to indicate that the maps
L(u_{0})
are only required to be right inverses. We say that the triple
(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{F},\Psi)
satisfies the LRI mapping hypotheses if condition
(b)
in the third item is augmented by the left-inverse condition
(b^{\prime}):

(2.2.3)

L(u_{0})D\Psi(u_{0})v=v\text{ for every }v\in E^{r+\mu}.

See Figure
4
for a diagrammatic depiction of how
L(u_{0})
and
D\Psi(u_{0})
interact with
\mathbf{E}
and
\mathbf{F}
.

F^{s+\mu}
E^{s+\mu}
L(u_{0})
F^{s}
E^{s}
L(u_{0})
D\Psi(u_{0})
F^{s-\mu}
E^{s-\mu}
L(u_{0})
D\Psi(u_{0})
F^{0}
E^{0}
F^{N}
E^{N}

Figure 4.
Commutative diagram arising from the LRI mapping hypotheses for
u_{0}\in E^{N}\cap B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{R})
and
r+\mu\leqslant s\leqslant R-\mu
. The ‘
\hookrightarrow
’ are the inclusion maps.

To conclude this subsection, we state our version of the Nash-Moser inverse function theorem, which is divided into two parts.

Theorem 2.21

(Inverse function theorem)
.

Let
\mathbf{E}=\{E^{s}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
and
\mathbf{F}=\{F^{s}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
be Banach scales over the same field, and assume that
\mathbf{E}
and
\mathbf{F}
satisfy one of the following two conditions:

I:

\mathbf{E}
and
\mathbf{F}
are LP-smoothable (see Definition
2.11
), or

II:

\mathbf{E}
is tame and
\mathbf{F}
is a tame direct summand of
\mathbf{E}
(see Definition
2.14
).

Assume the triple
(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{F},\Psi)
satisfies the LRI mapping hypotheses of Definition
2.20
with parameters
(\mu,r,R)
satisfying
2(r+\mu)+1<(r+R)/2
, and set
\beta=2(r+\mu)+1\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
. Then there exist
\varepsilon,\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}>0
such that the following hold.

(1)

Existence of local inverse: For every
g\in B_{F^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon)
there exists a unique
u\in B_{E^{\beta}}(0,\kappa_{1}\varepsilon)
such that
\Psi(u)=g
.

(2)

Estimates of local inverse: The induced bijection
\Psi^{-1}:B_{F^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon)\to\Psi^{-1}(B_{F^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon))\cap B_{E^{\beta}}(0,\kappa_{1}\varepsilon)
obeys the estimate

(2.2.4)

\lVert\Psi^{-1}(g)\rVert_{E^{\beta}}\leqslant\kappa_{1}\lVert g\rVert_{F^{\beta}}\text{ for all }g\in B_{F^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon).

Moreover, if
\mathbb{N}\ni\nu\leqslant R+r-2\beta
, then we have that

(2.2.5)

\Psi^{-1}:B_{F^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon)\cap F^{\beta+\nu}\to E^{\beta+\nu}

with the estimate

(2.2.6)

\lVert\Psi^{-1}(g)\rVert_{E^{\beta+\nu}}\lesssim\lVert g\rVert_{F^{\beta+\nu}},

where the implied constant is independent of
g
.

(3)

Basic continuous dependence: The map
\Psi^{-1}
obeys the Lipschitz bound

(2.2.7)

\lVert\Psi^{-1}(g_{0})-\Psi^{-1}(g_{1})\rVert_{E^{\beta-\mu}}\leqslant\kappa_{2}\lVert g_{0}-g_{1}\rVert_{F^{\beta-\mu}}

for all
g_{0},g_{1}\in B_{F^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon)
.

Remark 2.22
.

The inequality
3r+4\mu+2<R
is equivalent to
\beta=2(r+\mu)+1<(r+R)/2
. Furthermore, when
\llbracket N\rrbracket
is finite, the mapping hypotheses require that
R+\mu\leqslant N
, and so we obtain the necessary relation
3r+5\mu+2<N
.

We present the proof of Theorem
2.21
in Section
2.4
after first establishing two other theorems that prove separate components of the theorem under different hypotheses. We now turn to the statement of the second part of our inverse function theorem, beginning with some notation. Due to the derivative loss in the nonlinear operators under consideration, the higher regularity of the local inverse map is most conveniently phrased in terms of some variation on Gateaux derivatives rather than the usual Fréchet notion of differentiability. This is analogous to what is done in Section I.3 and elsewhere in Hamilton
[
40
]
.

Definition 2.23

(Continuous Gateaux differentiability)
.

Let
X
and
Y
be Banach spaces over a common field,
U\subseteq X
an open set, and
f:U\to Y
.

(1)

We say that
f
is continuously Gateaux differentiable on
U
if there exists a continuous map
Df:U\times X\to Y
such that for all
x\in U
we have that
Df(x)\in\mathcal{L}(X;Y)
and for all
z\in X

(2.2.8)

\lim_{t\to 0}\;t^{-1}(f(x+tz)-f(x))=Df(x)z.

(2)

For
\mathbb{N}\ni\ell\geqslant 2
, we say that
f
is
\ell
-times continuously Gateaux differentiable if
f
is continuously Gateaux differentiable and
Df:U\times X\to Y
is
(\ell-1)
-times continuously Gateaux differentiable.

We can now state the second part of our Nash-Moser inverse function theorem, the proof of which is in Section
2.5
.

Theorem 2.24

(Further conclusions of the inverse function theorem)
.

Assume the hypotheses of Theorem
2.21
and additionally that the Banach scale
\mathbf{E}
consists of reflexive spaces. The following additional conclusions hold for the local inverse map
\Psi^{-1}:B_{F^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon)\to E^{\beta}
.

(1)

Continuity: For
s\in[\beta,R+r-\beta-\mu)\cap\mathbb{N}
the map

(2.2.9)

\Psi^{-1}:B_{F^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon)\cap F^{s}\to E^{s}

is continuous.

(2)

Continuous differentiability: For
s\in[\beta,R+r-\beta-\mu)\cap\mathbb{N}
, the map

(2.2.10)

\Psi^{-1}:B_{F^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon)\cap F^{s}\to E^{s-\mu}

is differentiable in the Fréchet sense with
D\Psi^{-1}=L\circ\Psi^{-1}
. Moreover, when viewing
D\Psi^{-1}
as map

(2.2.11)

D\Psi^{-1}:(B_{F^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon)\cap F^{s})\times F^{s-\mu}\to E^{s-\mu}

we have that it is continuous.

(3)

Higher regularity: Let
\mathbb{N}\ni\ell\geqslant 2
and assume that the map
\Psi
is
\ell
-times continuously Gateaux differentiable. For
s\in[\beta+(\ell(\ell+1)/2-1)\mu,R+r-\beta-\mu)\cap\mathbb{N}
we have that the map

(2.2.12)

\Psi^{-1}:B_{F^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon)\cap F^{s}\to E^{s-\frac{\ell(\ell+1)}{2}\mu}

is also
\ell
-times continuously Gateaux differentiable.

2.3.
Local surjectivity and injectivity

We begin this section by proving an existence result modeled on the main theorem of Baldi and Haus
[
5
]
, but adapted to our specific setting. We emphasize that the following theorem only requires the existence of right inverses in the mapping hypotheses.

Theorem 2.25

(Local surjectivity)
.

Let
\mathbf{E}=\{E^{s}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
and
\mathbf{F}=\{F^{s}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
be Banach scales over the same field, and suppose that
(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{F},\Psi)
satisfies the RI mapping hypotheses of Definition
2.20
with parameters
(\mu,r,R)
such that
2(r+\mu)+1<(r+R)/2
. Set
\beta=2(r+\mu)+1\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
and let
A_{\beta}>0
be such that
\left\lVert\Updelta_{j}f\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}\leqslant A_{\beta}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
f\in F^{\beta}
. Let
C_{i}=C_{i}(R)
denote the constants from the mapping hypotheses for
i\in\{1,2\}
.

Then there exist
K_{1},K_{2},K_{3},K_{4}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that for every
g\in F^{\beta}
satisfying

(2.3.1)

\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}<\frac{\delta_{R}}{2[(1+A_{\beta})K_{1}+K_{2}+K_{3}+K_{4}+(1+A_{\beta})^{2}K_{1}(K_{1}+K_{3})]}

there exists a sequence
\{(u_{j},v_{j},h_{j},y_{j},f_{j},e_{j})\}_{j=0}^{\infty}\subseteq(B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{R})\cap E^{R})\times E^{N}\times E^{R}\times F^{N}\times F^{N}\times F^{R-\mu}
satisfying the following.

(1)

When
j=0
we have the identities

\displaystyle u_{0}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle v_{0}

\displaystyle=S_{0}u_{0}=0,

(2.3.2)

\displaystyle y_{0}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle f_{0}

\displaystyle=\Updelta_{0}g=S_{1}g,

\displaystyle h_{0}

\displaystyle=L(u_{0})f_{0}=L(0)S_{1}g,

\displaystyle e_{0}

\displaystyle=\Psi(u_{0}+h_{0})-\Psi(u_{0})-f_{0},

while for
j\geqslant 1
we have the recursive relations

\displaystyle u_{j}

\displaystyle=u_{j-1}+h_{j-1},

\displaystyle v_{j}

\displaystyle=S_{j}u_{j},

(2.3.3)

\displaystyle y_{j}

\displaystyle=-S_{j}\sum_{n=0}^{j-1}e_{n}-\sum_{n=0}^{j-1}y_{n},

\displaystyle f_{j}

\displaystyle=\Updelta_{j}g+y_{j},

\displaystyle h_{j}

\displaystyle=L(v_{j})f_{j},

\displaystyle e_{j}

\displaystyle=\Psi(u_{j}+h_{j})-\Psi(u_{j})-f_{j}.

(2)

For
j\in\mathbb{N}
we have the estimates

(2.3.4)

\lVert h_{j}\rVert_{E^{s}}\leqslant K_{1}(\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{-j}+\left\lVert\Updelta_{j}g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}})2^{j(s-\beta)}\text{ for all }s\in[r,R]\cap\mathbb{N},

(2.3.5)

\lVert v_{j}\rVert_{E^{s}}\leqslant K_{2}\lVert g\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{j(s-\beta)}\text{ for all }\beta\leqslant s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket,

(2.3.6)

\lVert u_{j}-v_{j}\rVert_{E^{s}}\leqslant K_{3}\lVert g\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{j(s-\beta)}\text{ for all }s\in[0,R]\cap\mathbb{N},

(2.3.7)

\lVert u_{j}\rVert_{E^{\beta}}\leqslant K_{4}\lVert g\rVert_{F^{\beta}}.

and

(2.3.8)

\lVert e_{j}\rVert_{F^{s}}\lesssim C_{1}\lVert g\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{j(s+\mu+r-2\beta)}\text{ for all }s\in[r-\mu,R-\mu]\cap\mathbb{N}.

(3)

There exists
u\in E^{\beta}
such that
u_{j}\to u
in
E^{\beta}
as
j\to\infty
,
\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{E^{\beta}}\leqslant K_{4}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}
, and
\Psi(u)=g
.

(4)

If we know additionally that
\nu\in\mathbb{N}
is such that
\nu\leqslant R+r-2\beta
and
g\in F^{\beta+\nu}
, then actually
u_{j}\to u
in
E^{\beta+\nu}
as
j\to\infty
and we have the estimate
\lVert u\rVert_{E^{\beta+\nu}}\lesssim\lVert g\rVert_{F^{\beta+\nu}}
. Here the implied constant depends on
K_{1},\dots,K_{4}
,
A_{\beta},A_{\beta+\nu}
,
\mu
,
r
,
\delta_{R}
, and
R
.

Proof.

We divide the proof into several steps. The first three steps establish a trio of crucial claims that will be used in the fourth step to inductively construct the sequence. The convergence result is then proved in the fifth step. The higher regularity assertions of the fourth item are then proved in the sixth step. Throughout the proof we will utilize the sequence
\{\xi_{n}\}_{n=0}^{\infty}\subseteq[0,\infty)
defined by

(2.3.9)

\xi_{n}=\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{-n}+\left\lVert\Updelta_{n}g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}.

Step 1
: An estimate in the style of Littlewood-Paley theory. Let
\ell,k\in\mathbb{N}
satisfy
0\leqslant\ell\leqslant k
. We claim that if
\{h_{j}\}_{j=0}^{k}\subseteq E^{R}
are given and satisfy (
2.3.4
) for
0\leqslant j\leqslant k
, then

(2.3.10)

\Big\lVert\sum_{n=\ell}^{k}h_{n}\Big\rVert_{E^{\beta}}\lesssim K_{1}\Big(\sum_{n=\ell}^{k}\xi_{n}^{2}\Big)^{1/2},

where
\xi_{n}
is defined by (
2.3.9
)

To prove the claim, we begin by noting that for any
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
0\leqslant n\leqslant k
, and
s=\beta+\mathrm{sgn}(j-n)\in[r,R]\cap\mathbb{N}
we may bound, via (
2.1.20
) and (
2.3.4
),

(2.3.11)

\lVert\Updelta_{j}h_{n}\rVert_{E^{\beta}}\lesssim 2^{j(\beta-s)}\lVert h_{n}\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim K_{1}\xi_{n}2^{j(\beta-s)}2^{n(s-\beta)}=K_{1}\xi_{n}2^{(j-n)(\beta-s)}=K_{1}\xi_{n}2^{-|j-n|}.

We estimate the sum on the left hand side of (
2.3.10
) via the ‘Littlewood-Paley’ characterization (
2.1.22
), the bound (
2.3.11
), and Young’s convolution inequality (setting
\xi_{n}=0
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
0<n
):

(2.3.12)

\Big\lVert\sum_{n=\ell}^{k}h_{n}\Big\rVert_{E^{\beta}}^{2}\lesssim\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\Big\lVert\Updelta_{j}\sum_{n=\ell}^{k}h_{n}\Big\rVert_{E^{\beta}}^{2}\leqslant\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\Big(\sum_{n=\ell}^{k}\lVert\Updelta_{j}h_{n}\rVert_{E^{\beta}}\Big)^{2}\lesssim K_{1}^{2}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\Big(\sum_{n=\ell}^{k}\xi_{n}2^{-|j-n|}\Big)^{2}\\
\leqslant K_{1}^{2}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big(\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\xi_{n}\mathds{1}_{[\ell,k]}(n)2^{-|j-n|}\Big)^{2}\leqslant K_{1}^{2}\Big(\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\xi_{n}^{2}\mathds{1}_{[\ell,k]}(n)\Big)\Big(\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{-\left|n\right|}\Big)^{2}\lesssim K_{1}^{2}\sum_{n=\ell}^{k}\xi_{n}^{2}.

This is (
2.3.10
).

Step 2
: An estimate from Taylor’s theorem. Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
. We claim that if
g\in F^{\beta}
satisfies (
2.3.1
) and
\{(u_{j},v_{j},h_{j})\}_{j=0}^{k}\subseteq(B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{R})\cap E^{R})\times E^{N}\times E^{R}
are given and satisfy (
2.3.4
)–(
2.3.7
) for
0\leqslant j\leqslant k
, as well as the conditions
u_{0}=v_{0}=0
and
u_{j}=u_{j-1}+h_{j-1}
if
1\leqslant j\leqslant k
, then

(2.3.13)

\{u_{j}\}_{j=0}^{k},\{u_{j}+h_{j}\}_{j=0}^{k},\{v_{j}\}_{j=0}^{k}\subseteq B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{R})\subseteq B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{r})

and

(2.3.14)

\lVert\Psi(u_{j}+h_{j})-\Psi(u_{j})-D\Psi(v_{j})h_{j}\rVert_{F^{s}}\lesssim C_{1}\lVert g\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{j(s+\mu+r-2\beta)}

for all
s\in[r-\mu,R-\mu]\cap\mathbb{N}
.

To prove this claim we begin by employing (
2.3.1
). Indeed, (
2.3.1
) and (
2.3.7
) imply that
\left\lVert u_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{r}}\leqslant\left\lVert u_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{\beta}}\leqslant K_{4}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}<\frac{\delta_{R}}{2}\leqslant\frac{\delta_{r}}{2}
, (
2.3.5
) and (
2.3.1
) imply that
\left\lVert v_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{r}}\leqslant\left\lVert v_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{\beta}}\leqslant K_{2}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}<\frac{\delta_{R}}{2}\leqslant\frac{\delta_{r}}{2}
for
0\leqslant j\leqslant k
, and (
2.3.4
) and (
2.3.1
) imply that
\left\lVert h_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{r}}\leqslant\left\lVert h_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{\beta}}\leqslant K_{1}\left(\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}+A_{\beta}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}\right)=K_{1}(1+A_{\beta})\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}<\frac{\delta_{R}}{2}\leqslant\frac{\delta_{r}}{2}
for the same range of
j
. Together, these three bounds imply the inclusions (
2.3.13
).

Now that (
2.3.13
) is established, we know that
\Psi(u_{j}+h_{j})
,
\Psi(u_{j})
, and
D\Psi(v_{j})
are well-defined. We may then employ the fundamental theorem of calculus, Taylor’s theorem, and the convexity of
B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{r})
to write

(2.3.15)

\Psi(u_{j}+h_{j})-\Psi(u_{j})-D\Psi(v_{j})h_{j}=(D\Psi(u_{j})-D\Psi(v_{j}))h_{j}+\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)D^{2}\Psi(u_{j}+th_{j})[h_{j},h_{j}]dt\\
=\int_{0}^{1}D^{2}\Psi((1-t)v_{j}+tu_{j})[h_{j},u_{j}-v_{j}]dt+\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)D^{2}\Psi(u_{j}+th_{j})[h_{j},h_{j}]dt=\mathbf{I}_{j}+\mathbf{II}_{j}.

By using the tame
C^{2}
estimate from the mapping hypotheses of Definition
2.20
, we readily deduce that for
r-\mu\leqslant s\in\llbracket N-\mu\rrbracket
,

(2.3.16)

\lVert\mathbf{I}_{j}\rVert_{F^{s}}\lesssim C_{1}\langle\left\lVert u_{j}-v_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{r}}\rangle\left\lVert u_{j}-v_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}\left\lVert h_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{r}}\\
+C_{1}\langle\left\lVert v_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}\rangle\left\lVert u_{j}-v_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{r}}\left\lVert h_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{r}}+C_{1}\left\lVert u_{j}-v_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{r}}\left\lVert h_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}

and

(2.3.17)

\left\lVert\mathbf{II}_{j}\right\rVert_{F^{s}}\lesssim C_{1}\langle\left\lVert h_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{r}}\rangle\left\lVert h_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}\left\lVert h_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{r}}+C_{1}\langle\left\lVert u_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}\rangle\left\lVert h_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{r}}^{2}.

Synthesizing these bounds, we find that

(2.3.18)

\left\lVert\Psi(u_{j}+h_{j})-\Psi(u_{j})-D\Psi(v_{j})h_{j}\right\rVert_{F^{s}}\lesssim C_{1}\langle\left\lVert u_{j}-v_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{r}}\rangle\left\lVert u_{j}-v_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}\left\lVert h_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{r}}\\
+C_{1}\left\lVert u_{j}-v_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{r}}\left\lVert h_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}+C_{1}\langle\left\lVert h_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{r}}\rangle\left\lVert h_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}\left\lVert h_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{r}}\\
+C_{1}\langle\left\lVert u_{j},v_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}\rangle\left\lVert h_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{r}}(\left\lVert h_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{r}}+\left\lVert u_{j}-v_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{r}})

for
r-\mu\leqslant s\in\llbracket N-\mu\rrbracket
.

Next we turn our attention to estimates for
u_{j}
and
v_{j}
. Note that if
1\leqslant j\leqslant k
, then the identity
u=\sum_{n=0}^{j-1}h_{n}
and (
2.3.4
) imply that

(2.3.19)

\left\lVert u_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{R}}\leqslant\sum_{n=0}^{j-1}\left\lVert h_{n}\right\rVert_{E^{R}}\leqslant K_{1}(1+A_{\beta})\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}\sum_{n=0}^{j-1}2^{n(R-\beta)}\lesssim K_{1}(1+A_{\beta})\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{j(R-\beta)}.

In turn, when we couple this with the smoothing estimates from Definition
2.11
, the interpolation result from Lemma
2.19
, and (
2.3.7
), this implies that

(2.3.20)

\left\lVert v_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim\left\lVert u_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim\left\lVert u_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{\beta}}^{\frac{R-s}{R-\beta}}\left\lVert u_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{R}}^{\frac{s-\beta}{R-\beta}}\lesssim K_{4}^{\frac{R-s}{R-\beta}}(K_{1}(1+A_{\beta}))^{\frac{s-\beta}{R-\beta}}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{j(s-\beta)}

for all
s\in\mathbb{N}\cap[\beta,R]
.
However, (
2.3.1
) implies that
K_{4}^{\frac{R-s}{R-\beta}}(K_{1}(1+A_{\beta}))^{\frac{s-\beta}{R-\beta}}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}\leqslant 1
, so we may further bound
\left\lVert v_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim\left\lVert u_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim 2^{j(s-\beta)}
for all
s\in\mathbb{N}\cap[\beta,R]
.

On the other hand, (
2.3.7
) and (
2.3.1
) give us that
\left\lVert v_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim\left\lVert u_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim\left\lVert u_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{\beta}}\lesssim K_{4}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}\lesssim 1
for all
s\in\mathbb{N}\cap[r,\beta]
. Upon combining these, we find that

(2.3.21)

\left\lVert u_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{s}}+\left\lVert v_{j}\right\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim 2^{j\max\{0,s-\beta\}}\text{ for all }s\in\mathbb{N}\cap[r,R]\text{ and }0\leqslant j\leqslant k,

where we note that these bounds are trivial for
j=0
since
u_{0}=v_{0}=0
.

Now we combine (
2.3.18
) and (
2.3.21
) with (
2.3.4
)–(
2.3.7
) to bound, for
0\leqslant j\leqslant k
and
s\in\mathbb{N}\cap[r-\mu,R-\mu]
,

(2.3.22)

\left\lVert\Psi(u_{j}+h_{j})-\Psi(u_{j})-D\Psi(v_{j})h_{j}\right\rVert_{F^{s}}\\
\lesssim C_{1}\langle K_{3}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{j(r-\beta)}\rangle K_{3}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{j(s+\mu-\beta)}\cdot K_{1}(1+A_{\beta})\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{j(r-\beta)}\\
+C_{1}K_{3}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{j(r-\beta)}\cdot K_{1}(1+A_{\beta})\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{j(s+\mu-\beta)}\\
+C_{1}\langle K_{1}(1+A_{\beta})\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{j(r-\beta)}\rangle K_{1}(1+A_{\beta})2^{j(s+\mu-\beta)}\cdot K_{1}(1+A_{\beta})2^{j(r-\beta)}\\
+C_{1}\langle 2^{j\max\{0,s+\mu-\beta\}}\rangle K_{1}(1+A_{\beta})\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{j(r-\beta)}\cdot(K_{1}(1+A_{\beta})\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{j(r-\beta)}+K_{3}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{j(r-\beta)}).

For the last term we note that

(2.3.23)

\begin{cases}r-\mu\leqslant s\leqslant\beta-\mu\Rightarrow 2r-2\beta\leqslant s+\mu+r-2\beta\\
\beta-\mu\leqslant s\leqslant R-\mu\Rightarrow s+\mu-\beta+2r-2\beta\leqslant s+\mu+r-2\beta.\end{cases}

Thus, upon regrouping and using (
2.3.1
), we then see that

(2.3.24)

\left\lVert\Psi(u_{j}+h_{j})-\Psi(u_{j})-D\Psi(v_{j})h_{j}\right\rVert_{F^{s}}\lesssim C_{1}(1+A_{\beta})^{2}K_{1}(K_{1}+K_{3})\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}^{2}2^{j(s+\mu+r-2\beta)}\\
\lesssim C_{1}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{j(s+\mu+r-2\beta)}

for all
s\in\mathbb{N}\cap[r-\mu,R-\mu]
and
0\leqslant j\leqslant k
. This is (
2.3.14
), and so the proof of the claim is complete.

Step 3
: A recursive identity. Suppose that
\{(y_{j},e_{j})\}_{j=0}^{k}\subseteq F^{N}\times F^{R-\mu}
is given for
2\leqslant k\in\mathbb{N}
and satisfies
y_{0}=0
and the recursive condition

(2.3.25)

y_{j}=-S_{j}\sum_{n=0}^{j-1}e_{n}-\sum_{n=0}^{j-1}y_{n}\text{ for }1\leqslant j\leqslant k.

We claim that

(2.3.26)

y_{j}=-S_{j}e_{j-1}-\Updelta_{j-1}\sum_{n=0}^{j-2}e_{n}\text{ for }2\leqslant j\leqslant k.

To see this, note that from (
2.3.25
) we deduce that for any
1\leqslant j\leqslant k
the identity
\sum_{n=0}^{j}y_{n}=-S_{j}\sum_{n=0}^{j-1}e_{n}
. Hence, for
2\leqslant j\leqslant k
we have
y_{j}=-S_{j}\sum_{n=0}^{j-1}e_{n}-S_{j-1}\sum_{n=0}^{j-2}e_{n}
, but by the definitions of the
\Updelta_{n}
and
e_{n}
, this is the same as
y_{j}=-S_{j}e_{j-1}-\Updelta_{j-1}\sum_{n=0}^{j-2}e_{n}
,
and therefore (
2.3.26
) holds.

Step 4
: Inductive construction of the sequence. We now aim to inductively construct the desired sequence under some assumptions on the constants
K_{1}
,
K_{2}
,
K_{3}
,
K_{4}
, which we will work out as we proceed.

We begin by seeding the sequence, i.e. constructing its elements with
j=0
. Define
u_{0}=0\in B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{R})\cap E^{R}
,
v_{0}=S_{0}u_{0}=0\in E^{N}
,
y_{0}=0\in F^{N}
,
f_{0}=\Updelta_{0}g=S_{1}g\in F^{N}
,
h_{0}=L(u_{0})f_{0}=L(0)S_{1}g\in E^{R}
, and
e_{0}=\Psi(h_{0})-\Psi(0)-f_{0}\in F^{R-\mu}
. By construction, the bounds (
2.3.5
), (
2.3.6
), and (
2.3.7
) hold trivially when
j=0
for all possible choices of
K_{2},K_{3},K_{4}>0
. On the other hand, we can apply the tame estimate for
L(0)
from the mapping hypotheses to see that for
s\in\mathbb{N}\cap[r,R]
,

(2.3.27)

\left\lVert h_{0}\right\rVert_{E^{s}}\leqslant C_{2}\left\lVert S_{1}g\right\rVert_{F^{s}}+C_{2}(1+\left\lVert 0\right\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}})\left\lVert S_{1}g\right\rVert_{F^{r}}\lesssim C_{2}\left\lVert\Updelta_{0}g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}+C_{2}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}\\
\leqslant K_{1}\left(\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{-0}+\left\lVert\Updelta_{j}g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}\right)2^{0(s-\beta)},

provided the constant
K_{1}
satisfies the bound

(2.3.28)

C_{2}\lesssim K_{1},

which we henceforth assume holds. We then have that (
2.3.4
) is satisfied for
j=0
.

We now proceed to the inductive step. Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and suppose that

(2.3.29)

\{(u_{j},v_{j},h_{j},y_{j},f_{j},e_{j})\}_{j=0}^{k}\subseteq(B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{R})\cap E^{R})\times E^{N}\times E^{R}\times F^{N}\times F^{N}\times F^{R-\mu}

are given and satisfy (
1
), (
2.3.4
)–(
2.3.7
) for
0\leqslant j\leqslant k
, and (
1
) if
1\leqslant j\leqslant k
. We will construct
(u_{k+1},v_{k+1},h_{k+1},y_{k+1},f_{k+1},e_{k+1})\in(B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{R})\cap E^{R})\times E^{N}\times E^{R}\times F^{N}\times F^{N}\times F^{R-\mu}
and show that (
2.3.4
)–(
2.3.7
) continue to hold for
j=k+1
.

First, we define
u_{k+1}=u_{k}+h_{k}\in E^{R}
. The claim established in Step 2 guarantees that
u_{k+1}\in B_{r}(0,\delta_{R})\subseteq B_{r}(0,\delta_{r})
. Moreover, we may use a telescoping argument to see that
u_{k+1}=u_{k+1}-u_{0}=\sum_{n=0}^{k}h_{n}
. Thus, the claim established in Step 1 shows that

(2.3.30)

\left\lVert u_{k+1}\right\rVert_{E^{\beta}}\lesssim K_{1}\Big(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\xi_{n}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\lesssim K_{1}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}},

where in the last inequality we have used the Littlewood-Paley bound (
2.1.22
). Thus,
u_{k+1}\in B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{R})\cap E^{R}
satisfies (
2.3.7
) with
j=k+1
, provided that
K_{1}
and
K_{4}
satisfy

(2.3.31)

K_{1}\lesssim K_{4},

which we henceforth assume.

Second, we define
v_{k+1}=S_{k+1}u_{k+1}\in E^{N}
. We then use (
2.3.4
) and the smoothing bounds (
2.1.17
)–(
2.1.20
) to estimate

(2.3.32)

\left\lVert v_{k+1}-u_{k+1}\right\rVert_{E^{R}}=\left\lVert(I-S_{k+1})u_{k+1}\right\rVert_{E^{R}}\lesssim\left\lVert u_{k+1}\right\rVert_{E^{R}}\leqslant\sum_{n=0}^{k}\left\lVert h_{n}\right\rVert_{E^{R}}\leqslant K_{1}\sum_{n=0}^{k}\xi_{n}2^{n(R-\beta)}\\
\leqslant K_{1}\Big(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\xi_{n}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\sum_{n=0}^{k}2^{2n(R-\beta)}\Big)^{1/2}\lesssim K_{1}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{(k+1)(R-\beta)}.

On the other hand, (
2.3.30
) and the smoothing bounds (
2.1.17
)–(
2.1.20
) show that

(2.3.33)

\left\lVert v_{k+1}-u_{k+1}\right\rVert_{E^{0}}=\left\lVert(I-S_{k+1})u_{k+1}\right\rVert_{E^{0}}\lesssim 2^{-(k+1)\beta}\left\lVert(I-S_{k+1})u_{k+1}\right\rVert_{E^{\beta}}\\
\lesssim 2^{-(k+1)\beta}\left\lVert u_{k+1}\right\rVert_{E^{\beta}}\lesssim K_{1}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{-(k+1)\beta}.

Upon combining these two estimates and interpolating with the help of Lemma
2.19
, we find that for
s\in\mathbb{N}\cap[0,R]
,

(2.3.34)

\left\lVert v_{k+1}-u_{k+1}\right\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim\left\lVert v_{k+1}-u_{k+1}\right\rVert_{E^{0}}^{1-s/R}\left\lVert v_{k+1}-u_{k+1}\right\rVert_{E^{R}}^{s/R}\lesssim K_{1}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{(k+1)(s-\beta)},

and so
v_{k+1}\in E^{N}
satisfies (
2.3.6
) with
j=k+1
provided that
K_{1}
and
K_{3}
satisfy

(2.3.35)

K_{1}\lesssim K_{3},

which we henceforth assume.

Continuing with
v_{k+1}
, we observe that for
\beta\leqslant s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
we can use the smoothing bounds of Definition
2.11
to estimate

(2.3.36)

\left\lVert v_{k+1}\right\rVert_{E^{s}}=\left\lVert S_{k+1}u_{k+1}\right\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim 2^{(k+1)(s-\beta)}\left\lVert S_{k+1}u_{k+1}\right\rVert_{E^{\beta}}\lesssim 2^{(k+1)(s-\beta)}\left\lVert u_{k+1}\right\rVert_{E^{\beta}}\\
\lesssim K_{1}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{(k+1)(s-\beta)},

where we have again used (
2.3.30
). Thus,
v_{k+1}
obeys the bound (
2.3.5
) for
j=k+1
provided that
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
satisfy

(2.3.37)

K_{1}\lesssim K_{2},

which we henceforth assume, and in this case (
2.3.1
) in turn implies that

(2.3.38)

\left\lVert v_{k+1}\right\rVert_{E^{r}}\leqslant\left\lVert v_{k+1}\right\rVert_{E^{\beta}}\leqslant K_{2}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}<\delta_{R}.

Third, we introduce some useful estimates for the terms
\{e_{j}\}_{j=0}^{k}
. For
0\leqslant j\leqslant k
we know that
h_{j}=L(v_{j})f_{j}
, and so
f_{j}=D\Psi(v_{j})h_{j}
by the RI mapping hypotheses. We may thus invoke the claim established in Step 2 in order to see that for
0\leqslant j\leqslant k
(
2.3.14
) implies that

(2.3.39)

\left\lVert e_{j}\right\rVert_{F^{s}}=\left\lVert\Psi(u_{j}+h_{j})-\Psi(u_{j})-f_{j}\right\rVert_{F^{s}}=\left\lVert\Psi(u_{j}+h_{j})-\Psi(u_{j})-D\Psi(v_{j})h_{j}\right\rVert_{F^{s}}\\
\lesssim C_{1}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{j(s+\mu+r-2\beta)}

for all
s\in\mathbb{N}\cap[r-\mu,R-\mu]
.

Fourth, we turn our attention to defining
y_{k+1}
. If
k=0
, then we simply set
y_{1}=-S_{1}e_{0}\in F^{N}
, while if
k\geqslant 1
then we set
y_{k+1}=-S_{k+1}\sum_{n=0}^{k}e_{n}-\sum_{n=0}^{k}y_{n}\in F^{N}
. We may then invoke the claim of Step 3 to see that the formula

(2.3.40)

y_{j}=-S_{j}e_{j-1}-\Updelta_{j-1}\sum_{n=0}^{j-2}e_{n}

holds whenever
1\leqslant j\leqslant k+1
, provided that we understand sums over empty ranges to mean zero. Next we use (
2.3.39
) to estimate
y_{k+1}
. Initially we use the smoothing operator properties together with (
2.3.39
) to estimate

(2.3.41)

\left\lVert S_{k+1}e_{k}\right\rVert_{F^{s}}\lesssim\begin{cases}\left\lVert e_{k}\right\rVert_{F^{s}}&\text{if }r-\mu\leqslant s\leqslant R-\mu\\
2^{(k+1)(s-R+\mu)}\left\lVert e_{k}\right\rVert_{F^{R-\mu}}&\text{if }R-\mu\leqslant s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket\end{cases}\lesssim C_{1}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{k(s+\mu+r-2\beta)}

for every
r-\mu\leqslant s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
. Similarly, if
k\geqslant 1
then we can use the fact that
R+r-2\beta>0
to bound

(2.3.42)

\Big\lVert\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\Updelta_{k}e_{j}\Big\rVert_{F^{s}}\leqslant\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\left\lVert\Updelta_{k}e_{j}\right\rVert_{F^{s}}\lesssim 2^{k(s-R+\mu)}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\left\lVert\Updelta_{k}e_{j}\right\rVert_{F^{R-\mu}}\lesssim 2^{k(s-R+\mu)}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\left\lVert e_{j}\right\rVert_{F^{R-\mu}}\\
\lesssim C_{1}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{k(s-R+\mu)}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}2^{j(R+r-2\beta)}\lesssim C_{1}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{k(s+\mu+r-2\beta)}

for every
r-\mu\leqslant s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
. Synthesizing (
2.3.40
), (
2.3.41
), and (
2.3.42
), we deduce that

(2.3.43)

\left\lVert y_{k+1}\right\rVert_{F^{s}}\lesssim C_{1}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{k(s+\mu+r-2\beta)}

for every
r-\mu\leqslant s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
.

As the penultimate update we define
f_{k+1}=\Updelta_{k+1}g+y_{k+1}\in F^{N}
. We know that
v_{k+1}
satisfies (
2.3.38
), so the operator
L(v_{k+1})
exists, and we may make the final update by setting
h_{k+1}=L(v_{k+1})f_{k+1}\in E^{R}
. The tame estimates for
L(v_{k+1})
then provide for the bound

(2.3.44)

\left\lVert h_{k+1}\right\rVert_{E^{s}}\leqslant C_{2}(\left\lVert\Updelta_{k+1}g\right\rVert_{F^{s}}+\left\lVert y_{k+1}\right\rVert_{F^{s}})+C_{2}\langle\left\lVert v_{k+1}\right\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}\rangle(\left\lVert\Updelta_{k+1}g\right\rVert_{F^{r}}+\left\lVert y_{k+1}\right\rVert_{F^{r}})

for
s\in\mathbb{N}\cap[r,R]
. From (
2.3.5
), which we established above holds for
j=k+1
, and (
2.3.1
) we may estimate
\left\lVert v_{k+1}\right\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}\lesssim K_{4}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{(k+1)(s+\mu-\beta)}\lesssim 2^{(k+1)(s+\mu-\beta)}
. By plugging this and (
2.3.43
) into (
2.3.44
), we then find that

(2.3.45)

\left\lVert h_{k+1}\right\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim C_{2}\left(\left\lVert\Updelta_{k+1}g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{(k+1)(s-\beta)}+C_{1}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{k(s+\mu+r-2\beta)}\right)\\
+C_{2}\langle 2^{(k+1)(s+\mu-\beta)}\rangle\left(\left\lVert\Updelta_{k+1}g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{(k+1)(r-\beta)}+C_{1}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{k(2r+\mu-2\beta)}\right)\\
\lesssim C_{2}(1+C_{1})2^{(k+1)(s-\beta)}(\left\lVert\Updelta_{k+1}g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}(2^{(k+1)(r-s)}+2^{(k+1)(\mu+r-\beta)})\\
+\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}(2^{(k+1)(\mu+r-\beta)}+2^{(k+1)(\mu+2r-s-\beta)}+2^{(k+1)(2r+2\mu-\beta)}).

To consolidate all of the exponents we recall that that
r-s\leqslant 0
and
1=\beta-2r-2\mu>0
, which show that
\mu+r-\beta\leqslant 2\mu+2r-\beta=-1\leqslant 0
and
\mu+2r-s-\beta\leqslant\mu+r-\beta\leqslant-1
. Hence, the previous estimate implies that
\left\lVert h_{k+1}\right\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim C_{2}(1+C_{1})2^{(k+1)(s-\beta)}(\left\lVert\Updelta_{k+1}g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}+\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{-(k+1)})
, and we deduce that
h_{k+1}
obeys (
2.3.4
) for
j=k+1
, provided that
K_{1}
satisfies

(2.3.46)

C_{2}(1+C_{1})\lesssim K_{1},

which we assume holds.

We have now established conditions on
K_{1}
,
K_{2}
,
K_{3}
, and
K_{4}
that are sufficient for the construction of
(u_{k+1},v_{k+1},h_{k+1},y_{k+1},f_{k+1},e_{k+1})\in(B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{R})\cap E^{R})\times E^{N}\times E^{R}\times F^{N}\times F^{N}\times F^{R-\mu}
, namely (
2.3.28
), (
2.3.31
), (
2.3.35
), (
2.3.37
), and (
2.3.46
). It is a simple matter to choose parameters satisfying these conditions, and so the inductive step is complete provided these parameters are chosen. We thus have the desired sequence. Note that (
2.3.8
) follows as above from the claim established in Step 2.

Step 5
: Convergence of the sequence. With the sequence in hand from Step 4, we use the claim from Step 1 with
0\leqslant\ell\leqslant k
and the fact that
u_{k+1}-u_{\ell}=\sum_{n=\ell}^{k}h_{\ell}
to see that

(2.3.47)

\lVert u_{k+1}-u_{\ell}\rVert_{E^{\beta}}\leqslant\sum_{n=\ell}^{k}\left\lVert h_{n}\right\rVert_{E^{\beta}}\lesssim K_{1}\Big(\sum_{n=\ell}^{\infty}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}^{2}2^{-2n}+\left\lVert\Updelta_{n}g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\to 0

as
\ell\to\infty
. Thus,
\{u_{j}\}_{j=0}^{\infty}
is a Cauchy sequence in
E^{\beta}
, and hence convergent to some
u\in E^{\beta}
. Sending
j\to\infty
in (
2.3.7
) shows that
\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{E^{\beta}}\leqslant K_{4}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}
.

It remains only to show that
\Psi(u)=g
. To this end, we again telescope and use that
\Psi(0)=\Psi(u_{0})=0
and (
1
) to write, for
