k\geqslant 2
,

(2.3.48)

\Psi(u_{k+1})=\sum_{j=0}^{k}(\Psi(u_{k+1})-\Psi(u_{k}))=\sum_{j=0}^{k}(\Psi(u_{k}+h_{k})-\Psi(u_{k}))=\sum_{j=0}^{k}(e_{j}+f_{j})\\
=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\Updelta_{j}g+\sum_{j=0}^{k}e_{j}+\sum_{j=0}^{k}y_{j}=S_{k+1}g+\sum_{j=0}^{k}e_{j}-S_{k}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}e_{j}=S_{k+1}g+e_{k}+(I-S_{k})\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}e_{j}.

Now, we know from the properties of the smoothing operators, which are given in Definition
2.11
, that
\left\lVert(I-S_{k+1})g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}\to 0
as
k\to\infty
,
while (
2.3.8
) implies that

(2.3.49)

\left\lVert e_{k}\right\rVert_{F^{\beta-\mu}}\lesssim C_{1}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{k(r-\beta)}\to 0\text{ as }k\to\infty

and (observing that
\beta-\mu-1\geqslant 2r+\mu\geqslant 1
)

(2.3.50)

\Big\lVert(I-S_{k})\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}e_{j}\Big\rVert_{F^{\beta-\mu-1}}\leqslant\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\left\lVert(I-S_{k})e_{j}\right\rVert_{F^{\beta-\mu-1}}\lesssim 2^{-k}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\lVert(I-S_{k})e_{j}\rVert_{F^{\beta-\mu}}\\
\lesssim C_{1}\lVert g\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{-k}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}2^{j(r-\beta)}\lesssim C_{1}\lVert g\rVert_{F^{\beta}}2^{-k}\to 0\text{ as }k\to\infty.

Upon combining these, we find that
\left\lVert\Psi(u_{k+1})-g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta-\mu-1}}\to 0
as
k\to\infty
but since
u_{k}\to u
in
E^{\beta}
as
k\to\infty
, the continuity of
\Psi
guarantees that
\Psi(u_{k+1})\to\Psi(u)
in
F^{\beta-\mu}
. Thus,
\Psi(u)=g
.

Step 6
: Higher regularity. Let
\mathbb{N}\ni\upnu\leqslant R+r-2\beta
and now suppose additionally that
g\in F^{\beta+\upnu}
. We wish to prove that the solution
u\in E^{\beta}
constructed in Step 5 actually belongs to
E^{\beta+\upnu}
and satisfies
\lVert u\rVert_{E^{\beta+\upnu}}\lesssim\lVert g\rVert_{F^{\beta+\upnu}}
. We will establish this via finite induction. The proposition to be proved inductively is the following statement, depending on
\nu\in\{{0,1,\dots,\upnu}\}
: if
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
s\in\mathbb{N}\cap[r,R]
, then

(2.3.51)

\lVert h_{j}\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim 2^{j(s-(\beta+\nu))}(\lVert\Updelta_{j}g\rVert_{F^{\beta+\nu}}+2^{-j}\lVert g\rVert_{F^{\beta+\nu}})

for an implicit constant depending on
\nu
, as well as
K_{1},\dots,K_{4}
,
A_{\beta},A_{\beta+\nu}
,
\mu
,
r
,
\delta_{R}
, and
R
. The case
\nu=0
was established in the fourth step, in particular in the verification of (
2.3.4
). Assume now that for some
\nu\in\{{0,1,\dots,\upnu-1}\}
we have that the induction hypothesis (
2.3.51
) holds at level
\nu
. We wish to prove it at level
\nu+1
.

We begin by mimicking Step 1 and deriving improved bounds on the sequence
\{{u_{j}}\}_{j=0}^{\infty}
. For
n\in\mathbb{N}
we set
\xi^{\nu}_{n}=\lVert\Updelta_{n}g\rVert_{F^{\beta+\nu}}+2^{-n}\lVert g\rVert_{F^{\beta+\nu}}
. By arguing as in the first step, we can show that for any
0\leqslant j\leqslant k
we have the estimate

(2.3.52)

\Big\lVert\sum_{n=\ell}^{k}h_{n}\Big\rVert_{E^{\beta+\nu}}\lesssim\Big(\sum_{n=\ell}^{k}(\xi^{\nu}_{n})^{2}\Big)^{1/2}.

Since
g\in F^{\beta+\upnu}
, we deduce from (
2.1.22
) that
\{{\xi^{\nu}_{n}}\}_{n=0}^{\infty}\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N})
; hence, the sequence of partial sums
\{{\sum_{n=0}^{j}h_{n}}\}_{j=0}^{\infty}\subset E^{\beta+\nu}
is Cauchy. We have already established convergence of the scheme, giving that
u=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}h_{j}
in
E^{\beta}
. Therefore,
u\in E^{\beta+\nu}
and, from (
2.3.52
), we deduce that for all
j\in\mathbb{N}

(2.3.53)

\lVert u_{j}\rVert_{E^{\beta+\nu}}\lesssim\Big(\sum_{n=0}^{j-1}(\xi^{\nu}_{n})^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\lesssim\lVert g\rVert_{F^{\beta+\nu}}.

Now we derive improved bounds on the sequence
\{{v_{j}}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
. From properties (
2.1.17
) and (
2.1.18
) of the smoothing operators and the improved bounds of (
2.3.53
), we deduce for
\beta+\nu\leqslant s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
that

(2.3.54)

\lVert v_{j}\rVert_{E^{s}}=\lVert S_{j}u_{j}\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim 2^{j(s-(\beta+\nu))}\lVert u_{j}\rVert_{E^{\beta+\nu}}\lesssim 2^{j(s-(\beta+\nu))}\lVert g\rVert_{F^{\beta+\nu}}.

Next, we bound the sequence
\{{u_{j}-v_{j}}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
. Thanks to Lemma
2.19
, for
s\in\mathbb{N}\cap[0,R]
we have the bound

(2.3.55)

\lVert u_{j}-v_{j}\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim\lVert u_{j}-v_{j}\rVert_{E^{0}}^{1-s/R}\lVert u_{j}-v_{j}\rVert_{E^{R}}^{s/R}.

The
E^{0}
-norm term we bound using properties (
2.1.17
) and (
2.1.19
) of the smoothing operators and estimate (
2.3.53
):

(2.3.56)

\lVert u_{j}-v_{j}\rVert_{E^{0}}=\lVert(I-S_{j})u_{j}\rVert_{E^{0}}\lesssim 2^{-j(\beta+\nu)}\lVert u_{j}\rVert_{E^{\beta+\nu}}\lesssim 2^{-j(\beta+\nu)}\lVert g\rVert_{E^{\beta+\nu}}.

On the other hand, the
E^{R}
term is handled via (
2.1.17
), the telescoping identity
u_{j}=\sum_{n=0}^{j-1}h_{n}
, induction hypothesis (
2.3.51
), and the bound
\xi^{\nu}_{n}\lesssim\lVert g\rVert_{F^{\beta+\nu}}
:

(2.3.57)

\lVert u_{j}-v_{j}\rVert_{E^{R}}\leqslant\sum_{n=0}^{j-1}\lVert(I-S_{j})h_{n}\rVert_{E^{R}}\lesssim\sum_{n=0}^{j-1}\lVert h_{n}\rVert_{E^{R}}\lesssim\lVert g\rVert_{F^{\beta+\nu}}\sum_{n=0}^{j-1}2^{n(R-(\beta+\nu))}\lesssim 2^{j(R-(\beta+\nu))}\lVert g\rVert_{F^{\beta+\nu}}.

We synthesize (
2.3.55
), (
2.3.56
), and (
2.3.57
) to get

(2.3.58)

\lVert u_{j}-v_{j}\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim 2^{j(s-(\beta+\nu))}\lVert g\rVert_{F^{\beta+\nu}}.

Our next endeavor is to derive improved bounds on the sequence
\{{e_{j}}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
. We turn to estimate (
2.3.18
) for
s\in[r-\mu,R-\mu]\cap\mathbb{N}
. To handle the right hand side, we invoke the following bounds:

(2.3.59)

\begin{split}\max\{{\lVert u_{j}-v_{j}\rVert_{E^{r}},\lVert h_{j}\rVert_{E^{r}}}\}&\lesssim 2^{j(r-\beta)}\min\{{1,2^{-j\nu}\lVert g\rVert_{F^{\beta+\nu}}}\},\\
\max\{{\lVert u_{j}-v_{j}\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}},\lVert h_{j}\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}}\}&\lesssim 2^{j(s+\mu-\beta)}\min\{{1,2^{-j\nu}\lVert g\rVert_{F^{\beta+\nu}}}\},\\
\lVert u_{j},v_{j}\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}&\lesssim 2^{j\max\{{0,s+\mu-\beta}\}},\end{split}

which are consequences of (
2.3.4
), (
2.3.6
), (
2.3.1
), (
2.3.51
), (
2.3.58
), and finally (
2.3.21
). In this way we acquire the estimate

(2.3.60)

\lVert e_{j}\rVert_{F^{s}}\lesssim 2^{j(s+\mu+r-2\beta-\nu)}\lVert g\rVert_{F^{\beta+\nu}}.

Now we estimate the sequence
\{{y_{j}}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
. For this we recall identity (
2.3.26
). By arguing as in (
2.3.41
), but using the bounds established in (
2.3.60
) for
\{{e_{j}}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
, we learn that for
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
r-\mu\leqslant s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket

(2.3.61)

\lVert S_{j+1}e_{j}\rVert_{F^{s}}\lesssim 2^{j(s+\mu+r-2\beta-\nu)}\lVert g\rVert_{F^{\beta+\nu}}.

Similarly, by arguing as in (
2.3.42
), but instead using (
2.3.60
) with
s=R-\mu
and the fact that
R+r-2\beta-\nu>0
(since
\nu<\upnu
), we gain the bound

(2.3.62)

\Big\lVert\sum_{n=0}^{j-1}\Updelta_{j}e_{n}\Big\rVert_{F^{s}}\lesssim 2^{j(s+\mu+r-2\beta-\nu)}\lVert g\rVert_{F^{\beta+\nu}}.

We combine (
2.3.61
) and (
2.3.62
) to see that for
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
r-\mu\leqslant s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
,

(2.3.63)

\lVert y_{j}\rVert_{F^{s}}\lesssim 2^{(j-1)(s+\mu+r-2\beta-\nu)}\lVert g\rVert_{F^{\beta+\nu}}.

At last, we are ready to obtain an improved estimate on the sequence
\{{h_{j}}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
and close the induction. By using the identity
h_{j}=L(v_{j})(\Updelta_{j}g+y_{j})
with the right inverse estimates of equation (
2.2.2
), we find that for
j\in\mathbb{N}
,

(2.3.64)

\lVert h_{j}\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim\lVert\Updelta_{j}g\rVert_{F^{s}}+\lVert y_{j}\rVert_{F^{s}}+\langle\lVert v_{j}\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}\rangle(\lVert\Updelta_{j}g\rVert_{F^{r}}+\lVert y_{j}\rVert_{E^{r}})\text{ for }s\in[r,R]\cap\mathbb{N}.

We estimate
\lVert y_{j}\rVert_{F^{s}}
and
\lVert y_{j}\rVert_{F^{r}}
according to the improved estimates of (
2.3.63
), the
\Updelta_{j}g
norms are handled via smoothing estimate (
2.1.20
), i.e.
\lVert\Updelta_{j}g\rVert_{F^{k}}=2^{j(k-(\beta+\nu+1))}\lVert\Updelta_{j}g\rVert_{F^{\beta+\nu+1}}
for
k\in\{{r,s}\}
, and the
v_{j}
-term is estimated according to (
2.1.17
) and (
2.3.7
) in the case
s\leqslant\beta
and via (
2.3.5
). Hence estimate (
2.3.64
) yields

(2.3.65)

\lVert h_{j}\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim 2^{j(s-(\beta+\nu+1))}\lVert\Updelta_{j}g\rVert_{F^{\beta+\nu+1}}+2^{j(s+\mu+r-2\beta-\nu)}\lVert g\rVert_{F^{\beta+\nu}}\\
+\langle 2^{j(s-\beta)}\rangle(2^{j(r-(\beta+\nu+1))}\lVert\Updelta_{j}g\rVert_{F^{\beta+\nu+1}}+2^{j(\mu+2r-2\beta-\nu)}\lVert g\rVert_{F^{\beta+\nu}}).

Upon regrouping, we acquire the bound

(2.3.66)

\lVert h_{j}\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim 2^{j(s-(\beta+\nu+1))}(1+\langle 2^{j(s-\beta)}\rangle 2^{j(r-s)})\lVert\Updelta_{j}g\rVert_{F^{\beta+\nu+1}}\\
+2^{j(s-(\beta+\nu+1))}2^{-j}(2^{j(2+\mu+r-\beta)}+\langle 2^{j(s-\beta)}\rangle 2^{j(2+\mu+2r-\beta-s))})\lVert g\rVert_{F^{\beta+\nu}}.

Since
r\leqslant\min\{{s,\beta}\}
, we have that

(2.3.67)

1+\langle 2^{j(s-\beta)}\rangle 2^{j(r-s)}\lesssim 1.

The inequalities
1\leqslant\mu+r
and
2(\mu+r)<\beta
imply that

(2.3.68)

2^{j(2+\mu+r-\beta)}\lesssim 1.

The inequality
\mu\geqslant 1
implies that
\min\{{2\beta,s+\beta}\}\geqslant\beta\geqslant 1+2(\mu+r)\geqslant 2+\mu+2r
and hence

(2.3.69)

\langle 2^{j(s-\beta)}\rangle 2^{j(2+\mu+2r-\beta-s)}\lesssim 1.

We combine inequalities (
2.3.66
), (
2.3.67
), (
2.3.68
), and (
2.3.69
) to see that

(2.3.70)

\lVert h_{j}\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim 2^{j(s-(\beta+\nu+1))}(\lVert\Updelta_{j}g\rVert_{F^{\beta+\nu+1}}+2^{-j}\lVert g\rVert_{F^{\beta+\nu}}).

This means that the inductive proposition (
2.3.51
) has been verified for
\nu+1
, and hence the induction is complete. We therefore know that (
2.3.51
) holds for
\nu=\upnu
. We then argue as in (
2.3.52
) and (
2.3.53
) with the sequence
\{{\xi^{\upnu}_{n}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
to deduce that
u\in E^{\beta+\upnu}
with the estimate
\lVert u\rVert_{E^{\beta+\upnu}}\lesssim\lVert g\rVert_{F^{\beta+\upnu}}
.
∎

We now complement the previous local surjectivity result with the following local injectivity result, which has no analog in Baldi and Haus
[
5
]
but is analogous to a result of Hamilton
[
40
]
. We emphasize that in the following we only need left inverses and weaker forms of the tame estimates than in the LRI mapping hypotheses.

Theorem 2.26

(Local injectivity)
.

Let
\mathbf{E}=\{E^{s}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
and
\mathbf{F}=\{F^{s}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
be Banach scales over the same field, and suppose that
\mathbf{E}
is terminally dense in the sense of Definition
2.2
. Let
\sigma,\mu\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
\sigma+\mu\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
. Assume that there exists
\gamma_{\sigma}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and a
C^{2}
map
\Psi:B_{E^{\sigma+\mu}}(0,\gamma_{\sigma})\to F^{\sigma}
such that the following hold.

(1)

For every
u_{0}\in B_{E^{\sigma+\mu}}(0,\gamma_{\sigma})
we have the bound

(2.3.71)

\left\lVert D^{2}\Psi(u_{0})[v,w]\right\rVert_{F^{\sigma}}\lesssim\left\langle\left\lVert u_{0}\right\rVert_{E^{\sigma+\mu}}\right\rangle(\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{E^{\sigma+\mu}}\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{E^{\sigma}}+\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{E^{\sigma}}\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{E^{\sigma+\mu}}).

(2)

For every
u_{0}\in B_{E^{\sigma+\mu}}(0,\gamma_{\sigma})\cap E^{N}
there exists a bounded linear operator
L(u_{0}):F^{\sigma}\to E^{\sigma}
satisfying the following two conditions.

(a)

L(u_{0})D\Psi(u_{0})v=v
for every
v\in E^{\sigma+\mu}
.

(b)

We have the estimate
\left\lVert L(u_{0})f\right\rVert_{E^{\sigma}}\lesssim\left\langle\left\lVert u_{0}\right\rVert_{E^{\sigma+\mu}}\right\rangle\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{F^{\sigma}}
for every
f\in F^{\sigma}
.

Then there exists
0<\delta_{\operatorname*{inj},\sigma}\leqslant\gamma_{\sigma}/4
such that if
u_{0}-u_{1}\in B_{E^{\sigma+\mu}}(0,2\delta_{\operatorname*{inj},\sigma})
, with
u_{i}\in B_{E^{\sigma+\mu}}(0,\gamma_{\sigma})
for
i\in\{{0,1}\}
, then

(2.3.72)

\left\lVert u_{1}-u_{0}\right\rVert_{E^{\sigma}}\lesssim\left\lVert\Psi(u_{1})-\Psi(u_{0})\right\rVert_{F^{\sigma}}.

In particular, the restriction
\Psi:B_{E^{\sigma+\mu}}(0,\delta_{\operatorname*{inj},\sigma})\to F^{\sigma}
is injective.

Proof.

Suppose initially that
u_{0}-u_{1}\in B_{E^{\sigma+\mu}}(0,2\delta)\cap E^{N}
and
u_{i}\in B_{E^{\sigma+\mu}}(0,\gamma_{\sigma})
for some
0<\delta\leqslant\gamma_{\sigma}/4
. We may use Taylor’s theorem to write

(2.3.73)

\Psi(u_{1})-\Psi(u_{0})=D\Psi(u_{0})(u_{1}-u_{0})+\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)D^{2}\Psi((1-t)u_{0}+tu_{1})[u_{1}-u_{0},u_{1}-u_{0}]\;\mathrm{d}t,

with the understanding that this equality holds in the space
F^{\sigma}
. We then apply the bounded linear map
L(u_{0}):F^{\sigma}\to E^{\sigma}
and rearrange to see that

(2.3.74)

u_{1}-u_{0}=L(u_{0})\Big(\Psi(u_{1})-\Psi(u_{0})-\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)D^{2}\Psi((1-t)u_{0}+tu_{1})[u_{1}-u_{0},u_{1}-u_{0}]\;\mathrm{d}t\Big).

Next we couple the identity (
2.3.74
) to the estimate for
L(u_{0})
and the trivial bound
\left\langle\left\lVert u_{0}\right\rVert_{E^{\sigma+\mu}}\right\rangle\lesssim 1
to see that

(2.3.75)

\left\lVert u_{1}-u_{0}\right\rVert_{E^{\sigma}}\lesssim\left\lVert\Psi(u_{1})-\Psi(u_{0})\right\rVert_{F^{\sigma}}+\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)\left\lVert D^{2}\Psi((1-t)u_{0}+tu_{1})[u_{1}-u_{0},u_{1}-u_{0}]\right\rVert_{F^{\sigma}}\;\mathrm{d}t.

For the latter term we use the
C^{2}
estimate to bound

(2.3.76)

\left\lVert D^{2}\Psi((1-t)u_{0}+tu_{1})[u_{1}-u_{0},u_{1}-u_{0}]\right\rVert_{F^{\sigma}}\lesssim\\
\left\langle\left\lVert(1-t)u_{0}+tu_{1}\right\rVert_{E^{\sigma+\mu}}\right\rangle\left\lVert u_{1}-u_{0}\right\rVert_{E^{\sigma+\mu}}\left\lVert u_{1}-u_{0}\right\rVert_{E^{\sigma}}\lesssim\left\lVert u_{1}-u_{0}\right\rVert_{E^{\sigma+\mu}}\left\lVert u_{1}-u_{0}\right\rVert_{E^{\sigma}}

for every
t\in[0,1]
. We then plug this bound into (
2.3.75
) to see that

(2.3.77)

\left\lVert u_{1}-u_{0}\right\rVert_{E^{\sigma}}\lesssim\left\lVert\Psi(u_{1})-\Psi(u_{0})\right\rVert_{F^{\sigma}}+\left\lVert u_{1}-u_{0}\right\rVert_{E^{\sigma+\mu}}\left\lVert u_{1}-u_{0}\right\rVert_{E^{\sigma}}\\
\lesssim\left\lVert\Psi(u_{1})-\Psi(u_{0})\right\rVert_{F^{\sigma}}+\delta\left\lVert u_{1}-u_{0}\right\rVert_{E^{\sigma}}.

From this we readily deduce the existence of
0<\delta_{\operatorname*{inj}}\leqslant\gamma_{\sigma}/4
such that if
\delta\leqslant\delta_{\operatorname*{inj}}
then we can absorb the right-most term onto the left to conclude that

(2.3.78)

\left\lVert u_{1}-u_{0}\right\rVert_{E^{\sigma}}\lesssim\left\lVert\Psi(u_{1})-\Psi(u_{0})\right\rVert_{F^{\sigma}}\text{ for all }u_{0}-u_{1}\in B_{\sigma+\mu}(0,2\delta_{\operatorname*{inj}})\cap E^{N}.

Estimate (
2.3.78
) is not quite the desired result since it requires
u_{0},u_{1}\in E^{N}
, but we can use the fact that
\mathbf{E}
is terminally dense to promote this result. Indeed, given
u_{0},u_{1}\in B_{E^{\sigma+\mu}}(0,\gamma_{\sigma})
such that
u_{0}-u_{1}\in B_{E^{\sigma+\mu}}(0,2\delta_{\operatorname*{inj}})
we can pick

\{u^{n}_{i}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq E^{N}
such that
u^{n}_{i}\to u_{i}
in
E^{\sigma+\mu}
for
i\in\{0,1\}
. Since
B_{E^{\sigma+\mu}}(0,2\delta_{\operatorname*{inj}})
and
B_{E^{\sigma+\mu}}(0,\gamma_{\sigma})
are open, we may assume without loss of generality, that
\{u^{n}_{0}-u^{n}_{1}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq B_{E^{\sigma+\mu}}(0,2\delta_{\operatorname*{inj}})
and for
i\in\{{0,1}\}
,
\{{u_{i}^{n}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset B_{E^{\sigma+\mu}}(0,\gamma_{\sigma})\cap E^{N}
. We then apply (
2.3.78
) to the sequence to see that
\left\lVert u_{1}^{n}-u_{0}^{n}\right\rVert_{E^{\sigma}}\lesssim\left\lVert\Psi(u_{1}^{n})-\Psi(u_{0}^{n})\right\rVert_{F^{\sigma}}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. By sending
n\to\infty
and using the continuity of
\Psi:B_{E^{\sigma+\mu}}(0,\gamma_{\sigma})\to F^{\sigma}
, we deduce that
\left\lVert u_{1}-u_{0}\right\rVert_{E^{\sigma}}\lesssim\left\lVert\Psi(u_{1})-\Psi(u_{0})\right\rVert_{F^{\sigma}}
for all
u_{0}-u_{1}\in B_{E^{\sigma+\mu}}(0,2\delta_{\operatorname*{inj}})
, with
\;u_{0},u_{1}\in B_{E^{\sigma+\mu}}(0,\gamma_{\sigma})
which is (
2.3.72
).
∎

2.4.
Proof of the inverse function theorem

We are now ready for the proofs of the inverse function theorem. The proofs are given under conditions
I
and
II
separately.

Proof of Theorem
2.21
, assuming
I
.

Assume that condition
I
is satisfied, i.e.
\mathbf{E}
and
\mathbf{F}
are LP-smoothable.

First note that Lemma
2.18
implies that
\mathbf{E}
is terminally dense. This and the LRI mapping hypotheses from Definition
2.20
imply the hypotheses of Theorem
2.26
with
\sigma=\beta-\mu
and
\gamma_{\sigma}=\delta_{R}\leqslant\delta_{r}.
Let
\delta_{\operatorname*{inj},\sigma}>0
be the constant from Theorem
2.26
. Then the theorem tells us that
\Psi:B_{E^{\beta}}(0,\delta_{\operatorname*{inj},\sigma})\to F^{\beta-\mu}
is injective and obeys the estimate (
2.3.72
).

On the other hand, by hypothesis, we know that the inequality
2(r+\mu)<\beta<(r+R)/2
is satisfied. Let
K_{1}
,
K_{2}
,
K_{3}
, and
K_{4}
be the constants from Theorem
2.25
and let
\varepsilon_{\operatorname*{surj}}>0
denote the constant appearing on the right side of (
2.3.1
). Then Theorem
2.25
guarantees that for every
g\in B_{F^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon_{\operatorname*{surj}})
there exists
u\in E^{\beta}
satisfying
\Psi(u)=g
as well as the bound

(2.4.1)

\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{E^{\beta}}\leqslant K_{4}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}.

Set
\varepsilon=\min\{\varepsilon_{\operatorname*{surj}},\delta_{\operatorname*{inj},\sigma}/K_{4}\}
,
\kappa_{1}=K_{4}
, and
\kappa_{2}>0
to be the constant on the right side of (
2.3.72
). Since
\kappa_{1}\varepsilon\leqslant\delta_{\operatorname*{inj},\sigma}
, the above analysis shows that for every
g\in B_{F^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon)
there exists a unique
u\in B_{E^{\beta}}(0,\kappa_{1}\varepsilon)
such that
\Psi(u)=g
. Moreover, the induced map
\Psi^{-1}:B_{F^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon)\to\Psi^{-1}(B_{F^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon))\cap B_{E^{\beta}}(0,\kappa_{1}\varepsilon)
satisfies (
2.2.4
) and (
2.2.7
) in light of (
2.4.1
) and (
2.3.72
).

Now suppose that
\mathbb{N}\ni\nu\leqslant R+r-2\beta
. From the fourth item of Theorem
2.25
, we deduce (
2.2.5
) and (
2.2.6
), i.e. that the inverse
\Psi^{-1}
sends
B_{F^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon)\cap F^{\beta+\nu}
to
E^{\beta+\nu}
with the tame estimate
\lVert\Psi^{-1}(g)\rVert_{E^{\beta+\nu}}\lesssim\lVert g\rVert_{F^{\beta+\nu}}
.
∎

A similar, but slightly more involved argument is needed to prove the inverse function theorem under assumption
II
.

Proof of Theorem
2.21
, assuming
II
.

Assume that condition
II
is satisfied, i.e.
\mathbf{E}
is tame and
\mathbf{F}
is a direct summand of
\mathbf{E}
.

Since
\mathbf{E}
is tame, there exists an LP-smoothable Banach scale
\mathbf{G}=\{G^{s}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
such that
\mathbf{E}
is a tame direct summand of
\mathbf{G}
with lifting and restriction operators
\lambda_{\mathbf{E}}:E^{0}\to G^{0}
and
\rho_{\mathbf{E}}:G^{0}\to E^{0}
. Similarly, since
\mathbf{F}
is a tame direct summand of
\mathbf{E}
we can pick associated lifting and restriction operators
\lambda_{\mathbf{F}}:F^{0}\to E^{0}
and
\rho_{\mathbf{F}}:E^{0}\to F^{0}
.

Before proceeding, we need to introduce three bits of notation. First, we define
\mathbf{H}=\mathbf{G}^{3}
, endowed with the
2-
norm for the sake of definiteness, which makes
\mathbf{H}
into an LP-smoothable Banach scale thanks to Lemma
2.17
. Next, for
s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
write
Q_{s}=\left\lVert\rho_{\mathbf{E}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(G^{s};E^{s})}
. Then by construction we have that

(2.4.2)

\rho_{\mathbf{E}}(B_{G^{s}}(0,\delta/Q_{s}))\subseteq B_{E^{s}}(0,\delta)\text{ for all }s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket,0<\delta.

Third, we define the map
\Phi:B_{H^{r}}(0,\delta_{r}/Q_{r})\to G^{r-\mu}
via

(2.4.3)

\Phi(u,v,w)=\lambda_{\mathbf{E}}(\lambda_{\mathbf{F}}\Psi(\rho_{\mathbf{E}}u)+(I-\lambda_{\mathbf{F}}\rho_{\mathbf{F}})\rho_{\mathbf{E}}v)+(I-\lambda_{\mathbf{E}}\rho_{\mathbf{E}})w,

which is well-defined in light of (
2.4.2
) and the fact that
\Psi:B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{r})\to F^{r-\mu}
.

We now claim that
(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G},\Phi)
satisfy the RI mapping hypotheses with parameters
(\mu,r,R)
. Clearly,
\Phi(0)=0
. Now let
r-\mu\leqslant s\in\llbracket N-\mu\rrbracket
. By the mapping properties of
\Psi
and the lifting and restriction operators, we readily deduce that
\Phi:B_{H^{r}}(0,\delta_{r}/Q_{r})\cap H^{s+1}\to G^{s}
is
C^{2}
and satisfies

(2.4.4)

D\Phi(u_{0},v_{0},w_{0})(a,b,c)=\lambda_{\mathbf{E}}(\lambda_{\mathbf{F}}D\Psi(\rho_{\mathbf{E}}u_{0})\rho_{\mathbf{E}}a+(I-\lambda_{\mathbf{F}}\rho_{\mathbf{F}})\rho_{\mathbf{E}}b)+(I-\lambda_{\mathbf{E}}\rho_{\mathbf{E}})c

and

(2.4.5)

D^{2}\Phi(u_{0},v_{0},w_{0})[(a_{1},b_{1},c_{1}),(a_{2},b_{2},c_{2})]=\lambda_{\mathbf{E}}\lambda_{\mathbf{F}}D^{2}\Psi(\rho_{\mathbf{E}}u_{0})[\rho_{\mathbf{E}}a_{1},\rho_{\mathbf{E}}a_{2}].

In turn, the tame
C^{2}
estimate for
D^{2}\Psi
and (
2.4.5
) imply that

(2.4.6)

\left\lVert D^{2}\Phi(u_{0},v_{0},w_{0})[(a_{1},b_{1},c_{1}),(a_{2},b_{2},c_{2})]\right\rVert_{H^{s}}\\
\leqslant C^{\prime}_{1}(s)[\left\lVert(a_{1},b_{1},c_{1})\right\rVert_{H^{s+\mu}}\left\lVert(a_{2},b_{2},c_{2})\right\rVert_{H^{r}}+\left\lVert(a_{1},b_{1},c_{1})\right\rVert_{H^{r}}\left\lVert(a_{2},b_{2},c_{2})\right\rVert_{H^{s+\mu}}]\\
+C^{\prime}_{1}(s)\left\langle\left\lVert(u_{0},v_{0},w_{0})\right\rVert_{H^{s+\mu}}\right\rangle\left\lVert(a_{1},b_{1},c_{1})\right\rVert_{H^{r}}\left\lVert(a_{2},b_{2},c_{2})\right\rVert_{H^{r}}

for

(2.4.7)

C^{\prime}_{1}(s)=C_{1}(s)\left\lVert\lambda_{\mathbf{E}}\lambda_{\mathbf{F}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(F^{s};G^{s})}(1+Q_{s+\mu})(Q_{r}+Q_{r}^{2}).

This proves the first and second items of the RI mapping hypotheses.

To complete the proof of the claim, it remains to show that the third item of the RI mapping hypotheses is satisfied. Let
0<\delta_{R}\leqslant\delta_{r}
be given by the RI mapping hypotheses for
(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{F},\Psi)
. For
(u_{0},v_{0},w_{0})\in B_{H^{r}}(0,\delta_{R}/Q_{R})\cap H^{N}
we then define the bounded linear operator
\Lambda(u_{0},v_{0},w_{0}):G^{s}\to H^{s}
, for
r\leqslant s\leqslant R
, via

(2.4.8)

\Lambda(u_{0},v_{0},w_{0})\xi=(\lambda_{\mathbf{E}}L(\rho_{\mathbf{E}}u_{0})\rho_{\mathbf{F}}\rho_{\mathbf{E}}\xi,\lambda_{\mathbf{E}}\rho_{\mathbf{E}}\xi,\xi),

which is well-defined since
\rho_{\mathbf{E}}u_{0}\in B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{R})\cap E^{N}
whenever
(u_{0},v_{0},w_{0})\in B_{H^{r}}(0,\delta_{R}/Q_{R})\cap H^{N}
. We now use (
2.4.4
) to verify that
\Lambda(u_{0},v_{0},w_{0})
is a right inverse of
D\Phi(u_{0},v_{0},w_{0})
, using the identities
\rho_{\mathbf{F}}\lambda_{\mathbf{F}}=1
and
\rho_{\mathbf{E}}\lambda_{\mathbf{E}}=1
:

(2.4.9)

D\Phi(u_{0},v_{0},w_{0})L(u_{0},v_{0},w_{0})\xi=\lambda_{\mathbf{E}}(\lambda_{\mathbf{F}}D\Psi(\rho_{\mathbf{E}}u_{0})\rho_{\mathbf{E}}\lambda_{\mathbf{E}}L(\rho_{\mathbf{E}}u_{0})\rho_{\mathbf{F}}\rho_{\mathbf{E}}\xi+(I-\lambda_{\mathbf{F}}\rho_{\mathbf{F}})\rho_{\mathbf{E}}\lambda_{\mathbf{E}}\rho_{\mathbf{E}}\xi)\\
+(I-\lambda_{\mathbf{E}}\rho_{\mathbf{E}})\xi=\lambda_{\mathbf{E}}(\lambda_{\mathbf{F}}\rho_{\mathbf{F}}\rho_{\mathbf{E}}\xi+(I-\lambda_{\mathbf{F}}\rho_{\mathbf{F}})\rho_{\mathbf{E}}\xi)+(I-\lambda_{\mathbf{E}}\rho_{\mathbf{E}})\xi=\lambda_{\mathbf{E}}\rho_{\mathbf{E}}\xi+(I-\lambda_{\mathbf{E}}\rho_{\mathbf{E}})\xi=\xi

for all
\xi\in G^{r}
. Moreover, for
\xi\in G^{s}
, the tame estimate for
L(u_{0})
allows us to bound

(2.4.10)

\left\lVert\Lambda(u_{0},v_{0},w_{0})\xi\right\rVert_{H^{s}}=\left\lVert\lambda_{\mathbf{E}}L(\rho_{\mathbf{E}}u_{0})\rho_{\mathbf{F}}\rho_{\mathbf{E}}\xi\right\rVert_{G^{s}}+\left\lVert\lambda_{\mathbf{E}}\rho_{\mathbf{E}}\xi\right\rVert_{G^{s}}+\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert_{G^{s}}\\
\leqslant C_{2}(s)\left\lVert\lambda_{\mathbf{E}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(E^{s};G^{s})}(\left\lVert\rho_{\mathbf{F}}\rho_{\mathbf{E}}\xi\right\rVert_{F^{s}}+\left\langle\left\lVert\rho_{\mathbf{E}}u_{0}\right\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}\right\rangle\left\lVert\rho_{\mathbf{F}}\rho_{\mathbf{E}}\xi\right\rVert_{F^{r}})\\
+\langle\left\lVert\lambda_{\mathbf{E}}\rho_{\mathbf{E}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(G^{s})}\rangle\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert_{G^{s}}\leqslant C_{2}^{\prime}(s)\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert_{G^{s}}+\left\langle\left\lVert(u_{0},v_{0},w_{0})\right\rVert_{H^{s+\mu}}\right\rangle\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert_{F^{r}},

where the constant
C_{2}^{\prime}(s)
depends on
C_{2}(s)
as well as on the quantities
\left\lVert\lambda_{\mathbf{E}}\rho_{\mathbf{E}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(G^{s})}
,
\left\lVert\lambda_{\mathbf{E}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(E^{s};G^{s})}
,
\left\lVert\rho_{\mathbf{F}}\rho_{\mathbf{E}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(G^{s};F^{s})}
, and
\left\lVert\rho_{\mathbf{E}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(G^{s+\mu};E^{s+\mu})}
. Thus, the third item of the RI hypotheses is satisfied by the triple
(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G},\Phi)
with parameters
(\mu,r,R)
, and the claim is proved. We emphasize, though, that we are not asserting that the LRI hypotheses are satisfied, as the left inverse condition fails in general for
D\Phi
and
\Lambda
.

With the claim in hand, we now consider
\beta=2(r+\mu)+1\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
and invoke Theorem
2.25
for the triple
(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G},\Phi)
. Let
K_{1}
,
K_{2}
,
K_{3}
, and
K_{4}
be the constants from Theorem
2.25
and let
\varepsilon^{\prime}_{\operatorname*{surj}}>0
denote the constant appearing on the right side of (
2.3.1
). Then Theorem
2.25
guarantees that for every
\xi\in B_{G^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon^{\prime}_{\operatorname*{surj}})
there exists
(u^{\prime},v^{\prime},w^{\prime})\in H^{\beta}=(G^{\beta})^{3}
satisfying
\Phi(u^{\prime},v^{\prime},w^{\prime})=\xi
as well as the bound

(2.4.11)

\lVert u^{\prime}\rVert_{G^{\beta}}+\lVert v^{\prime}\rVert_{G^{\beta}}+\lVert w^{\prime}\rVert_{G^{\beta}}=\lVert(u^{\prime},v^{\prime},w^{\prime})\rVert_{H^{\beta}}\leqslant K_{4}\lVert\xi\rVert_{G^{\beta}}.

Set
\varepsilon_{\operatorname*{surj}}=\varepsilon^{\prime}_{\operatorname*{surj}}/\lVert\lambda_{\mathbf{E}}\lambda_{\mathbf{F}}\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(F^{\beta};G^{\beta})}
and note that

(2.4.12)

\lambda_{\mathbf{E}}\lambda_{\mathbf{F}}(B_{F^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon_{\operatorname*{surj}}))\subseteq B_{G^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon^{\prime}_{\operatorname*{surj}})

by construction. Consequently, for any
g\in B_{F^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon_{\operatorname*{surj}})
there exists
(u^{\prime},v^{\prime},w^{\prime})\in H^{\beta}
such that
\Phi(u^{\prime},v^{\prime},w^{\prime})=\lambda_{\mathbf{E}}\lambda_{\mathbf{F}}g
, which unravels to

(2.4.13)

\lambda_{\mathbf{E}}(\lambda_{\mathbf{F}}\Psi(\rho_{\mathbf{E}}u^{\prime})+(I-\lambda_{\mathbf{F}}\rho_{\mathbf{F}})\rho_{\mathbf{E}}v^{\prime})+(I-\lambda_{\mathbf{E}}\rho_{\mathbf{E}})w^{\prime}=\lambda_{\mathbf{E}}\lambda_{\mathbf{F}}g.

Applying
\rho_{\mathbf{E}}
and using the identity
\rho_{\mathbf{E}}\lambda_{\mathbf{E}}=1
, this implies the identity

(2.4.14)

\lambda_{\mathbf{F}}\Psi(\rho_{\mathbf{E}}u^{\prime})+(I-\lambda_{\mathbf{F}}\rho_{\mathbf{F}})\rho_{\mathbf{E}}v^{\prime}=\lambda_{\mathbf{F}}g,

to which we apply
\rho_{\mathbf{F}}
and using the identity
\rho_{\mathbf{F}}\lambda_{\mathbf{F}}=1
to see that

(2.4.15)

\Psi(\rho_{\mathbf{E}}u^{\prime})=g.

Thus, if we set
u=\rho_{\mathbf{E}}u^{\prime}\in E^{\beta}
, then
\Psi(u)=g
, and (
2.4.11
) implies that

(2.4.16)

\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{E^{\beta}}\leqslant\kappa_{1}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{F^{\beta}}

for
\kappa_{1}=K_{4}\left\lVert\rho_{\mathbf{E}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(G^{\beta};E^{\beta})}\left\lVert\lambda_{\mathbf{E}}\lambda_{\mathbf{F}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(F^{\beta};G^{\beta})}
, which in particular means that
u\in B_{E^{\beta}}(0,\kappa_{1}\varepsilon_{\operatorname*{surj}})
.

On the other hand, Lemma
2.18
implies that
\mathbf{E}
is terminally dense. This and the LRI mapping hypotheses imply that the hypotheses of Theorem
2.26
are satisfied by the triple
(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{F},\Psi)
with
\sigma=\beta-\mu
and
\gamma_{\sigma}=\delta_{R}\leqslant\delta_{r}.
Let
\delta_{\operatorname*{inj},\sigma}>0
be the constant from Theorem
2.26
. Then the theorem tells us that
\Psi:B_{E^{\beta}}(0,\delta_{\operatorname*{inj},\sigma})\to F^{\beta-\mu}
is injective and obeys the estimate (
2.3.72
).

Set
\varepsilon=\min\{{\varepsilon_{\operatorname*{surj}},\delta_{\operatorname*{inj},\sigma}/\kappa_{1}}\}
and
\kappa_{2}>0
to be the constant on the right side of (
2.3.72
). Since
\kappa_{1}\varepsilon\leqslant\delta_{\operatorname*{inj},\sigma}
, the above analysis shows that for every
g\in B_{F^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon)
there exists a unique
u\in B_{E^{\beta}}(0,\kappa_{1}\varepsilon)
such that
\Psi(u)=g
. Moreover, the induced map
\Psi^{-1}:B_{F^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon)\to\Psi^{-1}(B_{F^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon))\cap B_{E^{\beta}}(0,\kappa_{1}\varepsilon)
satisfies (
2.2.4
) and (
2.2.7
) in light of (
2.4.16
) and (
2.3.72
).

Finally, if we assume that
\mathbb{N}\ni\nu\leqslant R+r-2\beta
and
g\in B_{F^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon)\cap F^{\beta+\nu}
, then we are assured by the fourth item of Theorem
2.25
that there exists
(u^{\prime},v^{\prime},w^{\prime})\in H^{\beta+\nu}
such that
\Phi(u^{\prime},v^{\prime},w^{\prime})=\lambda_{\mathbf{E}}\lambda_{\mathbf{F}}g
. By unraveling as in (
2.4.13
)–(
2.4.15
), we find that for
u=\rho_{\mathbf{E}}u^{\prime}\in E^{\beta+\nu}
we have that
\Psi(u)=g
and
\lVert u\rVert_{E^{\beta+\nu}}\lesssim\lVert g\rVert_{F^{\beta+\nu}}
.
∎

2.5.
Refinements

In this subsection we aim to strengthen the conclusions of Theorem
2.21
. In particular, we will study the continuity and higher order smoothness of the inverse map
\Psi^{-1}
provided by the theorem. First, we analyze the right and left linear inverse map
L
by making an extension to backgrounds outside of the terminal space
E^{N}
, and then proving various continuity and differentiability assertions. Second, we return to the map
\Psi^{-1}
and show a more refined continuity estimate than the basic assertion of (
2.2.7
). Then we prove differentiability of
\Psi^{-1}
and relate the derivative to the operator
L
. Once this is done, we conclude by reading off higher regularity assertions.

We now enumerate further properties of the family of inverses
L
.

Theorem 2.27

(Extension and regularity of the right and left inverse)
.

Under the LRI mapping hypotheses set forth in Definition
2.20
and the additional assumptions that
r+\mu\leqslant R
and that the Banach scale
\{{E^{s}}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
consists of reflexive spaces, we have that the following properties of
L
hold.

(1)

Existence of extension: There exists a family of bounded linear maps
\overline{L}:B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{R})\cap E^{r+\mu}\to\mathcal{L}(F^{r};E^{r})
such that the following extension properties are satisfied.

(a)

\overline{L}=L
on the subset
B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{R})\cap E^{N}
.

(b)

If
g\in F^{r}
and
u_{0}\in B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{R})\cap E^{r+\mu}
, then we have that
D\Psi(u_{0})\overline{L}(u_{0})g=g
. Additionally, if
u\in E^{r+\mu}
then
\overline{L}(u_{0})D\Psi(u_{0})u=u
.

(c)

For
s\in[r,R]\cap\mathbb{N}
we have that
\overline{L}:B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{R})\cap E^{s+\mu}\to\mathcal{L}(F^{s};E^{s})
with the tame estimate
\lVert\overline{L}(u_{0})g\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim\lVert g\rVert_{F^{s}}+\langle\lVert u_{0}\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}\rangle\lVert g\rVert_{F^{r}}
.

(2)

Continuity: For
s\in[r,R-\mu]\cap\mathbb{N}
, if we view
\overline{L}
as mapping
\overline{L}:(B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{R})\cap E^{s+\mu})\times F^{s}\to E^{s}
, then this map is continuous.

(3)

Higher regularity: Assume that, for some
\mathbb{N}\ni\ell\geqslant 2
, the map
\Psi
is
\ell
-times continuously Gateaux differentiable in the sense of Definition
2.23
. If for
s\in[r,R-\ell\mu]\cap\mathbb{N}
we view
\overline{L}
as a mapping

(2.5.1)

\overline{L}:(B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{R})\cap E^{s+\ell\mu})\times F^{s+(\ell-1)\mu}\to E^{s},

then
\overline{L}
is
(\ell-1)
-times continuously Gateaux-differentiable.

Proof.

We divide the proof into several steps.

Step 1
: Constructing
\overline{L}
. First, we provide a continuity estimate on the right and left inverse map
L
(a priori only defined for
E^{N}
-backgrounds). Suppose that
u_{0},w_{0}\in B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{R})\cap E^{N}
,
s\in[r,R-\mu]\cap\mathbb{N}
, and
g\in F^{s+\mu}
. We will estimate the difference

(2.5.2)

L(u_{0})g-L(w_{0})g=-L(u_{0})(D\Psi(u_{0})-D\Psi(w_{0}))L(w_{0})g\\
=-\int_{0}^{1}L(u_{0})D^{2}\Psi((1-t)w_{0}+tu_{0})(u_{0}-w_{0},L(w_{0})g)\;\mathrm{d}t

in
E^{s}
. By applying the tame estimates of (
2.2.1
) and (
2.2.2
) repeatedly and the embedding inequalities of the first item of Definition
2.2
, we get the somewhat crude estimate

(2.5.3)

\lVert L(u_{0})g-L(w_{0})g\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim\langle\lVert u_{0},w_{0}\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}\rangle^{3}\lVert u_{0}-w_{0}\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}\lVert g\rVert_{F^{r}}\\
+\langle\lVert u_{0},w_{0}\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}\rangle\lVert u_{0}-w_{0}\rVert_{E^{r}}(\lVert g\rVert_{F^{s+\mu}}+\langle\lVert w_{0}\rVert_{E^{s+2\mu}}\rangle\lVert g\rVert_{F^{r}}).

While forgoing tameness, this estimate is strong enough to allow us to define our extension.

Indeed, let
u_{0}\in B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{R})\cap E^{r+\mu}
and fix some
g\in F^{r}
. We claim that the sequence
\{{L(T_{j}u_{0})T_{j}g}\}_{j=\ell}^{\infty}\subset E^{r}
is Cauchy, where the operators
\{{T_{j}}\}_{j=0}^{\infty}
are from Lemma
2.18
and
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
is the first index for which
T_{j}u_{0}\in B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{R})
for all
\mathbb{N}\ni j\geqslant\ell
. We verify this by first estimating

(2.5.4)

\lVert L(T_{j+k}u_{0})T_{j+k}g-L(T_{j}u_{0})T_{j}g\rVert_{E^{r}}\leqslant\lVert L(T_{j+k}u_{0})(T_{j+k}g-T_{j}g)\rVert_{F^{r}}\\
+\lVert(L(T_{j+k}u_{0})-L(T_{j}u_{0}))T_{j}g\rVert_{E^{r}}=\mathbf{I}_{j,k}+\mathbf{II}_{j,k}.

For
\mathbf{I}_{j,k}
we simply apply estimate (
2.2.2
) and Lemma
2.18
:

(2.5.5)

\mathbf{I}_{j,k}\lesssim\langle\lVert T_{j+k}u_{0}\rVert_{E^{r+\mu}}\rangle\lVert T_{j+k}g-T_{j}g\rVert_{F^{r}}\lesssim\langle\lVert u_{0}\rVert_{E^{r+\mu}}\rangle(\mathbf{n}^{r}_{j}(g)+\mathbf{n}^{r}_{j+k}(g)).

Hence
\lim_{j,k\to\infty}\mathbf{I}_{j,k}=0
. On the other hand, for
\mathbf{II}_{j,k}
, we first employ estimate (
2.5.3
):

(2.5.6)

\mathbf{II}_{j,k}\lesssim\langle\lVert T_{j+k}u_{0},T_{j}u_{0}\rVert_{E^{r+\mu}}\rangle^{3}\lVert T_{j+k}u_{0}-T_{j}u_{0}\rVert_{E^{r+\mu}}\lVert T_{j}g\rVert_{F^{r}}\\
+\langle\lVert T_{j+k}u_{0},T_{j}u_{0}\rVert_{E^{r+\mu}}\rangle\lVert T_{j+k}u_{0}-T_{j}u_{0}\rVert_{E^{r}}(\lVert T_{j}g\rVert_{F^{r+\mu}}+\langle\lVert T_{j}u_{0}\rVert_{E^{r+2\mu}}\rangle\lVert T_{j}g\rVert_{F^{r}}).

Thanks again to Lemma
2.18
, we are free to make the following bounds:

\displaystyle\lVert T_{j+k}u_{0}\rVert_{E^{r+\mu}},\lVert T_{j}u_{0}\rVert_{E^{r+\mu}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lVert u_{0}\rVert_{F^{r+\mu}},

\displaystyle\lVert T_{j}g\rVert_{F^{r}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lVert g\rVert_{F^{r}},

\displaystyle\lVert T_{j+k}u_{0}-T_{j}u_{0}\rVert_{E^{r}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{-j\mu}(\mathbf{n}^{r+\mu}_{j}(u_{0})+\mathbf{n}^{r+\mu}_{j+k}(u_{0})),

\displaystyle\lVert T_{j}g\rVert_{F^{r+\mu}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{j\mu}\lVert g\rVert_{F^{r}},

(2.5.7)

\displaystyle\lVert T_{j+k}u_{0}-T_{j}u_{0}\rVert_{E^{r+\mu}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbf{n}^{r+\mu}_{j}(u_{0})+\mathbf{n}^{r+\mu}_{j+k}(u_{0})

\displaystyle\lVert T_{j}u_{0}\rVert_{E^{r+2\mu}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{j\mu}\lVert u_{0}\rVert_{E^{r+\mu}}.

Upon combining (
2.5.6
) and (
2.5
), we acquire the estimate

(2.5.8)

\mathbf{II}_{j,k}\lesssim\langle\lVert u_{0}\rVert_{E^{r+\mu}}\rangle^{3}\lVert g\rVert_{F^{r}}(\mathbf{n}^{r+\mu}_{j}(u_{0})+\mathbf{n}^{r+\mu}_{j+k}(u_{0})),

and hence
\lim_{j,k\to\infty}\mathbf{II}_{j,k}=0
. We deduce that the sequence
\{{L(T_{j}u_{0})T_{j}g}\}_{j=\ell}^{\infty}\subset E^{r}
is Cauchy. Hence there exists

(2.5.9)

\overline{L}(u_{0})g=\lim_{j\to\infty}L(T_{j}u_{0})T_{j}g,

and this defines
\overline{L}
as a family of linear maps.

Step 2
: Properties of
\overline{L}
. We now examine the restriction of
\overline{L}
to higher regularity (larger
s
) spaces in the scale. Suppose that
u_{0}\in B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{R})\cap E^{s+\mu}
and that
g\in F^{s}
for some
s\in[r,R]\cap\mathbb{N}
. For
\mathbb{N}\ni j
sufficiently large we can apply estimate (
2.2.2
) and again Lemma
2.18
to see that

(2.5.10)

\lVert L(T_{j}u_{0})T_{j}g\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim\lVert T_{j}g\rVert_{F^{s}}+\langle\lVert T_{j}u_{0}\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}\rangle\lVert T_{j}g\rVert_{F^{r}}\lesssim\lVert g\rVert_{F^{s}}+\langle\lVert u_{0}\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}\rangle\lVert g\rVert_{F^{r}}.

Hence, the sequence
\{{L(T_{j}u_{0})T_{j}g}\}_{j=\ell}^{\infty}\subset E^{s}
is bounded by the right hand expression above. The space
E^{s}
is reflexive, and this sequence already converges in
E^{r}
, so the limit
\overline{L}(u_{0})g
belongs to
E^{s}
and has norm bounded above by the right side of (
2.5.10
) thanks to the weak sequential lower semicontinuity of the norm.

We next prove that
\overline{L}
is a family of right and left inverses for
D\Psi
. First suppose that
g\in F^{r}
and
u_{0}\in B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{R})\cap E^{r+\mu}
. For
j\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large, we have that

(2.5.11)

D\Psi(T_{j}u_{0})L(T_{j}u_{0})T_{j}g=T_{j}g.

Since
\Psi:B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{r})\cap E^{r}\to F^{r-\mu}
is
C^{2}
,
\{{L(T_{j}u_{0})T_{j}g}\}_{j=0}^{\infty}\subset E^{r}
converges to
\overline{L}(u_{0})g
, and we have the convergences
T_{j}u_{0}\to u_{0}
in
E^{r+\mu}\hookrightarrow E^{r}
and
T_{j}g\to g
in
F^{r}
as
j\to\infty
, we may send
j\to\infty
in (
2.5.11
) to see that
D\Psi(u_{0})\overline{L}(u_{0})g=g
.

On the other hand, if we assume that
u\in E^{r+\mu}
and
u_{0}\in B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{R})\cap E^{r+\mu}
, then we have

(2.5.12)

L(T_{j}u_{0})T_{j}D\Psi(u_{0})u=u+L(T_{j}u_{0})(T_{j}D\Psi(u_{0})-D\Psi(T_{j}u_{0}))u.

The left hand side converges in
E^{r}
to
\overline{L}(u_{0})D\Psi(u_{0})u
as
j\to\infty
. For the right hand side we may estimate

(2.5.13)

\lVert L(T_{j}u_{0})(T_{j}D\Psi(u_{0})-D\Psi(T_{j}u_{0}))u\rVert_{E^{r}}\lesssim\langle\lVert u_{0}\rVert_{E^{r+\mu}}\rangle\lVert(T_{j}D\Psi(u_{0})-D\Psi(T_{j}u_{0}))u\rVert_{F^{r}}\\
\lesssim\langle\lVert u_{0}\rVert_{E^{r+\mu}}\rangle(\lVert(I-T_{j})D\Psi(u_{0})u\rVert_{F^{r}}+\lVert(D\Psi(u_{0})-D\Psi(T_{j}u_{0}))u\rVert_{F^{r}}),

and the right hand side of this evidently converges to zero as
j\to\infty
thanks again to the properties of
I-T_{j}
from Lemma
2.18
, the continuity properties of
D\Psi
, and the fact that
u,u_{0}\in E^{r+\mu}
. Hence by sending
j\to\infty
in (
2.5.12
) we learn that
\overline{L}(u_{0})D\Psi(u_{0})u=u
.

Now we are ready to prove that
\overline{L}
is actually an extension of
L
. Suppose that
u_{0}\in B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{R})\cap E^{N}
and that
g\in F^{r}
. Then by the right and left inverse properties, for every
j\in\mathbb{N}
we have the identity

(2.5.14)

\overline{L}(u_{0})T_{j}g=\overline{L}(u_{0})D\Psi(u_{0})L(u_{0})T_{j}g=L(u_{0})T_{j}g.

Upon sending
j\to\infty
and using that
\overline{L}(u_{0})
and
L(u_{0})
are bounded, we deduce that
\overline{L}(u_{0})g=L(u_{0})g
. This completes the proof of all of the assertions of the first item.

Step 3
: Continuity. We now study continuity. We have established that for
s\in[r,R-\mu]\cap\mathbb{N}
,
u_{0},w_{0}\in B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{R})\cap E^{s+2\mu}
, and
g\in F^{s+\mu}
the sequence
\{{L(T_{j}u_{0})T_{j}g-L(T_{j}w_{0})T_{j}g}\}_{j=0}^{\infty}\subset E^{s+\mu}
converges weakly up to a subsequence in
E^{s+\mu}
and strongly in
E^{r}
to the limit
\overline{L}(u_{0})g-\overline{L}(w_{0})g
. Therefore, upon invoking the log-convexity of Lemma
2.19
, we obtain strong convergence in
E^{s}
up to a subsequence. By passing along this subsequence in (
2.5.3
) and then taking the supremum over
\lVert g\rVert_{F^{s+\mu}}\leqslant 1
, we obtain the Lipschitz estimate

(2.5.15)

\lVert\overline{L}(u_{0})-\overline{L}(w_{0})\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(F^{s+\mu},E^{s})}\lesssim\langle\lVert u_{0},w_{0}\rVert_{E^{s+2\mu}}\rangle^{3}\lVert u_{0}-w_{0}\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}.

We use (
2.5.15
) as an intermediate step in deriving the stated continuity in the second item of the Theorem statement. Let
u_{0},w_{0}\in B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{R})\cap E^{s+\mu}
and
g,h\in F^{s}
. For
j\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large, we consider the difference

(2.5.16)

\overline{L}(u_{0})g-\overline{L}(w_{0})h=(\overline{L}(u_{0})g-\overline{L}(T_{j}u_{0})T_{j}g)\\
+(\overline{L}(T_{j}u_{0})T_{j}g-\overline{L}(T_{j}w_{0})T_{j}h)+(\overline{L}(T_{j}w_{0})T_{j}h-\overline{L}(w_{0})h)=\mathbf{I}_{j}+\mathbf{II}_{j}+\mathbf{III}_{j}

and estimate the three terms individually. For
\mathbf{I}_{j}
, we note that (
2.5.4
), (
2.5.5
), (
2.5.6
), and (
2.5
) hold with
r
replaced by
s
(by the same proof), and hence we may send
k\to\infty
to acquire the bound

(2.5.17)

\lVert\mathbf{I}_{j}\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim\langle\lVert u_{0}\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}\rangle^{3}\big(\lVert g\rVert_{F^{s}}\mathbf{n}_{j}^{s+\mu}(u_{0})+\mathbf{n}^{s}_{j}(g)\big).

For
\mathbf{II}_{j}
we simply apply the local Lipschitz estimate (
2.5.15
) and use properties of the operators
T_{j}
:

(2.5.18)

\lVert\mathbf{II}_{j}\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim\langle 2^{j\mu}\lVert g\rVert_{F^{s}}\rangle\langle 2^{j\mu}\lVert u_{0},w_{0}\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}\rangle^{3}\lVert u_{0}-w_{0},g-h\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}\times F^{s}}.

For
\mathbf{III}_{j}
, we begin by bounding as in (
2.5.17
):

(2.5.19)

\lVert\mathbf{III}_{j}\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim\langle\lVert w_{0}\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}\rangle^{3}\big(\lVert h\rVert_{F^{s}}\mathbf{n}_{j}^{s+\mu}(w_{0})+\mathbf{n}^{s}_{j}(h)\big).

Now, by Lemma
2.18
, we may estimate

(2.5.20)

\mathbf{n}_{j}^{s+\mu}(w_{0})\lesssim\mathbf{n}_{j}^{s+\mu}(u_{0})+\lVert u_{0}-w_{0}\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}\text{ and }\mathbf{n}_{j}^{s}(h)\lesssim\mathbf{n}_{j}^{s}(g)+\left\lVert g-h\right\rVert_{F^{s}},

and hence deduce that

(2.5.21)

\lVert\overline{L}(u_{0})g-\overline{L}(w_{0})h\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim 2^{3\mu j}\langle\lVert u_{0},w_{0}\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}},\lVert g,h\rVert_{F^{s}}\rangle^{3}\lVert u_{0}-w_{0},g-h\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}\times F^{s}}\\
+\langle\lVert u_{0},w_{0}\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}},\lVert g,h\rVert_{F^{s}}\rangle^{3}(\mathbf{n}_{j}^{s+\mu}(u_{0})+\mathbf{n}^{s}_{j}(g)).

By taking
j
large relative to
u_{0}
and
g
and then taking
w_{0}
and
h
sufficiently close
u_{0}
and
g
to we see that
\overline{L}
is continuous as a map from
E^{s+\mu}\times F^{s}
to
E^{s}
, but not necessarily uniformly so. This completes the proof of the second item.

Step 4
: Higher regularity. Finally, we prove the third assertion. By arguing as in the derivation of (
2.5.10
), we learn that for
s\in[r,R-2\mu]\cap\mathbb{N}
,
w_{0},h_{0},w_{0}+h_{0}\in B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{R})\cap E^{s+2\mu}
,
g\in F^{s+\mu}
, and
\tau\in(0,1)
we have the decomposition

(2.5.22)

\tau^{-1}(\overline{L}(w_{0}+\tau h_{0})g-\overline{L}(w_{0})g)+\overline{L}(w_{0})D^{2}\Psi(w_{0})(h_{0},\overline{L}(w_{0})g)=\mathbf{I}_{\tau}+\mathbf{II}_{\tau}

where

(2.5.23)

\mathbf{I}_{\tau}=-(\overline{L}(w_{0}+\tau h_{0})-\overline{L}(w_{0}))\int_{0}^{1}D^{2}\Psi(w_{0}+\tau h_{0})(h_{0},\overline{L}(w_{0})g)\;\mathrm{d}t

and

(2.5.24)

\mathbf{II}_{\tau}=-\overline{L}(w_{0})\int_{0}^{1}(D^{2}\Psi(w_{0}+t\tau h_{0})-D^{2}\Psi(w_{0}))(h_{0},\overline{L}(w_{0})g)\;\mathrm{d}t.

Since
\Psi
is
\mu
-tamely
C^{2}
,
\overline{L}
satisfies the continuity assertions of the second item, and
\overline{L}
obeys the same tame estimates as
L
, it holds that
\lVert\mathbf{I}_{\tau},\mathbf{II}_{\tau}\rVert_{E^{s}}\to 0
as
\tau\to 0
. This proves that the map

(2.5.25)

\overline{L}:(B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{R})\cap E^{s+2\mu})\times F^{s+\mu}\to E^{s}

is Gateaux differentiable with derivative given by

(2.5.26)

D\overline{L}(u_{0},g_{0})[w,h]=-\overline{L}(u_{0})D^{2}\Psi(u_{0})(w,L(u_{0})g_{0})+\overline{L}(u_{0})h

for
u_{0}\in B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{R})\cap E^{s+2\mu}
,
w\in E^{s+2\mu}
, and
g_{0},h\in F^{s+\mu}
. Now suppose that
\mathbb{N}\ni\ell\geqslant 3
and that
\Psi
is
\ell
-times continuously Gateaux differentiable. By a simple induction argument using the formula (
2.5.26
), we find the remaining conclusions of the third item.
∎

Now that we have a refined understanding of the mapping properties of the family of right and left inverses
\overline{L}
, we return to studying the local inverse map
\Psi^{-1}
, which we recall is granted by the conclusions of Theorem
2.21
. Indeed, we now prove Theorem
2.24
.

Proof of Theorem
2.24
.

Throughout the proof we will use the operator
\overline{L}
from Theorem
2.27
, which is an extension of the operator
L
from the LRI mapping hypotheses. By a very mild abuse of notation, we write
L
in place of
\overline{L}
in what follows.

Under either hypothesis
I
or
II
, the Banach scales
\mathbf{E}
and
\mathbf{F}
are tame, and so there exist smoothing operators
\{{T_{j}}\}_{j=0}^{\infty}
as in Lemma
2.18
. We aim to establish that if
s\in[\beta,R+r-\beta-\mu)\cap\mathbb{N}
and
g\in B_{F^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon)\cap F^{s}
, then
\Psi^{-1}(T_{j}g)\to\Psi^{-1}(g)
in the space
E^{s}
as
j\to\infty
. By Taylor expanding
\Psi
at
T_{j}g
to second order as in the proof of Lemma
2.26
and using the left invertibility of
D\Psi(T_{j}g)
by
L\circ\Psi^{-1}(T_{j}g)
, we arrive at the identity

(2.5.27)

\Psi^{-1}(T_{j+1}g)-\Psi^{-1}(T_{j}g)=L\circ\Psi^{-1}(T_{j}g)(T_{j+1}-T_{j})g\\
-L\circ\Psi^{-1}(T_{j}g)\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)D^{2}\Psi((1-t)\Psi^{-1}(T_{j}g)+t\Psi^{-1}(T_{j+1}g))(\Psi^{-1}(T_{j+1}g)-\Psi^{-1}(T_{j}g))^{\otimes 2}\;\mathrm{d}t\\
=\mathbf{I}_{j}+\mathbf{II}_{j}.

We will estimate the right hand side of the above in the spaces
E^{s+\sigma}
for
\sigma\in\{{-1,0,1}\}
. From the assumed tame structure, we have the estimate

(2.5.28)

\lVert L\circ\Psi^{-1}(T_{j}g)h\rVert_{E^{s+\sigma}}\lesssim\langle\lVert\Psi^{-1}(T_{j}g)\rVert_{E^{s+\mu+\sigma}}\rangle\lVert h\rVert_{F^{r}}+\lVert h\rVert_{F^{s+\sigma}}\lesssim\langle 2^{j(\mu+\sigma)}\lVert g\rVert_{F^{s}}\rangle\lVert h\rVert_{F^{r}}+\lVert h\rVert_{F^{s+\sigma}}.

For
h=(T_{j+1}-T_{j})g
we estimate

(2.5.29)

\begin{cases}\lVert(T_{j+1}-T_{j})g\rVert_{F^{r}}\lesssim 2^{j(r-s)}\mathbf{m}_{j}^{s}(g),\\
\lVert(T_{j+1}-T_{j})g\rVert_{F^{s+\sigma}}\lesssim 2^{j\sigma}\mathbf{m}_{j}^{s}(g),\end{cases}

and hence (since
\mu+r\leqslant s
)
\lVert\mathbf{I}_{j}\rVert_{E^{s+\sigma}}\lesssim 2^{j\sigma}\langle\lVert g\rVert_{F^{s}}\rangle\mathbf{m}_{j}^{s}(g)
, where we recall that the seminorms
\mathbf{m}_{j}^{s}
are from Lemma
2.18
. On the other hand, for some
t\in[0,1]
, we take

(2.5.30)

h_{t}=D^{2}\Psi((1-t)\Psi^{-1}(T_{j}g)+t\Psi^{-1}(T_{j+1}g))(\Psi^{-1}(T_{j+1}g)-\Psi^{-1}(T_{j}g))^{\otimes 2}

and estimate for
\ell\in\{{r,s+\sigma}\}

(2.5.31)

\lVert h_{t}\rVert_{E^{\ell}}\lesssim\langle\lVert T_{j}g,T_{j+1}g\rVert_{F^{\ell+\mu}}\rangle\lVert\Psi^{-1}(T_{j+1}g)-\Psi^{-1}(T_{j}g)\rVert_{E^{r}}^{2}\\
+\lVert\Psi^{-1}(T_{j+1}g)-\Psi^{-1}(T_{j}g)\rVert_{E^{r}}\lVert\Psi^{-1}(T_{j+1}g)-\Psi^{-1}(T_{j}g)\rVert_{E^{\ell+\mu}}.

If
j
is sufficiently large, say
j\geqslant J(g)
, then we have that
T_{j}g,T_{j+1}g\in B_{F^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon)
, and hence we can apply the estimate from the third conclusion of Theorem
2.21
, to bound

(2.5.32)

\begin{cases}\lVert\Psi^{-1}(T_{j+1}g)-\Psi^{-1}(T_{j}g)\rVert_{E^{r}}\\
\lVert\Psi^{-1}(T_{j+1}g)-\Psi^{-1}(T_{j}g)\rVert_{E^{r+\mu}}\end{cases}\lesssim\lVert(T_{j+1}-T_{j})g\rVert_{E^{\beta-\mu}}\lesssim 2^{j(\beta-\mu-s)}\mathbf{m}_{j}^{s}(g).

On the other hand, we trivially bound

(2.5.33)

\lVert\Psi^{-1}(T_{j+1}g)-\Psi^{-1}(T_{j}g)\rVert_{E^{s+\sigma+\mu}}\lesssim\lVert T_{j+1}g,T_{j}g\rVert_{F^{s+\sigma+\mu}}\lesssim 2^{j(\mu+\sigma)}\lVert g\rVert_{F^{s}}.

Therefore, we get that

(2.5.34)

\lVert h_{t}\rVert_{E^{s+\sigma}}\lesssim 2^{2j(\beta-\mu-s)+j(\mu+\sigma)}\langle\lVert g\rVert_{F^{s}}\rangle\mathbf{m}_{j}^{s}(g)^{2}+2^{j(\beta+\sigma-s)}\lVert g\rVert_{F^{s}}\mathbf{m}_{j}^{s}(g)\lesssim 2^{j\sigma}\langle\lVert g\rVert_{F^{s}}\rangle^{2}\mathbf{m}_{j}^{s}(g)

and
\langle 2^{j(\mu+\sigma)}\lVert g\rVert_{F^{s}}\rangle\lVert h_{t}\rVert_{E^{r}}\lesssim 2^{j\sigma}\langle\lVert g\rVert_{F^{s}}\rangle^{3}\mathbf{m}_{j}^{s}(g)
. Upon synthesizing these bounds we arrive at the estimate
\lVert\mathbf{II}_{j}\rVert_{E^{s+\sigma}}\lesssim 2^{j\sigma}\langle\lVert g\rVert_{F^{s}}\rangle^{3}\mathbf{m}_{j}^{s}(g)
, and hence (for
\sigma\in\{{-1,0,1}\}
and
\mathbb{N}\ni j\geqslant J(g)
) we have

(2.5.35)

\lVert\Psi^{-1}(T_{j+1}g)-\Psi^{-1}(T_{j}g)\rVert_{E^{s+\sigma}}\lesssim 2^{j\sigma}\langle\lVert g\rVert_{F^{s}}\rangle^{3}\mathbf{m}_{j}^{s}(g).

We now use (
2.5.35
) to prove that the sequence
\{{\sum_{j=J(g)}^{n}(\Psi^{-1}(T_{j+1}g)-\Psi^{-1}(T_{j}g))}\}_{n=J(g)}^{\infty}
is Cauchy in
E^{s}
. Again, the Banach scales
\mathbf{E}
and
\mathbf{F}
are tame in the sense of Definition
2.14
, and so there exist LP-smoothable Banach scales
\overline{\mathbf{E}}
and
\overline{\mathbf{F}}
with lifting and restriction pairs
\lambda_{\mathbf{E}},\rho_{\mathbf{E}}
and
\lambda_{\mathbf{F}},\rho_{\mathbf{F}}
witnessing the definition of tameness for
\mathbf{E}
and
\mathbf{F}
, respectively. Let
\xi_{j}(g)=\lambda_{\mathbf{E}}(\Psi^{-1}(T_{j+1}g)-\Psi^{-1}(T_{j}g))\in\overline{E}^{R+r-\beta}
and
\eta_{j}(g)=\langle\lVert g\rVert_{F^{s}}\rangle^{3}(S_{j+1}-S_{j})\circ\lambda_{\mathbf{F}}g\in\overline{F}^{R+r-\beta}
. By the continuity of
\lambda_{\mathbf{E}}
, (
2.5.35
), and properties of the LP-smoothing operators from Definition
2.11
, we deduce that for
j,k\in\mathbb{N}
with
j\geqslant J(g)
we have the bound

(2.5.36)

\lVert\Updelta_{k}\xi_{j}(g)\rVert_{\overline{E}^{s}}\lesssim 2^{-|j-k|}\lVert\eta_{j}(g)\rVert_{\overline{F}^{s}}.

Hence, we obtain the following estimates for
\ell,n\in\mathbb{N}
with
\ell\geqslant J(g)
:

(2.5.37)

\Big\lVert\sum_{j=\ell}^{\ell+n}\xi_{j}(g)\Big\rVert_{\overline{E}^{s}}^{2}\lesssim\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Big(\sum_{j=\ell}^{\ell+n}\lVert\Updelta_{k}\xi_{j}(g)\rVert_{\overline{E}^{s}}\Big)^{2}\lesssim\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Big(\sum_{j=\ell}^{\ell+n}2^{-|j-k|}\lVert\eta_{j}(g)\rVert_{\overline{F}^{s}}\Big)^{2}\lesssim\sum_{j=\ell}^{\ell+n}\lVert\eta_{j}(g)\rVert_{\overline{F}^{s}}^{2},

where in the last inequality above we have employed Young’s convolution inequality, as was done in (
2.3.12
). Since
g\in F^{s}
, we have the inclusion
\{{\lVert\eta_{j}(g)\rVert_{\overline{F}^{s}}}\}_{j=0}^{\infty}\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N})
and hence from (
2.5.37
), we deduce that
\{{\sum_{j=J(g)}^{n}\xi_{j}(g)}\}_{n=J(g)}^{\infty}\subset\overline{E}^{s}
is Cauchy. The map
\rho_{\mathbf{E}}
is continuous and linear and thus by taking the image of this sequence we learn that

(2.5.38)

\Big\{{\sum_{j=J(g)}^{n}(\Psi^{-1}(T_{j+1}g)-\Psi^{-1}(T_{j}g))}\Big\}_{n=J(g)}^{\infty}=\{{\Psi^{-1}(T_{n+1}g)-\Psi^{-1}(T_{J(g)}g)}\}_{n=0}^{\infty}\subset E^{s}

is Cauchy, as desired. On the other hand, the third conclusion of Theorem
2.21
yields:

(2.5.39)

\lVert\Psi^{-1}(T_{n+1}g)-\Psi^{-1}(g)\rVert_{E^{\beta-\mu}}\lesssim\lVert(I-T_{n+1})g\rVert_{F^{\beta-\mu}}\to 0\quad\text{as}\quad n\to\infty.

Therefore, the limit in
E^{s}
of the above sequence, which exists by the satisfaction of the Cauchy condition, necessarily is
\Psi^{-1}(g)
. By tracing back through the estimates, we find the following quantitative rate of convergence for
n\geqslant J(g)
:

(2.5.40)

\lVert\Psi^{-1}(T_{n+1}g)-\Psi^{-1}(g)\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim\langle\lVert g\rVert_{F^{s}}\rangle^{3}\Big(\sum_{j=n}^{\infty}\mathbf{m}_{j}^{s}(g)^{2}\Big)^{1/2}.

We now have all the tools we need to establish that the map
\Psi^{-1}:B_{F^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon)\cap F^{s}\to E^{s}
is continuous for every
s\in[\beta,R+r-\beta-\mu)\cap\mathbb{N}
. Let
g,h,g+h\in B_{F^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon)\cap F^{s}
, and assume that
\lVert h\rVert_{F^{s}}
is sufficiently small so that
J(g+h)\leqslant J(g)+1
, where once more we let
J(g+h)
denote the first index for which
j\geqslant J(g+h)
implies that
T_{j}(g+h)\in B_{F^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon)
. For any
\mathbb{N}\ni n\geqslant J(g)+1
, we may then estimate

(2.5.41)

\lVert\Psi^{-1}(g)-\Psi^{-1}(g+h)\rVert_{E^{s}}\leqslant\lVert\Psi^{-1}(T_{n+1}g)-\Psi^{-1}(g)\rVert_{E^{s}}\\
+\lVert\Psi^{-1}(T_{n+1}g)-\Psi^{-1}(T_{n+1}(g+h))\rVert_{E^{s}}+\lVert\Psi^{-1}(T_{n+1}(g+h))-\Psi^{-1}(g+h)\rVert_{E^{s}}\\
=\mathbf{I}_{n}+\mathbf{II}_{n}+\mathbf{III}_{n}.

For
\mathbf{I}_{n}
and
\mathbf{III}_{n}
we employ the quantitative rate of convergence (
2.5.40
) along with the inequality (which is true by Lemma
2.18
)

(2.5.42)

\Big(\sum_{j=n}^{\infty}\mathbf{m}_{j}^{s}(g+h)^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\lesssim\lVert h\rVert_{F^{s}}+\Big(\sum_{j=n}^{\infty}\mathbf{m}_{j}^{s}(g)^{2}\Big)^{1/2}

to see that

(2.5.43)

\mathbf{I}_{n}+\mathbf{III}_{n}\lesssim\langle\lVert g,h\rVert_{F^{s}}\rangle^{3}\Big(\lVert h\rVert_{F^{s}}+\Big(\sum_{j=n}^{\infty}\mathbf{m}_{j}^{s}(g)^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\Big).

It remains to handle
\mathbf{II}_{n}
. As before, we have

(2.5.44)

\Psi^{-1}(T_{n+1}(g+h))-\Psi^{-1}(T_{n+1}g)=L\circ\Psi^{-1}(T_{n+1}g)\Big(T_{n+1}h-\\
\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)D^{2}\Psi((1-t)\Psi^{-1}(T_{n+1}g)+t\Psi^{-1}(T_{n+1}(g+h)))(\Psi^{-1}(T_{n+1}(g+h))-\Psi^{-1}(T_{n+1}g))^{\otimes 2}\;\mathrm{d}t\Big)

as a consequence of Taylor’s theorem. Hence we have the estimate

(2.5.45)

\mathbf{II}_{n}\lesssim\langle 2^{\mu n}\lVert g\rVert_{F^{s}}\rangle\big(\lVert h\rVert_{F^{s}}+\\
\langle 2^{\mu n}\lVert g,h\rVert_{F^{s}}\rangle\lVert\Psi^{-1}(T_{n+1}(g+h))-\Psi^{-1}(T_{n+1}g)\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}\lVert\Psi^{-1}(T_{n+1}(g+h))-\Psi^{-1}(T_{n+1}g)\rVert_{E^{r}}\big).

We then crudely estimate

(2.5.46)

\lVert\Psi^{-1}(T_{n+1}(g+h))-\Psi^{-1}(T_{n+1}g)\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}\lesssim 2^{n\mu}\lVert g,h\rVert_{F^{s}}

and also apply the third conclusion of Theorem
2.21
to see that

(2.5.47)

\lVert\Psi^{-1}(T_{n+1}(g+h))-\Psi^{-1}(T_{n+1}g)\rVert_{E^{r}}\leqslant\lVert T_{n+1}h\rVert_{F^{\beta-\mu}}\lesssim\lVert h\rVert_{F^{s}}.

Synthesizing these bounds shows that
\mathbf{II}_{n}\lesssim\langle 2^{\mu n}\lVert g,h\rVert_{F^{s}}\rangle^{3}\lVert h\rVert_{F^{s}}
, and hence

(2.5.48)

\lVert\Psi^{-1}(g)-\Psi^{-1}(g+h)\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim\langle\lVert g,h\rVert_{F^{s}}\rangle^{3}\Big(\lVert h\rVert_{F^{s}}+\Big(\sum_{j=n}^{\infty}\mathbf{m}_{j}^{s}(g)^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\Big)+\langle 2^{\mu n}\lVert g,h\rVert_{F^{s}}\rangle^{3}\lVert h\rVert_{F^{s}}.

By taking
n
large relative to
g
and then taking
h
small, we see that the above estimate proves that
\Psi^{-1}
is continuous at
g
. This completes the proof of the first item.

We continue by studying the differentiability of the inverse map
\Psi^{-1}
. Suppose that
s\in[\beta,R+r-\beta-\mu)\cap\mathbb{N}
,
g,h,g+h\in B_{F^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon)\cap F^{s}
. By expanding
\Psi
to second order via Taylor’s theorem with integral remainder and utilizing the left invertibility of
L
(see e.g. (
2.5.44
)), we derive the equality

(2.5.49)

\Psi^{-1}(g+h)-\Psi^{-1}(g)-L\circ\Psi^{-1}(g)h=\\
-L\circ\Psi^{-1}(g)\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)D^{2}\Psi((1-t)\Psi^{-1}(g)+t\Psi^{-1}(g+h))(\Psi^{-1}(g+h)-\Psi^{-1}(g))^{\otimes 2}\;\mathrm{d}t.

By taking the norm of both sides in the space
E^{s-\mu}
, employing tame estimates, and utilizing the third item of Theorem
2.21
, we find that

(2.5.50)

\lVert\Psi^{-1}(g+h)-\Psi^{-1}(g)-L\circ\Psi^{-1}(g)h\rVert_{E^{s-\mu}}\\
\lesssim\langle\lVert g,h\rVert_{F^{s}}\rangle^{2}\lVert\Psi^{-1}(g+h)-\Psi^{-1}(g)\rVert_{E^{s}}\lVert\Psi^{-1}(g+h)-\Psi^{-1}(g)\rVert_{E^{r}}\\
\lesssim\langle\lVert g,h\rVert_{F^{s}}\rangle^{2}\lVert\Psi^{-1}(g+h)-\Psi^{-1}(g)\rVert_{E^{s}}\lVert h\rVert_{F^{\beta-\mu}}.

Since
F^{s}\hookrightarrow F^{\beta-\mu}
and we have already established that
\Psi^{-1}
is continuous with respect to the
F^{s}
and
E^{s}
topologies, estimate (
2.5.50
) shows that
\Psi^{-1}
is differentiable at
g
when viewed as a map from
F^{s}
to
E^{s-\mu}
, and we have the derivative formula
D\Psi^{-1}(g)h=L\circ\Psi^{-1}(g)h
. Moreover, since
\Psi^{-1}
is continuous relative to the
F^{s}
and
E^{s}
topologies, and
L
is continuous relative to the
E^{s}\times F^{s-\mu}
and
E^{s-\mu}
topologies, we find, by composition of continuity, that
D\Psi^{-1}
is continuous with respect to the
F^{s}\times F^{s-\mu}
and
E^{s-\mu}
topologies. This proves the second item.

The third item now follows by pairing the final item of Theorem
2.27
and the derivative formula
D\Psi^{-1}=L\circ\Psi^{-1}
with a simple induction argument.
∎

3.
Nonlinear analysis of traveling free boundary compressible Navier-Stokes

With our tools from nonlinear analysis now established in abstract form, we return to our main goal: the analysis of the traveling wave free boundary compressible Navier-Stokes equations, (
1.4.9
). In this section we verify most of the ‘nonlinear’ hypotheses for our Nash-Moser inverse function theorem and make preparations for the linear analysis that follows in subsequent sections.

We select a nonlinear mapping with tame Banach scales for the domain and codomain. In Section
3.1
we verify condition II of Theorem
2.21
. To check that our rather complicated operator is
1
-tamely
C^{2}
, we analyze each of the atomic nonlinearities individually in Section
3.2
and then synthesize in Section
3.3
via the calculus of tame maps from Section
2.1
. Once this is done, in Section
3.4
we then use our newly developed understanding of the nonlinear map to decompose its derivative into a principal part and a remainder term. We then conclude in Section
3.5
with some preliminary results for our linear analysis.

3.1.
Banach scales for the traveling wave problem

We begin by defining some function spaces that will comprise our Banach scales. First, we introduce spaces that will play a role in the domain of our nonlinear map. For
s\in\{{-1,0}\}\cup\mathbb{R}^{+}
we define

(3.1.1)

\mathbb{X}_{s}=H^{1+s}(\Omega)\times H^{2+s}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\mathcal{H}^{5/2+s}(\Sigma),

where
\mathcal{H}^{5/2+s}(\Sigma)
denotes the anisotropic Sobolev space defined in (
B.1.1
), with
\Sigma
identified with
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
, and we endow
\mathbb{X}_{s}
with the Hilbert norm

(3.1.2)

\lVert q,u,\eta\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}=\sqrt{\lVert q\rVert_{H^{1+s}}^{2}+\lVert u\rVert_{H^{2+s}}^{2}+\lVert\eta\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{5/2+s}}}.

We single out an important closed subspace of
\mathbb{X}_{s}
:

(3.1.3)

\mathbb{X}^{s}=\{{(q,u,\eta)\in\mathbb{X}_{s}\;:\;\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}(u)=0,\;\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma}(u\cdot e_{n})+\partial_{1}\eta=0}\}.

Second, we define some spaces that will play a role in the codomain of our nonlinear map. For
s\in\{{-1,0}\}\cup\mathbb{R}^{+}
we define the space

(3.1.4)

\mathbb{Y}_{s}=\begin{cases}L^{2}(\Omega)\times({{}_{0}}H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n}))^{\ast}&\text{if }s=-1,\\
H^{1+s}(\Omega)\times H^{s}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})\times H^{1/2+s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}^{n})&\text{if }s\geqslant 0\end{cases}

and endow it with the norm

(3.1.5)

\begin{cases}\lVert g,F\rVert_{\mathbb{Y}_{s}}=\sqrt{\lVert g\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}+\lVert F\rVert_{({{}_{0}}H^{1})^{\ast}}^{2}}&\text{if }s=-1,\\
\lVert g,f,k\rVert_{\mathbb{Y}_{s}}=\sqrt{\lVert g\rVert^{2}_{H^{1+s}}+\lVert f\rVert^{2}_{H^{s}}+\lVert k\rVert^{2}_{H^{1/2+s}}}&\text{if }s\geqslant 0.\end{cases}

We also single out an important subspace of
\mathbb{Y}_{s}
:

(3.1.6)

\mathbb{Y}^{s}=\begin{cases}\Big\{{(g,F)\in\mathbb{Y}_{s}\;:\;\int_{0}^{b}g(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y\in\dot{H}^{-1}(\Sigma)}\Big\}&\text{if }s=-1,\\
\Big\{{(g,f,k)\in\mathbb{Y}_{s}\;:\;\int_{0}^{b}g(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y\in\dot{H}^{-1}(\Sigma)}\Big\}&\text{if }s\geqslant 0,\end{cases}

which we endow with the norm

(3.1.7)

\begin{cases}\lVert g,F\rVert_{\mathbb{Y}^{s}}=\sqrt{\lVert g,F\rVert_{\mathbb{Y}_{-1}}^{2}+[{\int_{0}^{b}g(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y}]^{2}_{\dot{H}^{-1}}}&\text{if }s=-1,\\
\lVert g,f,k\rVert_{\mathbb{Y}^{s}}=\sqrt{\lVert g,f,k\rVert^{2}_{\mathbb{Y}_{s}}+[{\int_{0}^{b}g(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y}]^{2}_{\dot{H}^{-1}}}&\text{if }s\geqslant 0.\end{cases}

The spaces
\mathbb{Y}_{s}
and
\mathbb{Y}^{s}
are clearly complete and are Hilbert spaces in the case
s\geqslant 0
.

Remark 3.1
.

In the notation of (
C.1.1
), we have that
\mathbb{Y}^{s}=\hat{H}^{1+s}(\Omega)\times H^{s}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})\times H^{1/2+s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
for
\mathbb{R}\ni s\geqslant 0
, while
\mathbb{Y}^{-1}=\hat{H}^{0}(\Omega)\times({{}_{0}}H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n}))^{\ast}
.

Third, we introduce spaces that will contain the stress and forcing data tuple
(T,G,F)
. For
\mathbb{R}\ni s\geqslant 0
we define the space

(3.1.8)

\mathbb{W}_{s}=H^{1+s}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})\times H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})\times H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})

and endow it with the norm

(3.1.9)

\lVert\mathcal{T},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F}\rVert_{\mathbb{W}_{s}}=\sqrt{\lVert\mathcal{T}\rVert_{H^{1+s}}^{2}+\lVert\mathcal{G}\rVert_{H^{s}}^{2}+\lVert\mathcal{F}\rVert^{2}_{H^{s}}}.

Finally, for
s\in\mathbb{N}
we set

(3.1.10)

\mathbb{E}^{s}=\mathbb{X}^{s}\times\mathbb{W}_{1+s}\times\mathbb{R}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{F}^{s}=\mathbb{Y}^{s}\times\mathbb{W}_{1+s}\times\mathbb{R}

and endow these with the norm from Remark
2.3
.

From these spaces we now define the following Banach scales:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathbb{X}}

\displaystyle=\{{\mathbb{X}^{s}}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathbb{Y}}

\displaystyle=\{{\mathbb{Y}^{s}}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathbb{W}}

\displaystyle=\{{\mathbb{W}_{1+s}}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}},

(3.1.11)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathbb{E}}

\displaystyle=\{{\mathbb{E}^{s}}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathbb{F}}

\displaystyle=\{{\mathbb{F}^{s}}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}.

It is a simple matter to check that these all satisfy the conditions required by Definition
2.2
to be Banach scales.

In the next result we check the second condition in the statement of Theorem
2.21
for these scales.

Lemma 3.2

(Tameness of domain and codomain)
.

The Banach scale
\boldsymbol{\mathbb{E}}
is tame, and the Banach scale
\boldsymbol{\mathbb{F}}
is a tame direct summand of
\boldsymbol{\mathbb{E}}
in the sense of Definition
2.14
.

Proof.

We begin by proving that
\boldsymbol{\mathbb{E}}
is a tame Banach scale. The scale
\boldsymbol{\mathbb{W}}
is tame in light of Lemma
2.17
and Example
2.13
, as it is the product of scales of
L^{2}
-based Sobolev spaces on all of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Since
\boldsymbol{\mathbb{W}}
is tame, this lemma also shows that it suffices to prove that
\mathbb{R}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathbb{X}}=\{{\mathbb{X}^{s}}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
are tame.
\mathbb{R}
is handled by Example
2.12
, so it remains only to handle
\boldsymbol{\mathbb{X}}
.

For
s\in\mathbb{N}
write
\overline{\mathbb{X}}_{s}=H^{1+s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\times H^{2+s}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\mathcal{H}^{5/2+s}(\Sigma)
. Thanks to Lemma
B.6
, Example
2.13
, and Lemma
2.17
the Banach scale
\{{\overline{\mathbb{X}}_{s}}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
is LP smoothable. We now show that
\{{\mathbb{X}^{s}}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
is tame by showing it is a tame direct summand of
\{{\overline{\mathbb{X}}_{s}}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
. To this end, we define an auxiliary mapping
\mathcal{E}_{1}:H^{1/2+s}(\Sigma_{0};\mathbb{R}^{n})\times H^{1/2+s}(\Sigma)\to H^{1+s}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
via
\mathcal{E}_{1}(\phi,\varphi)=w
, where
w
is the unique solution to the PDE

(3.1.12)

\begin{cases}-\Delta w=0&\text{in }\Omega,\\
w=\phi&\text{on }\Sigma_{0},\\
(I-e_{n}\otimes e_{n})w=0&\text{on }\Sigma,\\
w\cdot e_{n}=\varphi&\text{on }\Sigma.\end{cases}

Also, let
\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}
denote a Stein extension operator, in the sense of Definition
A.2
, and write
\mathfrak{R}_{\Omega}
for the linear operator corresponding to restriction from
\mathbb{R}^{n}
to
\Omega
. We then define the lifting operators
\lambda_{\mathbb{X}}:\mathbb{X}^{s}\to\overline{\mathbb{X}}_{s}
and the restriction operators
\rho_{\mathbb{X}}:\overline{\mathbb{X}}_{s}\to\mathbb{X}^{s}
via

(3.1.13)

\lambda_{\mathbb{X}}(q,u,\eta)=(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}q,\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}u,\eta)\text{ and }\rho_{\mathbb{X}}(q,u,\eta)=(\mathfrak{R}_{\Omega}q,\mathfrak{R}_{\Omega}u-\mathcal{E}_{1}(\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}u,\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma}(u\cdot e_{n})+\partial_{1}\eta),\eta).

In light of Proposition
B.4
, it is straightforward to check that
\lambda_{\mathbb{X}}
and
\rho_{\mathbb{X}}
indeed define bounded linear maps between their stated domains and codomains. Additionally, we have that
\rho_{\mathbb{X}}\lambda_{\mathbb{X}}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{X}^{0}}
, which completes the proof that
\{{\mathbb{X}^{s}}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
is a tame direct summand of
\{{\overline{\mathbb{X}}_{s}}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
, and hence is tame. In turn, this completes the proof that
\boldsymbol{\mathbb{E}}
is tame.

We continue by showing that
\boldsymbol{\mathbb{F}}
is a tame direct summand of
\boldsymbol{\mathbb{E}}
. For this, it is clearly sufficient to prove that
\boldsymbol{\mathbb{Y}}
is a tame direct summand of
\boldsymbol{\mathbb{X}}
. In fact, we have the stronger result that these spaces are isomorphic. To see this, consider the map
\Gamma:\mathbb{X}^{s}\to\mathbb{Y}^{s}
defined by
\Gamma(q,u,\eta)=(g,f,k)
, where

(3.1.14)

\begin{cases}\nabla\cdot u=g&\text{in }\Omega,\\
-\partial_{1}u+\nabla(q+\eta)-\Delta u=f&\text{in }\Omega,\\
-(q-\mathbb{D}u)e_{n}-\Delta_{\|}\eta e_{n}=k&\text{on }\Sigma,\\
u\cdot e_{n}+\partial_{1}\eta=0&\text{on }\Sigma,\\
u=0&\text{on }\Sigma_{0}.\end{cases}

This is well-defined by virtue of Proposition
B.4
. The proof of Theorem 6.6 in Leoni and Tice
[
60
]
establishes that this map is a Banach isomorphism, although the theorem itself states the isomorphism between slightly different spaces, which is needed in
[
60
]
due to a change of unknown made by taking a particular linear combination of
q
and
\eta
.
∎

3.2.
Analysis of atomic nonlinearities

In this subsection we put to use the abstract analysis of smooth tame structures from Section
2.1
and the tools for verifying smoothness and tame estimates from Appendices
D.1
and
D.2
in the study of the nonlinear expressions appearing in the equations under consideration, namely system (
1.4.9
). The majority of the results in this subsection are a recasting of the tame calculus estimates from Appendix
D.2
in the language of Section
2.1
. As such, some of the proofs are not much more than mere referrals; it is the statements that are important as we move forward. Once these are established, we conclude this subsection by considering two nonlinearities that play a distinguished role in our analysis of (
1.4.9
). These aid in understanding the mapping properties of the continuity equation, which is rather subtle.

The Banach scales we work with in this subsection are built from combinations of the following atomic scales:

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}(U;V)

\displaystyle=\{{H^{s}(U;V)}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}},

\displaystyle\bm{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

\displaystyle=\{{\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}},

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}(U;V)

\displaystyle=\{{W^{s,\infty}(U;V)}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}},

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}\bm{\mathcal{H}}_{(\kappa)}

\displaystyle=\{{W^{s,\infty}((0,b);\mathcal{H}^{s}_{(\kappa)}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}},

(3.2.1)

\displaystyle\bm{\mathcal{H}}_{(\kappa)}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

\displaystyle=\{{\mathcal{H}_{(\kappa)}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}},

where
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
d\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
,
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a Stein extension domain (see Definition
A.2
),
V
is a finite dimensional vector space over
\mathbb{R}
, and the spaces
\mathcal{H}^{s}
,
\mathcal{H}_{(\kappa)}^{s}
are defined in Appendix
B.1
. We will often suppress the domain and codomain in this notation when they are clear from context. It is a simple matter to check that these are indeed Banach scales.

We begin by looking at products. Recall the spaces of (strongly)-tame maps introduced in Definition
2.4
.

Lemma 3.3

(Smooth tameness of products, 1)
.

Let
V_{1},V_{2}
and
W
be real finite dimensional vector spaces,
B:V_{1}\times V_{2}\to W
be a bilinear map, and
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a Stein-extension domain (see Definition
A.2
). The following inclusions hold when viewing
B
as a product for functions taking values in
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
.

(1)

B\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{0,1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}(\mathbf{H}(U;V_{1})\times\mathbf{H}(U;V_{2});\mathbf{H}(U;W))
.

(2)

B\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{0,0}(\mathbf{H}(U;V_{1})\times\mathbf{W}(U;V_{2});\mathbf{H}(U;W))
.

Proof.

By using the universality of tensor products to factor
B=L\circ\otimes
for a linear map
L:V_{1}\otimes V_{2}\to W
and then working component-wise, we see that this is a direct application of the high-low product estimates of Corollary
D.7
.
∎

We now handle more complicated products also involving sums.

Lemma 3.4

(Smooth tameness of products, 2)
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
,
V
,
W
, and
V_{1},\dots,V_{m}
be real finite dimensional vector spaces,
B:V_{1}\times\cdots\times V_{m}\times V\to W
be
(m+1)
-linear, and
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a Stein extension domain (see Definition
A.2
). The
(m+1)
-linear map
P_{B}
defined via
P_{B}((g_{j},\psi_{j})_{j=1}^{m},\varphi)=B(g_{1}+\psi_{1},\dots,g_{m}+\psi_{m},\varphi)
satisfies the inclusion

(3.2.2)

P_{B}\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{0,1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}\Big(\prod_{j=1}^{m}(\mathbf{W}(U;V_{j})\times\mathbf{H}(U;V_{j}))\times\mathbf{H}(U;V);\mathbf{H}(U;W)\Big).

Proof.

We proceed via induction on
m\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
. The base case,
m=1
, is a simple application of Lemma
3.3
. Now suppose that
m\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
and the result holds for
m
. We wish to prove it for
m+1
, so let
B:V_{1}\times\cdots V_{m}\times V_{m+1}\times V\to W
be an
(m+2)
-linear map on some finite dimensional real vector spaces. We claim that there exists a bilinear map
B_{1}:V_{1}\times(V_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes V_{m+1}\otimes V)\to W
and an
(m+1)
-linear map
B_{2}:V_{2}\times\cdots\times V_{m+1}\times V\to V_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes V_{m+1}\otimes V
such that

(3.2.3)

B(v_{1},\dots,v_{m+1},v)=B_{1}(v_{1},B_{2}(v_{2},\dots,v_{m+1},v)).

Indeed, by the universality property of tensor products there exists a linear map
L:V_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes V_{m+1}\otimes V\to W
defined via

(3.2.4)

L(v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{m+1}\otimes v)=B(v_{1},\dots,v_{m+1},v),

so we set
B_{1}(v_{1},\tau)=L(v_{1}\otimes\tau)
and
B_{2}(v_{2},\dots,v_{m+1},v)=v_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{m+1}\otimes v
. We then apply the induction hypothesis to the operator
P_{B_{2}}
to see that

(3.2.5)

P_{B_{2}}\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{0,1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}\Big(\prod_{j=2}^{m+1}(\mathbf{W}(U;V_{j})\times\mathbf{H}(U;V_{j}))\times\mathbf{H}(U;V);\mathbf{H}(U;V_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes V_{m+1}\otimes V)\Big)

Similarly, we apply the base case to the operator
P_{B_{1}}
and acquire the inclusion

(3.2.6)

P_{B_{1}}\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{0,1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}(\mathbf{W}(U;V_{1})\times\mathbf{H}(U;V_{1})\times\mathbf{H}(U;V_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes V_{m+1}\otimes V);\mathbf{H}(U;W)).

Equation (
3.2.3
) implies that
P_{B}((g_{j},\psi_{j})_{j=1}^{m+1},\varphi)=P_{B_{1}}((g_{1},\varphi_{1}),P_{B_{2}}((g_{j},\psi_{j})_{j=2}^{m+1},\varphi))
. Hence, the inclusion
P_{B}\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{0,1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}
is a consequence of the composition of smooth tame maps, Lemma
2.8
.
∎

We also consider products with members of the anisotropic Sobolev spaces, which are defined in Appendix
B.1
.

Lemma 3.5

(Smooth tameness of products, 3)
.

The following hold.

(1)

Let
r_{d}\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
be as defined in the second item of Proposition
B.2
. We have that the map
\prod_{j=1}^{r_{d}}\mathcal{H}^{0}_{(1)}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\ni(\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{r_{d}})\mapsto\prod_{j=1}^{r_{d}}\eta_{j}\in H^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
belongs to
{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{0,0}(\prod_{j=1}^{r_{d}}\bm{\mathcal{H}}_{(1)};\mathbf{H})
.

(2)

For any
\chi\in W^{\infty,\infty}(0,b)
and
\upnu\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
we have that the map
\prod_{j=1}^{\upnu}\mathcal{H}^{0}_{(1)}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\ni(\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{\upnu})\mapsto\chi\prod_{j=1}^{\upnu}\eta_{j}\in W^{0,\infty}((0,b);\mathcal{H}^{0}_{(\upnu)}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
belongs to
{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{0,0}(\prod_{j=1}^{\nu}\bm{\mathcal{H}}_{(1)};\mathbf{W}\bm{\mathcal{H}}_{(\nu)})
.

Proof.

The fact that the maps in the statement are well-defined and smooth follows from multilinearity and Proposition
B.2
.
Note that for
s\in\mathbb{N}
, the product map of the first item actually smoothly maps
\prod_{j=1}^{r_{d}}\mathcal{H}^{0}_{(1)}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

and the map of the second item smoothly maps
\prod_{j=1}^{\upnu}\mathcal{H}^{0}_{(1)}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to W^{s,\infty}((0,b);\mathcal{H}^{s}_{(\upnu)}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. Then the strong tame estimates on all of the derivatives follow from Remark
2.6
and these improved mapping properties.
∎

The next key nonlinear structure we handle is superposition as a Sobolev multiplier.

Lemma 3.6

(Smooth tameness of superposition multipliers)
.

Let
V_{1}
,
V_{2}
,
W
, and
Z
be real finite dimensional vector spaces, and
B:V_{1}\times V_{2}\to W
be a bilinear map. Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
O\subseteq Z
be Stein extension domains (see Definition
A.2
), and
f\in C^{\infty}_{b}(O;V_{1})
. Finally, suppose that
Y\subseteq W^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor,\infty}(U;Z)\times H^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(U;Z)
is an open set with the property that if
(g,\psi)\in Y
, then
(g+\psi)(U)\subseteq O
. Then the nonlinear operator
P
defined via
P(g,\psi,\varphi)=B(f(g+\psi),\varphi)
belongs to

(3.2.7)

{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{0,2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(Y\times H^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(U;V_{2}),\mathbf{W}(U;Z)\times\mathbf{H}(U;Z)\times\mathbf{H}(U;V_{2});\mathbf{H}(U;W)).

Proof.

By working component-wise and using the universality of tensor products, we see that as a consequence of Theorem
D.3
and the smoothness of the Sobolev multiplier times Sobolev to Sobolev pairing, for any
s\geqslant 2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor
the map

(3.2.8)

P:(Y\times H^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(U,V_{2}))\cap(W^{s,\infty}(U;Z)\times H^{s}(U;Z)\times H^{s}(U;V_{2}))\to H^{s}(U;W)

is well-defined and smooth. Thus, we need only check that the derivatives of
P
obey the required tame estimates. Consider first the case of zero derivatives, which we refer to as the base case. Employing the third item of Corollary
D.9
, working component-wise, and using the universality of tensor products, we readily deduce that the map in (
3.2.8
) belongs to the space
{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{0}_{0,2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}
.

Now for
k\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
we consider the case of
k
-derivatives of
P
. A short computation reveals the derivative formula

(3.2.9)

D^{k}P(g,\psi,\varphi)(g_{j},\psi_{j},\varphi_{j})_{j=1}^{k}=B(D^{k}f(g+\psi)(g_{j}+\psi_{j})_{j=1}^{k},\varphi)+\sum_{i=1}^{k}B(D^{k-1}f(g+\psi)(g_{j}+\psi_{j})_{j\neq i},\varphi_{i}).

Again by the universality of tensor products, there are linear maps

(3.2.10)

L^{k}:\mathcal{L}^{k}(Z;V_{1})\otimes\Big(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}Z\Big)\otimes V_{2}\to W\text{ and }L_{k-1}:\mathcal{L}^{k-1}(Z;V_{1})\otimes\Big(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k-1}Z\Big)\otimes V_{2}\to W

(depending only on
k
and
B
) such that

(3.2.11)

B(D^{k}f(g+\psi)(g_{j}+\psi_{j})_{j=1}^{k},\varphi)=L^{k}\Big(D^{k}f(g+\psi)\otimes\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}(g_{j}+\psi_{j})\otimes\varphi\Big)

and

(3.2.12)

B(D^{k-1}f(g+\psi)(g_{j}+\psi_{j})_{j\neq i},\varphi_{i})=L_{k-1}\Big(D^{k-1}f(g+\psi)\otimes\bigotimes_{j\neq i}(g_{j}+\psi_{j})\otimes\varphi_{i}\Big).

We then find that the operators in (
3.2.11
) and (
3.2.12
) belong to
{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{0}_{0,2+{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}
by applying the base case, the tameness of composition from Lemma
2.8
, and the second version of tameness of products, Lemma
3.4
. This shows that, indeed,
D^{k}P
belongs to
{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{0}_{0,2+{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
∎

The next nonlinear structure we examine is superposition.

Lemma 3.7

(Smooth tameness of superposition)
.

Let
\mathbb{N}\ni k\geqslant 2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor
. The following hold.

(1)

Let
V
,
W
be real finite dimensional vector spaces,
0\in O\subseteq V
be a Stein extension domain (see Definition
A.2
) that is star shaped with respect to the origin,
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a Stein extension domain, and
f\in C^{\infty}(O;W)
be such that
f(0)=0
and
Df\in C^{\infty}_{b}(O;\mathcal{L}(V;W))
. Let
Y\subseteq H^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(U;V)
be an open set with the property that if
\varphi\in Y
, then
\varphi(U)\subseteq O
. Then the operator
\varphi\mapsto f(\varphi)

belongs to
{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{0,2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(Y,\mathbf{H}(U;V);\mathbf{H}(U;W))
.

(2)

Suppose that
\Phi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
is a bi-Lipschitz homeomorphism and a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism such that
D\Phi\in C^{\infty}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
. There exists an open set
0\in U_{\Phi}\subseteq W^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})\times H^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
, depending only on
\Phi
and the dimension, such that for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
, the operator
P
defined via
P(f,g,h)=f(\Phi+g+h)

satisfies

(3.2.13)

P\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{m}_{0,2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(H^{m+2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{d})\times U_{\Phi},\mathbf{I}_{m};\mathbf{H}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{d})),

where
\mathbf{I}_{m}=\{{H^{m+s}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{d})\times W^{s,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n})\times H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
.

Proof.

We begin by proving the first item. Note that by using the fundamental theorem of calculus, the map from the first item has the equivalent formula

(3.2.14)

\varphi\mapsto f(\varphi)=\int_{0}^{1}Df(t\varphi)(\varphi)\;\mathrm{d}t.

For each
t\in[0,1]
, the map
\varphi\mapsto Df(t\varphi)(\varphi)
is seen to be
{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{0,2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}
as a consequence of our previous result on the smooth tameness of superposition multipliers, Lemma
3.6
, and the composition of smooth tame maps, Lemma
2.8
. In fact, it is a simple matter to verify that we actually have uniformity of the defining inequalities with respect to
t
as well as the satisfaction of the remaining hypotheses of Lemma
2.10
. Hence, the first item follows by applying the lemma.

We next consider the second item. Theorem
D.2
establishes the existence of an open set
U_{\Phi}\subseteq W^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})\times H^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
s\geqslant 2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor
the map

(3.2.15)

P:H^{m+s}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{d})\times(U_{\Phi}\cap(W^{s,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})\times H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})))\to H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{d})

is well-defined and
C^{m}
. Thus we need only verify that the derivatives of
P
, which are enumerated in (
D.1.7
), are
{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{0}_{0,2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}
. The case of the
0^{\mathrm{th}}
derivative (
m=0
) follows immediately from the second item of Corollary
D.10
and the bound
\lVert\det D(g+h)^{-1}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant 2^{n}\lVert D\Phi^{-1}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}^{n}
for all
(g,h)\in U_{\Phi}
, which holds as a consequence of Lemma
D.1
and Theorem
D.2
. When
m>0
, the formula (
D.1.7
) shows that the
m^{\mathrm{th}}
derivative is built from simple products and the
0^{\mathrm{th}}
derivative structure. Consequently, the result follows in this case by supplementing this observation with Lemmas
2.8
and
3.4
.
∎

The next two results, which happen to be the most subtle part of the nonlinear analysis, deal with superposition-like nonlinearities whose argument contains a member of the anisotropic Sobolev space. Recall that Appendix
B.1
gives the notation and an enumeration of basic properties for these specialized spaces. In the following statement
r_{d}\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, for
d=n-1
, refers to the number defined in the second item of Proposition
B.2
.

Lemma 3.8

(Taylor expansion trick)
.

Let
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
be an open interval containing
[-\mathfrak{g}b,0]
and
\phi\in C^{\infty}(I)
. There exists a
\rho_{I}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
,

(3.2.16)
