\mathcal{N}_{\phi}^{(1)}\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{0,2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(B_{H^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho_{I})\times B_{\mathcal{H}^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho_{I}),\mathbf{H}(\Omega)\times\bm{\mathcal{H}}(\Sigma);\mathbf{H}(\Omega)),

and

(3.2.17)

\mathcal{N}_{\phi}^{(2)}\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{0,0}(\bm{\mathcal{H}}_{(1)}(\Sigma);\mathbf{W}\bm{\mathcal{H}}_{(r_{n-1}-1)})

such that
\mathcal{N}_{\phi}^{(1)}(0,0)=0
,
\mathcal{N}^{(2)}_{\phi}(0)=0
, and

(3.2.18)

\mathcal{N}_{\phi}(q,\eta)-\mathcal{N}_{\phi}(0,0)=\mathcal{N}^{(1)}_{\phi}(q,\eta)+\mathcal{N}^{(2)}_{\phi}(\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta),

where the projectors
\Uppi
are defined in (
B.1.4
) and we write

(3.2.19)

\mathcal{N}_{\phi}(q,\eta)=\phi(-\mathfrak{g}\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\cdot e_{n}+q+\mathfrak{g}(I-\mathcal{E})\eta)\text{ and }\mathcal{N}_{\phi}(0,0)=\phi(-\mathfrak{g}\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\cdot e_{n}).

Proof.

We begin by choosing
\rho_{I}
. Set
R_{I}=\mathrm{dist}(\mathbb{R}\setminus I,[-\mathfrak{g}b,0])/2\in(0,\infty]
. Thanks to the supercritical Sobolev embeddings, properties of anisotropic Sobolev from Proposition
B.1
, and the boundedness of the extension operator
\mathcal{E}
from Lemma
A.1
, we see that the map

(3.2.20)

H^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(\Omega)\times\mathcal{H}^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(\Sigma)\ni(q,\eta)\mapsto q+\mathfrak{g}(I-\mathcal{E})\eta\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)

is well-defined and continuous. Hence, the preimage of
B_{L^{\infty}}(0,R_{I})
under this map is an open set
U_{I}
containing the origin. We take
\rho_{I}=\mathrm{dist}(0,\partial U_{I})/\sqrt{2}
so that

(3.2.21)

B_{H^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho_{I})\times B_{\mathcal{H}^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho_{I})\subseteq U_{I}.

Now let
(q,\eta)\in B_{H^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho_{I})\times B_{\mathcal{H}^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho_{I})
. Note that
\mathcal{N}_{\phi}(q,\eta)
, as defined in (
3.2.19
), is well-defined as a function from
\Omega
to
\mathbb{R}
. We write

(3.2.22)

\mathcal{N}_{\phi}(q,\eta)=\phi(g_{0}+g+\varphi),

where
g_{0}=-\mathfrak{g}\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\cdot e_{n}
,
g=\mathfrak{g}(I-\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\cdot e_{n}/b)\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta
, and
\varphi=q+\mathfrak{g}(I-\mathcal{E})\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{H}}\eta
. Hence,

(3.2.23)

\mathcal{N}_{\phi}(q,\eta)-\mathcal{N}_{\phi}(0,0)=(\phi(g_{0}+g+\varphi)-\phi(g_{0}+g))+(\phi(g_{0}+g)-\phi(g_{0}))=\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{II}.

For
\mathbf{I}
we use the fundamental theorem of calculus to express

(3.2.24)

\mathbf{I}=\varphi\int_{0}^{1}\phi^{\prime}(g_{0}+g+\tau\varphi)\;\mathrm{d}\tau,

whereas for
\mathbf{II}
we use Taylor’s formula with integral remainder to write

(3.2.25)

\mathbf{II}=\sum_{j=1}^{r_{d}-1}\frac{\phi^{(j)}(g_{0})}{j!}g^{j}+g^{r_{d}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{(1-\tau)^{r_{d}-1}}{(r_{d}-1)!}\phi^{(r_{d})}(g_{0}+\tau g)\;\mathrm{d}\tau,

where
r_{d}=r_{n-1}\geqslant 1
is given by (
B.1.5
) and sums over empty intervals are understood as zero. This leads us to define

(3.2.26)

\mathcal{N}^{(1)}_{\phi}(q,\eta)=\varphi\int_{0}^{1}\phi^{\prime}(g_{0}+g+\tau\varphi)\;\mathrm{d}\tau+g^{r_{d}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{(1-\tau)^{r_{d}-1}}{(r_{d}-1)!}\phi^{(r_{d})}(g_{0}+\tau g)\mathrm{d}\tau

and

(3.2.27)

\mathcal{N}^{(2)}_{\phi}(\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta)=\sum_{j=1}^{r_{d}-1}\frac{\phi^{(j)}(g_{0})}{j!}g^{j}.

The computation leading up to these definitions shows that decomposition (
3.2.18
) holds and that
\mathcal{N}^{(1)}_{\phi}
and
\mathcal{N}_{\phi}^{(2)}
vanish at the origin.

Now we analyze the tame smoothness of
\mathcal{N}^{(1)}_{\phi}
and
\mathcal{N}^{(2)}_{\phi}
. The former is
{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{0,2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}
thanks to the results on addition of smooth tame maps, Lemma
2.10
, smooth tameness of superposition multipliers, Lemma
3.6
, composition of smooth tame maps, Lemma
2.8
, and the third version of smooth tameness of products, Lemma
3.5
. On the other hand, the map
\mathcal{N}_{\phi}^{(2)}
is
{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{0,0}
as a consequence of Lemma
3.5
.
∎

We now use the previous result to understand the mapping properties of the vector field argument of the divergence in the continuity equation of (
1.4.9
).

Lemma 3.9

(A vector field decomposition)
.

Let
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
be an open interval containing
[-\mathfrak{g}b,0]
,
\phi\in C^{\infty}(I)
, and
\rho_{I}
be as in Lemma
3.8
. Let the open set
O_{I}
be given by
B_{H^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho_{I})\times H^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})\times B_{\mathcal{H}^{3+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho_{I})
, and consider the Banach scale

(3.2.28)

\mathbf{J}=\{{H^{s}(\Omega)\times H^{s}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\mathcal{H}^{1+s}(\Sigma)}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}.

There exist

(3.2.29)

\mathcal{M}_{\phi}^{(1)}\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{0,2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(O_{I},\mathbf{J};\mathbf{H}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n}))

and

(3.2.30)

e_{1}\cdot\mathcal{M}^{(2)}_{\phi}\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{0,0}(\bm{\mathcal{H}}_{(1)}(\Sigma);\mathbf{W}\bm{\mathcal{H}}_{(r_{n-1})})

such that
\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\phi}(0,0,0)=0
,
e_{1}\cdot\mathcal{M}^{(2)}_{\phi}(0)=0
,

(3.2.31)

\mathrm{Tr}_{\partial\Omega}(\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\phi}(q,u,\eta)\cdot e_{n})=\mathrm{Tr}_{\partial\Omega}(\mathcal{N}_{\phi}(q,\eta))\big((\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma}(u\cdot e_{n})+\partial_{1}\eta)\mathds{1}_{\Sigma}+\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}(u\cdot e_{n})\mathds{1}_{\Sigma_{0}}\big),

and

(3.2.32)

\mathcal{M}_{\phi}(q,u,\eta)-\mathcal{M}_{\phi}(0,0,0)=\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\phi}(q,u,\eta)+e_{1}\cdot\mathcal{M}_{\phi}^{(2)}(\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta)e_{1},

where we write

(3.2.33)

\mathcal{M}_{\phi}(q,u,\eta)=\mathcal{N}_{\phi}(q,\eta)(u-M_{\eta}e_{1})\text{ and }\mathcal{M}_{\phi}(0,0,0)=-\mathcal{N}_{\phi}(0,0)e_{1}

for
\mathcal{N}_{\phi}(q,\eta)
,
M_{\eta}
, and
\Uppi
given by (
3.2.19
), (
1.4.10
), and (
B.1.4
), respectively.

Proof.

The precise form of the decomposition (
3.2.32
), which uses Lemma
3.8
as well as the identities
M_{\eta}e_{1}=(1+\partial_{n}\mathcal{E}\eta)e_{1}-\partial_{1}\mathcal{E}\eta e_{n}
and
\partial_{n}\mathcal{E}\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta=\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta/b
, is given by

(3.2.34)

\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\phi}(q,u,\eta)=\mathcal{N}_{\phi}(q,\eta)(u-\partial_{n}\mathcal{E}\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{H}}\eta e_{1}+\partial_{1}\mathcal{E}\eta e_{n})-\mathcal{N}^{(1)}_{\phi}(q,\eta)(1+\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta/b)e_{1}

and

(3.2.35)

e_{1}\cdot\mathcal{M}_{\phi}^{(2)}(\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta)=-(\mathcal{N}_{\phi}(0,0)\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta/b+\mathcal{N}^{(2)}_{\phi}(\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta)(1+\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta/b)).

That
\mathcal{M}_{\phi}^{(1)}
is
{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{0,2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}
is a consequence of the composition of tame maps, Lemma
2.8
, the second product result, Lemma
3.4
, the mapping properties of
\mathcal{N}_{\phi}
, Lemma
3.8
, and the properties of anisotropic Sobolev spaces enumerated in (
B.1.25
) and Proposition
B.1
. That
e_{1}\cdot\mathcal{M}_{\phi}^{(2)}
is
{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{0,0}
is a consequence of Lemmas
3.5
and
3.8
.
∎

3.3.
Smooth tameness of the nonlinear operator

We begin by defining a nonlinear operator associated with the PDE (
1.4.9
). We will employ the Banach scales defined in Section
3.1
. For some
r\geqslant 2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor
,
0<\rho\leqslant\rho_{\mathrm{WD}}
, with
\rho_{\mathrm{WD}}
determined by Theorem
3.17
, and
s\geqslant r
, we set

(3.3.1)

\overline{\Psi}:(B_{\mathbb{X}^{r}}(0,\rho)\cap\mathbb{X}^{1+s})\times\mathbb{W}_{s}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\to\mathbb{Y}^{s}\times\mathbb{W}_{s}\times\mathbb{R}

via

(3.3.2)

\overline{\Psi}(q,u,\eta,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F},\gamma)=(\Psi(q,u,\eta,\gamma)+\Phi(q,u,\eta,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F}),\mathcal{T},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F},\gamma),

where

(3.3.3)

\Psi:(B_{\mathbb{X}^{r}}(0,\rho)\cap\mathbb{X}^{1+s})\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\to\mathbb{Y}^{s}\text{ and }\Phi:(B_{\mathbb{X}^{r}}(0,\rho)\cap\mathbb{X}^{s})\times\mathbb{W}_{s}\to\mathbb{Y}^{s}

are the principal and auxiliary parts of
\overline{\Psi}
, which are defined via

(3.3.4)

\Psi(q,u,\eta,\gamma)=(\Psi_{1}(q,u,\eta),\Psi_{2}(q,u,\eta,\gamma),\Psi_{3}(q,u,\eta,\gamma))

and

(3.3.5)

\Phi(q,u,\eta,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F})=(0,\Phi_{2}(q,\eta,\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F}),\Phi_{3}(\eta,\mathcal{T})),

where

(3.3.6)

\Psi_{1}(q,u,\eta)=\nabla\cdot(\sigma_{q,\eta}(u-M_{\eta}e_{1})),

(3.3.7)

\Psi_{2}(q,u,\eta,\gamma)=\gamma^{2}\sigma_{q,\eta}M_{\eta}^{-\mathrm{t}}((u-\gamma M_{\eta}e_{1})\cdot\nabla(M_{\eta}^{-1}u))+\sigma_{q,\eta}\nabla(q+\mathfrak{g}\eta)-\gamma M_{\eta}^{-\mathrm{t}}\nabla\cdot(\mathbb{S}^{\sigma_{q,\eta}}_{\mathcal{A}_{\eta}}(M_{\eta}^{-1}u)M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}}),

(3.3.8)

\Psi_{3}(q,u,\eta,\gamma)=-((P-P_{\mathrm{ext}})\circ\sigma_{q,\eta}-\gamma\mathbb{S}^{\sigma_{q,\eta}}_{\mathcal{A}_{\eta}}(M_{\eta}^{-1}u))M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}}e_{n}-\varsigma\mathscr{H}(\eta)M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}}e_{n},

(3.3.9)

\Phi_{2}(q,\eta,\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F})=-J_{\eta}M_{\eta}^{-\mathrm{t}}(\sigma_{q,\eta}\mathcal{G}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}+\mathcal{F}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}),

and

(3.3.10)

\Phi_{3}(\eta,\mathcal{T})=-\mathcal{T}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}}e_{n}.

Here we recall that
\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}
is defined by (
1.4.4
),
\mathcal{A}_{\eta}
and
J_{\eta}
by (
1.4.5
),
\mathbb{S}^{\uptau}_{\mathcal{A}}
by (
1.4.8
),
M_{\eta}
by (
1.4.10
), and
\sigma_{q,\eta}
by (
1.4.11
). We also recall the assumptions on
\varsigma
,
\upmu
, and
\uplambda
stated in (
1.1.7
).

Armed with the results from the previous two subsections, we now endeavor to study the smooth tameness of the map
\overline{\Psi}
from (
3.3.1
). Our strategy is to handle the
\Psi
and
\Phi
pieces separately and component-wise. Our first result studies the continuity equation piece,
\Psi_{1}
, which we recall is defined in (
3.3.6
). This
\Psi_{1}
term is the source of the derivative loss and also requires the most careful analysis among the smooth tameness verification results.

Proposition 3.10

(Smooth tameness of the continuity equation)
.

There exists a
\rho_{\mathrm{cont}}\in(0,\infty)
, depending only on the domain of the inverse enthalpy (see (
1.1.11
)) such that the following hold

(1)

There exists constants
c,C\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that for all
(q,u,\eta)\in B_{\mathbb{X}^{1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho_{\mathrm{cont}})
we have the estimate
c\leqslant\sigma_{q,\eta}\leqslant C
where
\sigma_{q,\eta}
is defined in (
1.4.11
).

(2)

\Psi_{1}\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{1,1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(B_{\mathbb{X}^{1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho_{\mathrm{cont}}),\boldsymbol{\mathbb{X}};\{{\hat{H}^{1+s}(\Omega)}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}})
, where we recall that the spaces
\hat{H}^{s}(\Omega)
are defined in (
C.1.1
) (see also Remark
3.1
).

Proof.

We begin by proving the second item. First, we use Lemma
3.9
to set
\rho_{\mathrm{cont}}=\rho_{(H_{\mathrm{min}},H_{\mathrm{max}})}
, where
H_{\mathrm{min}}
and
H_{\mathrm{max}}
are defined by (
1.1.11
), to see that
\nabla\cdot\mathcal{M}_{H^{-1}}(0,0,0)=0
, and to equate

(3.3.11)

\Psi_{1}(q,u,\eta)=\nabla\cdot(\mathcal{M}_{H^{-1}}^{(1)}(q,u,\eta))+\partial_{1}(e_{1}\cdot\mathcal{M}^{(2)}_{H^{-1}}(\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta))=\Psi^{\mathbf{I}}_{1}(q,u,\eta)+\Psi_{1}^{\mathbf{II}}(\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta).

In the above
H^{-1}
refers to the inverse enthalpy function (see
1.1.10
). As a direct consequence of Lemma
3.9
, we obtain the inclusion

(3.3.12)

\Psi_{1}^{\mathbf{I}}\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{1,1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(B_{\mathbb{X}^{1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho_{\mathrm{cont}}),\boldsymbol{\mathbb{X}};\{{H^{1+s}(\Omega)}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}).

On the other hand, the boundary conditions built into the definition of the space
\mathbb{X}^{1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}
in (
3.1.3
), together with condition (
3.2.31
), imply that

(3.3.13)

\int_{0}^{b}\Psi^{\mathbf{I}}_{1}(q,u,\eta)(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y=(\nabla_{\|},0)\cdot\int_{0}^{b}\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{H^{-1}}(q,u,\eta)(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y,

and hence we deduce that
\Psi_{1}^{\mathbf{I}}
also maps smoothly into the space
\hat{H}^{0}(\Omega)
. This fact combined with (
3.3.12
) proves that
\Psi^{\mathbf{I}}_{1}
is
{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{1,1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}
with respect to the Banach scales stated in the hypotheses.

We next handle the
\Psi_{1}^{\mathbf{II}}
piece. By the norm in the anisotropic Sobolev spaces given in (
B.1.25
), we readily see that the embedding
W^{0,\infty}((0,b);\partial_{1}\mathcal{H}^{0}_{(r_{n-1})}(\Sigma))\hookrightarrow H^{0}(\Omega)
holds and hence we deduce from conclusion (
3.2.30
) of Lemma
3.9
that
\Psi_{1}^{\mathbf{II}}\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{0,0}(\bm{\mathcal{H}}_{(1)}(\Sigma);\mathbf{H}(\Omega))
.

By inspection of the anisotropic norm from (
B.1.25
) again, we also deduce the embedding

(3.3.14)

W^{0,\infty}((0,b);\partial_{1}\mathcal{H}^{0}_{(r_{n-1})}(\Sigma))\hookrightarrow W^{0,\infty}((0,b);\dot{H}^{-1}(\Sigma)),

and hence the map

(3.3.15)

\mathcal{H}^{0}_{(1)}(\Sigma)\ni\zeta\mapsto\int_{0}^{b}\Psi_{1}^{\mathbf{II}}(\zeta)(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y\in\dot{H}^{-1}(\Sigma)

is well-defined and smooth. These facts merge to show that
\Psi_{1}^{\mathbf{II}}
is
{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{0,0}
with respect to the Banach scales
\bm{\mathcal{H}}_{(1)}(\Sigma)
and
\{{\hat{H}^{s}(\Omega)}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
. We synthesize our results for
\Psi_{1}^{\mathbf{I}}
and
\Psi_{1}^{\mathbf{II}}
to complete the proof of the second item.

We now deduce the bounds stated in the first item. By unpacking the meaning of
\rho_{\mathrm{cont}}=\rho_{I}
from Lemma
3.8
, for
I=(H_{\mathrm{min}},H_{\mathrm{max}})
, we find that for
(q,u,\eta)\in B_{\mathbb{X}^{1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho_{\mathrm{cont}})
we have the inclusion

(3.3.16)

(-\mathfrak{g}\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\cdot e_{n}+q+\mathfrak{g}(I-\mathcal{E})\eta)(\Omega)\subseteq(h_{\star},h^{\star})\Subset(H_{\mathrm{min}},H_{\mathrm{max}}),

for some universal
h_{\star},h^{\star}\in(H_{\mathrm{min}},H_{\mathrm{max}})
.
Hence, by applying the inverse enthalpy, we find that
H^{-1}(h_{\star})\leqslant\sigma_{q,\eta}\leqslant H^{-1}(h^{\star})
, which is the first item with
c=H^{-1}(h_{\star}),C=H^{-1}(h^{\star})\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
.
∎

Next we examine the
\Psi_{2}
piece of the momentum equation, which we recall is defined in (
3.3.7
).

Proposition 3.11

(Smooth tameness of the momentum equation, 1)
.

There exits a
\rho_{\mathrm{mome}}\in(0,\rho_{\mathrm{cont}}]
such that the following hold.

(1)

For
(q,u,\eta)\in B_{\mathbb{X}^{1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho_{\mathrm{mome}})
, we have the bounds
1/2\leqslant J_{\eta}\leqslant 3/2
, where
J_{\eta}
is defined in (
1.4.5
).

(2)

\Psi_{2}\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{0,1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(B_{\mathbb{X}^{1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho_{\mathrm{mome}})\times\mathbb{R}^{+},\boldsymbol{\mathbb{X}}\times\mathbb{R};\mathbf{H}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n}))
.

Proof.

We begin by selecting
\rho_{\mathrm{mome}}
. The map

(3.3.17)

\mathcal{H}^{3/2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(\Sigma)\ni\eta\mapsto J_{\eta}-1=\partial_{n}\mathcal{E}\eta\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)

is bounded thanks to the supercritical Sobolev embedding and the continuity properties of
\mathcal{E}
from Proposition
A.1
. Denote the preimage of the set
B_{L^{\infty}}(0,1/2)
under this map by
O\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{3/2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(\Sigma)
.
O
is open and we set
\widetilde{\rho}_{\mathrm{mome}}=\mathrm{dist}(0,\partial O)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and
\rho_{\mathrm{mome}}=\min\{{\widetilde{\rho}_{\mathrm{mome}},\rho_{\mathrm{cont}}}\}
, where the latter is defined in Proposition
3.10
.

With this choice of
\rho_{\mathrm{mome}}
we have that
1/2\leqslant J_{\eta}\leqslant 3/2
, and hence the inverse matrix
M_{\eta}^{-1}
exists whenever
\left\lVert\eta\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{3/2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}<\rho_{\mathrm{mome}}
. Consequently, all of the expressions appearing in
\Psi_{2}
are pointwise well-defined. We next decompose

(3.3.18)

\Psi_{2}(q,u,\eta,\gamma)=\gamma^{2}\Psi_{2}^{\mathbf{I}}(q,u,\eta)+\Psi_{2}^{\mathbf{II}}(q,\eta)+\gamma\Psi_{2}^{\mathbf{III}}(q,u,\eta),

where

(3.3.19)

\begin{cases}\Psi_{2}^{\mathbf{I}}(q,u,\eta)=\sigma_{q,\eta}M_{\eta}^{-\mathrm{t}}[(u-M_{\eta}e_{1})\cdot\nabla(M_{\eta}^{-1}u)],\\
\Psi_{2}^{\mathbf{II}}(q,\eta)=\sigma_{q,\eta}\nabla(q+\mathfrak{g}\eta),\\
\Psi_{2}^{\mathbf{III}}(q,u,\eta)=-M_{\eta}^{-\mathrm{t}}\nabla\cdot(\mathbb{S}^{\sigma_{q,\eta}}_{\mathcal{A}_{\eta}}(M_{\eta}^{-1}u)M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}}).\end{cases}

The
\gamma
dependence in (
3.3.18
) is simple, and it is sufficient to study the pieces of (
3.3.19
) individually. For the first piece, we use Lemma
3.8
to write

(3.3.20)

\sigma_{q,\eta}=\varrho+\mathcal{N}_{H^{-1}}^{(1)}(q,\eta)+\mathcal{N}^{(2)}_{H^{-1}}(\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta),

where
H^{-1}
is the inverse of the enthalpy (see (
1.1.10
)). The embeddings of the second item of Proposition
B.1
allow us to view the latter map as
\mathcal{N}^{(2)}_{H^{-1}}\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{0,0}(\bm{\mathcal{H}}_{(1)}(\Sigma);\mathbf{W}(\Omega))
. Thanks to the smooth tameness of superposition multipliers proved in Proposition
3.6
, the map
(u,\eta)\mapsto\nabla(M_{\eta}^{-1}u)
is
{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T_{0,1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}^{\infty}
with respect to the Banach scales
\{{H^{2+s}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\mathcal{H}^{5/2+s}(\Sigma)}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\{{H^{s}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
. By applying the product of tame maps result from Lemma
3.4
and the composition of smooth tame maps result from Lemma
2.8
, we then see that the map
(u,\eta)\mapsto(u-M_{\eta}e_{1})\cdot\nabla(M_{\eta}^{-1}u)
is also
{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T_{0,1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}^{\infty}
with respect to the aforementioned Banach scales. By invoking Lemmas
3.6
and
2.8
again, we get that the same conclusion is true for the map
(u,\eta)\mapsto M^{-\mathrm{t}}_{\eta}[(u-M_{\eta}e_{1})\cdot\nabla(M_{\eta}^{-1}u)]
. Finally, by using Lemmas
3.8
,
3.4
, and
2.8
, we deduce that
(q,u,\eta)\mapsto(\varrho+\mathcal{N}_{H^{-1}}^{(1)}(q,\eta)+\mathcal{N}^{(2)}_{H^{-1}}(\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta))M^{-\mathrm{t}}_{\eta}[(u-M_{\eta}e_{1})\cdot\nabla(M_{\eta}^{-1}u)]
, i.e.
\Psi_{2}^{\mathbf{I}}
, is
{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T_{0,1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}^{\infty}
with respect to the Banach scales
\{{\mathbb{X}_{s}}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\{{H^{s}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
.

Now we examine the
\Psi_{2}^{\mathbf{II}}
piece. We again employ the density decomposition in (
3.3.20
). Then we see that
\Psi_{2}^{\mathbf{II}}
is
{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{0,1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}
for the Banach scales
\{{H^{1+s}(\Omega)\times\mathcal{H}^{5/2+s}(\Sigma)}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\{{H^{s}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
as a consequence of Lemmas
3.8
and
3.4
.

Finally, we examine the
\Psi_{2}^{\mathbf{III}}
piece, decomposing further:

(3.3.21)

\Psi_{2}^{\mathbf{III}}(q,u,\eta)=\Psi_{2}^{\mathbf{III_{1}}}(q,u,\eta)+\Psi_{2}^{\mathbf{III_{2}}}(q,u,\eta)+\Psi_{2}^{\mathbf{III_{3}}}(q,u,\eta)+\Psi_{2}^{\mathbf{III_{4}}}(q,u,\eta),

where

(3.3.22)

\begin{cases}\Psi_{2}^{\mathbf{III_{1}}}(q,u,\eta)=-M_{\eta}^{-\mathrm{t}}\nabla\cdot\big(\upmu(\sigma_{q,\eta})J_{\eta}^{-1}\nabla(M_{\eta}^{-1}u)M_{\eta}M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}}\big),\\
\Psi_{2}^{\mathbf{III_{2}}}(q,u,\eta)=-M_{\eta}^{-\mathrm{t}}\nabla\cdot(\upmu(\sigma_{q,\eta})J_{\eta}^{-1}M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}}\nabla(M_{\eta}^{-1}u)^{\mathrm{t}}M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}}),\\
\Psi_{2}^{\mathbf{III_{3}}}(q,u,\eta)=2M_{\eta}^{-\mathrm{t}}\nabla\cdot(\upmu(\sigma_{q,\eta})J_{\eta}^{-1}(\nabla\cdot u)M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}}),\\
\Psi_{2}^{\mathbf{III}_{4}}(q,u,\eta)=-M_{\eta}^{-\mathrm{t}}\nabla\cdot(\uplambda(\sigma_{q,\eta})J_{\eta}^{-1}(\nabla\cdot u)M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}}).\end{cases}

We handle these four terms in more or less the same way. The
\upmu
and
\uplambda
viscosity coefficients are decomposed as

(3.3.23)

\begin{cases}\upmu(\sigma_{q,\eta})=\upmu(\varrho)+\mathcal{N}^{(1)}_{\upmu\circ H^{-1}}(q,\eta)+\mathcal{N}^{(2)}_{\upmu\circ H^{-1}}(\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta),\\
\uplambda(\sigma_{q,\eta})=\uplambda(\varrho)+\mathcal{N}^{(1)}_{\uplambda\circ H^{-1}}(q,\eta)+\mathcal{N}^{(2)}_{\uplambda\circ H^{-1}}(\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta),\end{cases}

via Lemma
3.8
, while the
M_{\eta}
,
M_{\eta}^{-1}
,
M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}}
,
M_{\eta}^{-\mathrm{t}}
, and
J_{\eta}^{-1}
terms are viewed as superposition multipliers and thus are handled via Lemma
3.6
. Hence, the fact that each
\Psi_{2}^{\mathbf{III}_{j}}
,
j\in\{{1,\dots,4}\}
, is
{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{0,1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}
with respect to the Banach scales
\{{\mathbb{X}_{s}}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\{{H^{s}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
is a consequence of the aforementioned lemmas, the second result on products, Lemma
3.4
, and the result on composition of smooth tame maps, Lemma
2.8
.
∎

Now we examine the
\Psi_{3}
piece of the dynamic boundary condition, which we recall is defined in (
3.3.8
).

Proposition 3.12

(Smooth tameness of the dynamic boundary condition, 1)
.

With
\rho_{\mathrm{dyna}}=\rho_{\mathrm{mome}}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
as in Proposition
3.11
, we have

(3.3.24)

\Psi_{3}\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{0,1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(B_{\mathbb{X}^{1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho_{\mathrm{dyna}})\times\mathbb{R}^{+},\boldsymbol{\mathbb{X}}\times\mathbb{R};\{{H^{1/2+s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}^{n})}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}).

Proof.

As before, we begin the proof by decomposing

(3.3.25)

\Psi_{3}(q,u,\eta,\gamma)=\Psi_{3}^{\mathbf{I}}(q,\eta)+\gamma\Psi_{3}^{\mathbf{II}}(q,u,\eta)+\Psi_{3}^{\mathbf{III}}(\eta)

where

(3.3.26)

\begin{cases}\Psi_{3}^{\mathbf{I}}(q,\eta)=-(P\circ\sigma_{q,\eta}-P_{\mathrm{ext}})M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}}e_{n},\\
\Psi_{3}^{\mathbf{II}}(q,u,\eta)=\mathbb{S}^{\sigma_{q,\eta}}_{\mathcal{A}_{\eta}}(M_{\eta}^{-1}u)M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}}e_{n},\\
\Psi_{3}^{\mathbf{III}}(\eta)=-\varsigma\mathscr{H}(\eta)M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}}e_{n}.\end{cases}

For the first piece we recall that
H^{-1}
is the inverse of the enthalpy, as in (
1.1.10
), and use the fact that
\sigma_{q,\eta}=H^{-1}(-\mathfrak{g}b+q)
and
P_{\mathrm{ext}}=P\circ\varrho(b)=P\circ H^{-1}(-\mathfrak{g}b)
on
\Sigma
to rewrite

(3.3.27)

\Psi_{3}^{\mathbf{I}}(q,\eta)=(P\circ H^{-1}(\cdot-\mathfrak{g}b)-P\circ H^{-1}(-\mathfrak{g}b))(q)M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}}e_{n}.

From this, the operator
\Psi_{3}^{\mathbf{I}}
is readily seen to belong to
{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{0,1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}
with respect to the Banach scales
\{{H^{1+s}(\Omega)\times\mathcal{H}^{5/2+s}(\Sigma)}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\{{H^{1/2+s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}^{n})}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
, as a consequence of the first item of Lemma
3.7
regarding the tame smoothness of superposition, the result on tame smoothness of superposition multipliers, Lemma
3.6
, and the result on the composition of smooth tame maps, Lemma
2.8
.

The fact that
\Psi_{3}^{\mathbf{II}}
is
{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{0,1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}
for the scales
\{{\mathbb{X}_{s}}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\{{H^{1/2+s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}^{n})}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
follows from an argument similar to the analysis of the
\Psi_{2}^{\mathbf{III}}
-term from the proof of Lemma
3.11
.

Finally, we handle
\Psi_{3}^{\mathbf{III}}
. The mean curvature operator has the expression
\mathscr{H}(\eta)=\nabla_{\|}\cdot(\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}(\nabla_{\|}\eta))
, where the map
\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1};\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
is given by
\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}(v)=\langle v\rangle^{-1}v
. From this we deduce that
\Psi_{3}^{\mathbf{III}}
is
{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{0,1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}
by combining the conclusions of Lemmas
3.7
(the first item),
3.6
, and
2.8
.
∎

We now synthesize our previous results to deduce the smooth tameness of the principal part nonlinear operator from (
3.3.4
).

Theorem 3.13

(Smooth tameness of the principal part nonlinear operator)
.

There exists a
\rho_{\mathrm{prin}}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that

(3.3.28)

\Psi\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{1,1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(B_{\mathbb{X}^{1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho_{\mathrm{prin}})\times\mathbb{R}^{+},\boldsymbol{\mathbb{X}}\times\mathbb{R};\boldsymbol{\mathbb{Y}}).

Proof.

We set
\rho_{\mathrm{prin}}=\min\{{\rho_{\mathrm{cont}},\rho_{\mathrm{mome}},\rho_{\mathrm{dyna}}}\}
and apply Propositions
3.10
,
3.11
, and
3.12
.
∎

The remainder of this subsection is devoted to the study of the auxiliary piece
\Phi
of the nonlinear operator
\overline{\Psi}
. The next result handles the
\Phi_{2}
piece, which we recall is defined in (
3.3.9
).

Proposition 3.14

(Smooth tameness of the momentum equation, 2)
.

There exists a
\rho_{\mathrm{bulk}}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
,

(3.3.29)

\Phi_{2}\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{m}_{0,2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(B_{\mathbb{X}^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho_{\mathrm{bulk}})\times\mathbb{W}_{m+2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor},\{{\mathbb{X}^{s}\times\mathbb{W}_{m+s}}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}};\mathbf{H}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})).

Proof.

Thanks to the smooth tameness of superposition multipliers, Lemma
3.6
, the decomposition of
\sigma_{q,\eta}=\mathcal{N}^{(1)}_{H^{-1}}(q,\eta)+\mathcal{N}^{(2)}_{H^{-1}}(\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta)+\varrho
from Lemma
3.8
, and the result on composition of smooth tame maps, Lemma
2.8
, we see that it is sufficient to prove that the nonlinear operator

(3.3.30)

\Lambda:H^{m+2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})\times B_{\mathcal{H}^{9/2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(\Sigma)}(0,\rho)\to H^{0}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})\text{ given by }\Lambda(\mathcal{I},\eta)=\mathcal{I}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}

is, for some
\rho>0
, well-defined and
{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{m}_{0,2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}
for the scales
\{{H^{m+s}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\mathcal{H}^{5/2+s}(\Sigma)}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\{{H^{s}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})}\}
. For this we let
\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}
and
\mathfrak{R}_{\Omega}
denote the Stein extension (see Definition
A.2
) and the restriction operators for
\Omega
, respectively, and note that we have the equivalent formula

(3.3.31)

\Lambda(\mathcal{I},\eta)=\mathfrak{R}_{\Omega}\mathcal{I}(\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}+\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\mathcal{E}(\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta+\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{H}}\eta)).

Hence, according to Lemma
D.2
and properties of the maps
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}
(see Lemma
A.1
and Example
2.16
), there exists a
\rho_{\mathrm{comp}}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
(depending only on the dimension and
\Omega
) such that whenever
\rho\leqslant\rho_{\mathrm{comp}}
the map (
3.3.30
) is
{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{m}_{0,2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}
with respect to the aforementioned Banach scales.
∎

The penultimate result of this subsection considers the
\Phi_{3}
piece of
\overline{\Psi}
, which is defined in (
3.3.10
).

Proposition 3.15

(Smooth tameness of the dynamic boundary condition, 2)
.

For
\rho_{\mathrm{surf}}=\rho_{\mathrm{bulk}}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, where the latter is from Proposition
3.14
, we have that for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
,

(3.3.32)

\Phi_{3}\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{m}_{0,2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(B_{\mathbb{X}^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho_{\mathrm{surf}})\times\mathbb{W}_{m+2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor},\{{\mathbb{X}^{s}\times\mathbb{W}_{m+s}}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}};\{{H^{1/2+s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}^{n})}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}).

Proof.

We argue is in the proof of Proposition
3.14
and reduce to studying the map

(3.3.33)

\widetilde{\Lambda}:H^{m+2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})\times B_{\mathcal{H}^{9/2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(\Sigma)}(0,\rho)\to H^{0}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}^{n})

given by
\widetilde{\Lambda}(\mathcal{T},\eta)=\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{T}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\eta})
. We then use the equivalent formula

(3.3.34)

\widetilde{\Lambda}(\mathcal{T},\eta)=\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma}\mathfrak{R}_{\Omega}\mathcal{T}(\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}+\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\mathcal{E}(\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta+\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{H}}\eta)),

and conclude as before.
∎

Remark 3.16
.

The parameter
m\in\mathbb{N}
appearing in Propositions
3.14
and
3.15
(and subsequently in Theorem
3.17
) gives the data terms
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F})

m
-extra derivatives to ensure that the composition type nonlinearities of
\Phi_{2}
and
\Phi_{3}
are
C^{m}
into the correct codomain space. One sees that this extra regularity is necessary upon inspection of the derivative formulae (
D.1.6
) and (
D.1.7
).

At last, we are ready to deduce the smooth tameness of the nonlinear operator
\overline{\Psi}
, which we recall is defined in (
3.3.1
). In the following statement
\mathrm{WD}
is an acronym for ‘well-defined’.

Theorem 3.17

(Smooth tameness of the nonlinear operator)
.

There exists a
\rho_{\mathrm{WD}}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that the following hold.

(1)

For
\mathbb{N}\ni s\geqslant 2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor
and
(q,u,\eta)\in\mathbb{X}^{s}\cap B_{\mathbb{X}^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho_{\mathrm{WD}})
we have that the flattening map
\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}
defined in (
1.4.4
) is a smooth diffeomorphism from
\Omega
to
\Omega[\eta]
that extends to a
C^{s+2-\lfloor n/2\rfloor}
diffeomorphism from
\overline{\Omega}
to
\overline{\Omega[\eta]}
.

(2)

For every
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\overline{\Psi}\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{m}_{1,2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(B_{\mathbb{X}^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho_{\mathrm{WD}})\times\mathbb{W}_{m+1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\times\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathbf{O}_{m};\mathbf{P}_{m})
, where we have denoted
\mathbf{O}_{m}=\{{\mathbb{X}^{s}\times\mathbb{W}_{m-1+s}\times\mathbb{R}}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\mathbf{P}_{m}=\{{\mathbb{Y}^{s}\times\mathbb{W}_{m+s-1}\times\mathbb{R}}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
.

Proof.

We set
\rho_{\mathrm{WD}}=\min\{{\rho_{\mathrm{prin}},\rho_{\mathrm{bulk}},\rho_{\mathrm{surf}}}\}
and apply Theorem
3.13
and Propositions
3.14
and
3.15
. This immediately gives the second item. For the first item, we note that Proposition
3.11
guarantees that
J_{\eta}>0
and
J_{\eta},1/J_{\eta}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
, which means that
\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}
is a continuous bijection from
\overline{\Omega}
to
\overline{\Omega[\eta]}
. On the other hand, the identity
\partial_{n}(\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}\cdot e_{n})=J_{\eta}=\det(\nabla\mathfrak{F}_{\eta})
and the inverse function theorem guarantee that
\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}:\Omega\to\Omega[\eta]
is a smooth diffeomorphism. The regularity of
\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}:\overline{\Omega}\to\overline{\Omega[\eta]}
and its inverse now follow from Sobolev embeddings and Proposition
B.1
.
∎

Remark 3.18
.

In the case
m=2
, the second item of Theorem
3.17
is stating that
\overline{\Psi}\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{2}_{1,2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}
for the Banach scales
\boldsymbol{\mathbb{E}}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathbb{F}}
, which are introduced in (
3.1.10
).

3.4.
Derivative splitting

We now turn our attention to the study of the derivative of the map
\overline{\Psi}
from (
3.3.1
). When written in full, it is rather complicated, so our focus now is to identify a principal part and handle the remainder terms. Let

(3.4.1)

(q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0})\in B_{\mathbb{X}^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho_{\mathrm{WD}}),\quad(\mathcal{T}_{0},\mathcal{G}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0})\in\mathbb{W}_{3+\lfloor n/2\rfloor},

(3.4.2)

(q,u,\eta)\in\mathbb{X}^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor},\quad(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F})\in\mathbb{W}_{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor},\quad\gamma_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{+},\;\gamma\in\mathbb{R},

and write the
6-
tuple

(3.4.3)

\theta_{0}=(q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0},\mathcal{T}_{0},\mathcal{G}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0}).

The derivative of the map
\overline{\Psi}
has the formula

(3.4.4)

D\overline{\Psi}(\theta_{0},\gamma_{0})(q,u,\eta,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F},\gamma)=\big(D\Psi(q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0},\gamma_{0})(q,u,\eta,\gamma)+D\Phi(\theta_{0})(q,u,\eta,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F}),\mathcal{T},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F},\gamma\big).

We will decompose the above into a principal part,
A
, and remainder terms,
P
,
Q
, and
R
(these symbols will come equipped with various adornments, but for brevity we will often refer to them without these in the main text). The derivative
D\Psi
is split as follows:

(3.4.5)

D\Psi(w_{0},\gamma_{0})(q,u,\eta,\gamma)=\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{A}(q,u,\eta)+\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{P}(q,u,\eta,\gamma),

where for brevity we define the triple

(3.4.6)

w_{0}=(q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}).

The
A
piece is meant to be as close as possible to
D\Psi(0,0,0,\gamma_{0})
, but to retain the entirety of the structure responsible for derivative loss in the continuity equation. To that end, we set

(3.4.7)

\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{A}(q,u,\eta)=\big(\overset{w_{0}}{A^{1}}(q,u,\eta),\overset{\gamma_{0}}{A^{2}}(q,u,\eta),\overset{\gamma_{0}}{A^{3}}(q,u,\eta)\big),

where

(3.4.8)

\overset{w_{0}}{A^{1}}(q,u,\eta)=\nabla\cdot(\varrho u)+\nabla\cdot(v_{w_{0}}(q+\mathfrak{g}\eta)),

(3.4.9)

\overset{\gamma_{0}}{A^{2}}(q,u,\eta)=D\Psi_{2}(0,0,0,\gamma_{0})(q,u,\eta,0)=-\gamma_{0}^{2}\varrho\partial_{1}u+\varrho\nabla(q+\mathfrak{g}\eta)-\gamma_{0}\nabla\cdot\mathbb{S}^{\varrho}u,

and

(3.4.10)

\overset{\gamma_{0}}{A^{3}}(q,u,\eta)=D\Psi_{3}(0,0,0,\gamma_{0})(q,u,\eta,0)=-(\varrho q-\gamma_{0}\mathbb{S}^{\varrho}u)e_{n}-\varsigma\Delta_{\|}\eta e_{n}.

Here
v_{w_{0}}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the vector field

(3.4.11)

v_{w_{0}}=\mathcal{M}_{(H^{-1})^{\prime}}(q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0})=(H^{-1})^{\prime}(-\mathfrak{g}\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\cdot e_{n}+q_{0}+\mathfrak{g}(I-\mathcal{E})\eta_{0})(u_{0}-M_{\eta_{0}}e_{1}),

where the
\mathcal{M}
notation is from Lemma
3.9
and
(H^{-1})^{\prime}
refers to the derivative of the inverse enthalpy (see equation (
1.1.10
)). Note that since
\varrho(y)=H^{-1}(-\mathfrak{g}y)
for
y\in[0,b]
we have that

(3.4.12)

v_{0}=-(H^{-1})^{\prime}(-\mathfrak{g}\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\cdot e_{n})e_{1}=\mathfrak{g}^{-1}\varrho^{\prime}e_{1}.

The
P
piece is, of course, the remainder of the derivative of
\Psi
and has the formula

(3.4.13)

\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{P}(q,u,\eta,\gamma)=\big(\overset{w_{0}}{P^{1}}(q,u,\eta),\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{P^{2}}(q,u,\eta,\gamma),\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{P^{3}}(q,u,\eta,\gamma)\big)

where

(3.4.14)

\overset{w_{0}}{P^{1}}(q,u,\eta)=\nabla\cdot((\sigma_{q_{0},\eta_{0}}-\varrho)(u-\dot{M}[\eta]e_{1}))-\mathfrak{g}\nabla\cdot((v_{w_{0}}-\varrho^{\prime}e_{1}/\mathfrak{g})\mathcal{E}\eta)-\nabla\cdot(\varrho\dot{M}[\eta]e_{1})-\nabla\cdot(\varrho^{\prime}\mathcal{E}\eta e_{1}),

\dot{M}[\eta]:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is the matrix field given by

(3.4.15)

\dot{M}[\eta]=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{n}\mathcal{E}\eta I_{(n-1)\times(n-1)}&0_{(n-1)\times 1}\\
-\mathcal{E}(\nabla_{\|}\eta)&0\end{pmatrix},

and

(3.4.16)

\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{P^{j}}(q,u,\eta,\gamma)=(D\Psi_{j}(w_{0},\gamma_{0})-D\Psi_{j}(0,0,0,\gamma_{0}))(q,u,\eta,0)+D\Psi_{j}(w_{0},\gamma_{0})(0,0,0,\gamma)

for
j\in\{{2,3}\}
.

Before we define a similar decomposition of the
D\Phi
piece of
D\overline{\Psi}
, we will prove some basic properties about the
A+P
decomposition. First, we have the following lemma which, aside from the final item, is a reprise of Lemma
3.9
.

Lemma 3.19

(Properties of the derivative loss vector field)
.

Let
0<\rho\leqslant\rho_{\mathrm{WD}}
, where the latter is defined in Theorem
3.17
, and
w_{0}=(q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0})\in B_{\mathbb{X}^{1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho)\cap\mathbb{X}^{\infty}
. Define the vector field
v_{w_{0}}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
as in (
3.4.11
). There exists a decomposition

(3.4.17)

v_{w_{0}}=\mathfrak{g}^{-1}\varrho^{\prime}e_{1}+v^{(1)}_{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}+v^{(2)}_{\eta_{0}}

such that the following hold.

(1)

The vector field
v^{(1)}_{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}
has vanishing normal trace,
\mathrm{Tr}_{\partial\Omega}(v^{(1)}_{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}\cdot e_{n})=0
, satisfies the inclusion
v^{(1)}_{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}\in H^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and obeys the estimates

(3.4.18)

\lVert v_{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}^{(1)}\rVert_{H^{1+s}}\lesssim\begin{cases}\rho&\text{if }s=1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor,\\
\lVert q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}&\text{if }s>1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor.\end{cases}

(2)

The vector field
v^{(2)}_{\eta_{0}}
is parallel to
e_{1}
, satisfies the inclusion

(3.4.19)

v^{(2)}_{\eta_{0}}\cdot e_{1}\in W^{\infty,\infty}((0,b);\mathcal{H}^{0}_{(r_{n-1})}(\Sigma))\hookrightarrow W^{\infty,\infty}(\Omega),

and obeys the estimates

(3.4.20)

\lVert v^{(2)}_{\eta_{0}}\cdot e_{1}\rVert_{W^{s,\infty}((0,b);\mathcal{H}^{0}_{(r_{n-1})}(\Sigma))}\lesssim\rho\text{ for }s\geqslant 1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor,

where
r_{n-1}\in\mathbb{N}
is from the second item of Proposition
B.2
with
d=n-1
.

(3)

We have the inclusion
\nabla\cdot v_{w_{0}}\in H^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
along with the estimates

(3.4.21)

\lVert\nabla\cdot v_{w_{0}}\rVert_{H^{s}}\lesssim\begin{cases}\rho&\text{if }s=1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor,\\
\lVert q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}&\text{if }s>1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor.\end{cases}

(4)

If
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
q\in H^{1+s}(\Omega)
, and
\eta\in\mathcal{H}^{1+s}(\Sigma)
, then we have that
\nabla\cdot(v_{w_{0}}(q+\mathfrak{g}\eta))\in\hat{H}^{1+s}(\Omega)
, where the latter space is defined in (
C.1.1
) (see also Remark
3.1
), as well as the estimates

(3.4.22)

\lVert\nabla\cdot(v_{w_{0}}(q+\mathfrak{g}\eta))\rVert_{\hat{H}^{s}}\lesssim\lVert q,\eta\rVert_{H^{1+s}\times\mathcal{H}^{1+s}}+\begin{cases}0&\text{if }s\leqslant\lfloor n/2\rfloor,\\
\langle\lVert q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}\rangle\lVert q,\eta\rVert_{H^{1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\times\mathcal{H}^{1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}&\text{if }\lfloor n/2\rfloor<s.\end{cases}

In the above, the implicit constants depend on the dimension, the physical parameters,
s
, and
\rho_{\mathrm{WD}}
.

Proof.

We apply Lemma
3.9
to obtain identity (
3.4.17
) with
\varrho^{\prime}e_{1}/\mathfrak{g}=\mathcal{M}_{(H^{-1})^{\prime}}(0,0,0)
,
v^{(1)}_{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}=\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{(H^{-1})^{\prime}}(q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0})
, and
v^{(2)}_{\eta_{0}}=e_{1}\cdot\mathcal{M}^{(2)}_{(H^{-1})^{\prime}}(\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta_{0})e_{1}
. The qualitative smoothness assertions in the first, second, and third items now follow immediately the lemma. We will prove the quantitative bounds via the fundamental theorem of calculus. Since
\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{(H^{-1})^{\prime}}(0,0,0)=0
, we have that

(3.4.23)

v^{(1)}_{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}=\int_{0}^{1}D\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{(H^{-1})^{\prime}}(tq_{0},tu_{0},t\eta_{0})(q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0})\;\mathrm{d}t,

and hence, by using the strong smooth tameness assertions in Lemma
3.9
and the derivative estimates on smooth tame maps from Lemma
2.7
, we find that for
s\geqslant 1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor
,

(3.4.24)

\lVert v^{(1)}_{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}\rVert_{H^{1+s}}\leqslant\int_{0}^{1}\lVert D\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{(H^{-1})^{\prime}}(tq_{0},tu_{0},t\eta_{0})[q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}]\rVert_{H^{1+s}}\;\mathrm{d}t\lesssim\lVert q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}.

The same technique proves the quantitative bounds asserted in the second item.

Next, we justify the divergence estimates of the third item. By using (
3.4.17
), we compute that

(3.4.25)

\nabla\cdot v_{w_{0}}=\nabla\cdot v^{(1)}_{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}+\partial_{1}(v^{(2)}_{\eta_{0}}\cdot e_{1}).

From this identity, the quantitative estimates of the first and second items, and the properties of band limited anisotropic Sobolev spaces from Proposition
B.1
, we deduce that for
s\geqslant 1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor
,

(3.4.26)

\lVert\nabla\cdot v^{(1)}_{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}\rVert_{H^{s}}\lesssim\lVert v^{(1)}_{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}\rVert_{H^{1+s}}\lesssim\lVert q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}

and

(3.4.27)

\lVert\partial_{1}(v^{(2)}_{\eta_{0}}\cdot e_{1})\rVert_{H^{1+s}}\lesssim\lVert v^{(2)}_{\eta_{0}}\cdot e_{1}\rVert_{W^{1+s,\infty}((0,b);\mathcal{H}^{0}_{(r_{n-1})}(\Sigma))}\lesssim\rho.

These complete the proof of the third item.

Finally, to prove the fourth item we first note that (
3.4.17
) yields the formula

(3.4.28)

\nabla\cdot(v_{w_{0}}(q+\mathfrak{g}\eta))=\nabla\cdot(v_{w_{0}}(q+\mathfrak{g}\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{H}}\eta))+\mathfrak{g}\nabla\cdot(v_{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}^{(1)}\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta)\\
+\mathfrak{g}\partial_{1}(e_{1}\cdot v^{(2)}_{\eta_{0}}\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta)+\varrho^{\prime}\partial_{1}\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta=\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{II}+\mathbf{III}+\mathbf{IV}.

For
\mathbf{I}
we use Proposition
B.1
, the first and second items above, and Corollary
D.7
to estimate

(3.4.29)

\lVert\mathbf{I}\rVert_{H^{s}}\lesssim\lVert v_{w_{0}}(q+\mathfrak{g}\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{H}}\eta)\rVert_{H^{1+s}}\leqslant\lVert v^{(1)}_{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}(q+\mathfrak{g}\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{H}}\eta)\rVert_{H^{1+s}}+\lVert(v^{(2)}_{\eta_{0}}+\varrho^{\prime}e_{1}/\mathfrak{g})(q+\mathfrak{g}\eta_{\mathrm{H}})\rVert_{H^{1+s}}\\
\lesssim\lVert q,\eta\rVert_{H^{1+s}\times\mathcal{H}^{1+s}}+\begin{cases}0&\text{if }s\leqslant\lfloor n/2\rfloor,\\
\langle\lVert q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}\rangle\lVert q,\eta\rVert_{H^{1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\times\mathcal{H}^{1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}&\text{if }\lfloor n/2\rfloor<s.\end{cases}

On the other hand, since
\mathrm{Tr}_{\partial\Omega}(v_{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}\cdot e_{n})=0
, we use estimate (
B.2.12
) from Proposition
B.10
to bound

(3.4.30)

\Big[{\int_{0}^{b}\mathbf{I}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y}\Big]_{\dot{H}^{-1}}\lesssim\lVert v_{w_{0}}(q+\mathfrak{g}\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{H}}\eta)\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert q,\eta\rVert_{L^{2}\times\mathcal{H}^{0}}.

From (
3.4.29
) and (
3.4.30
) we deduce that

(3.4.31)

\lVert\mathbf{I}\rVert_{\hat{H}^{s}}\lesssim\lVert q,\eta\rVert_{H^{1+s}\times\mathcal{H}^{1+s}}+\begin{cases}0&\text{if }s\leqslant\lfloor n/2\rfloor,\\
\langle\lVert q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}\rangle\lVert q,\eta\rVert_{H^{1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\times\mathcal{H}^{1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}&\text{if }\lfloor n/2\rfloor<s.\end{cases}

For
\mathbf{II}
, we use the first item along with Proposition
B.1
and Corollary
D.7
again to estimate

(3.4.32)

\lVert\mathbf{II}\rVert_{H^{s}}\lesssim\lVert v^{(1)}_{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta\rVert_{W^{1+s,\infty}}+\lVert v^{(1)}_{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}\rVert_{H^{1+s}}\lVert\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\lVert q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}\lVert\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}.

On the other hand, since
\mathrm{Tr}_{\partial\Omega}(v_{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}^{(1)}\cdot e_{n})=0
, we have

(3.4.33)

\Big[{\int_{0}^{b}\mathbf{II}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y}\Big]_{\dot{H^{-1}}}\lesssim\lVert v^{(1)}_{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}.

From (
3.4.32
) and (
3.4.33
) we deduce that
\lVert\mathbf{II}\rVert_{\hat{H}^{s}}
is controlled by the right hand side of (
3.4.22
). We now estimate
\mathbf{III}
, using the second item and the algebraic properties of the anisotropic Sobolev spaces from Proposition
B.2
to get

(3.4.34)

\lVert\mathbf{III}\rVert_{H^{s}}\lesssim\lVert e_{1}\cdot v^{(2)}_{\eta_{0}}\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta\rVert_{W^{s,\infty}((0,b);\mathcal{H}^{0}_{(r_{n-1}+1)}(\Sigma))}\lesssim\lVert e_{1}\cdot v^{(2)}_{\eta_{0}}\rVert_{W^{s,\infty}\mathcal{H}^{0}_{(r_{n-1})}}\lVert\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}\lesssim\lVert\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}

and

(3.4.35)

\Big[{\int_{0}^{b}\mathbf{III}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y}\Big]_{\dot{H}^{-1}}\leqslant\Big\lVert\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta\int_{0}^{b}e_{1}\cdot v^{(2)}_{\eta_{0}}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y\Big\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}\lesssim\int_{0}^{b}\lVert e_{1}\cdot v^{(2)}_{\eta_{0}}(\cdot,y)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}\;\mathrm{d}y\cdot\lVert\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}\lesssim\lVert\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}.

The bounds (
3.4.34
) and (
3.4.35
) imply that
\lVert\mathbf{III}\rVert_{\hat{H}^{s}}
is controlled by the right hand side of (
3.4.22
). The norm
\lVert\mathbf{IV}\rVert_{\hat{H}^{s}}
is trivially controlled by the same quantity thanks to (
B.1.25
). The proof of the fourth item is then complete upon synthesizing these bounds on
\mathbf{I}
,
\mathbf{II}
,
\mathbf{III}
, and
\mathbf{IV}
.
∎

We now introduce a new scale of adapted spaces pertinent to the analysis of the
A
piece of
D\Psi
. As we will see, these are the natural domains on which
A
is an isomorphism of Banach spaces. To define the spaces, we simply build an extra condition into the spaces
\{{\mathbb{X}^{s}}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
, which we recall were defined in (
3.1.3
), to create a new family in which the effect of the derivative loss is mitigated. What is notable is that these domains depend on the background point
w_{0}=(q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0})
in a non-trivial way. For
s\in\{{-1,0}\}\cup\mathbb{R}^{+}
and
(q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0})
as in the hypotheses of Lemma
3.19
we define the space

(3.4.36)

\overset{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}{\mathbb{X}^{s}}=\{{(q,u,\eta)\in\mathbb{X}^{s}\;:\;\nabla\cdot(v_{w_{0}}q)\in H^{1+s}(\Omega)}\}.

and equip it with the norm

(3.4.37)

\lVert q,u,\eta\rVert_{\overset{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}{\mathbb{X}^{s}}}=\sqrt{\lVert q,u,\eta\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}^{2}+\lVert\nabla\cdot(v_{w_{0}}q)\rVert_{H^{1+s}}^{2}}.

Remark 3.20
.

A simple modification of the proof of the first item of Proposition
B.7
reveals that the spaces
\overset{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}{\mathbb{X}^{s}}
are Hilbert.

The following result captures that the
A+P
decomposition of
D\Psi
from (
3.4.5
) is such that
A
contains all of the derivative loss and
P
is a small correction term without derivative loss. Note that the conclusions in what follows are stronger than what can be deduced from a direct application of the smooth-tameness verification result, namely Theorem
3.13
.

Proposition 3.21

(Properties of the
A+P
decomposition of
D\Psi
)
.

Let
\rho\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and
w_{0}=(q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0})
be as in the hypotheses of Lemma
3.19
and let
I\Subset\mathbb{R}^{+}
be an interval with
\gamma_{0}\in I
. The following hold.

(1)

For every
s\in\mathbb{N}
, the map
\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{A}:\overset{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}{\mathbb{X}^{s}}\to\mathbb{Y}^{s}
is well-defined and continuous.

(2)

For every
\mathbb{N}\ni s\geqslant 1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor
, the map
\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{P}:\mathbb{X}^{s}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{Y}^{s}
is well-defined, continuous, and obeys the estimates

(3.4.38)

\big\lVert\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{P}(q,u,\eta,\gamma)\big\rVert_{\mathbb{Y}^{s}}\lesssim\rho\lVert q,u,\eta\rVert_{\mathbb{X}^{s}}+\lVert q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{1+s}}\lVert q,u,\eta,\gamma\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\times\mathbb{R}}.

The implicit constants depend on the dimension, the physical parameters,
s
,
\rho_{\mathrm{WD}}
, and
I
.

Proof.

Upon inspecting the components of
\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{A}
in (
3.4.7
), we see that the second and third are trivially well-defined and continuous, so to prove the first item we heed to Remark
3.1
and reduce to showing that the map

(3.4.39)

\overset{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}{\mathbb{X}^{s}}\ni(q,u,\eta)\mapsto\overset{w_{0}}{A^{1}}(q,u,\eta)\in\hat{H}^{1+s}(\Omega)

is well-defined and continuous. As a consequence of the fourth item of Lemma
3.19
, the identity

(3.4.40)

\int_{0}^{b}\nabla\cdot(\varrho u)(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y=(\nabla_{\|},0)\cdot\int_{0}^{b}(\varrho u)(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y-\varrho(b)\partial_{1}\eta

(which is true by virtue of the boundary condition build into the
\mathbb{X}^{s}
spaces, as defined in (
3.1.3
)), and the norm equivalence (
B.1.25
) from Proposition
B.4
, we see that the operator in (
3.4.39
) continuously maps into
\hat{H}^{0}(\Omega)
. On the other hand, by employing the first, second, and third items of Lemma
3.19
, the equality

(3.4.41)

\nabla\cdot(v_{w_{0}}(q+\mathfrak{g}\eta))=\mathfrak{g}\nabla\cdot(v_{w_{0}}\eta)+\nabla\cdot(v_{w_{0}}q),

and the definition of the norm in (
3.4.37
), we readily deduce that
\overset{w_{0}}{A^{1}}
maps boundedly into
H^{1+s}(\Omega)
. This completes the proof of the first item.

We now turn to the proof of the second item. Recall from (
3.4.13
) that
\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{P}
has components
\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{P^{i}}
for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
. We divide the remainder of the proof into three steps, each of which handles one of these components.

Step 1
: Estimates on
\overset{w_{0}}{P^{1}}
. We first aim to exploit some hidden cancellation by observing that the sum of the final two terms in (
3.4.14
) vanishes. Indeed, since
\dot{M}[\eta]e_{1}=\partial_{n}\mathcal{E}\eta e_{1}-\partial_{1}\mathcal{E}\eta e_{n}
we have that
\nabla\cdot(\dot{M}[\eta]e_{1})=0
, and therefore

(3.4.42)

-\nabla\cdot(\varrho^{\prime}\mathcal{E}\eta e_{1})-\nabla\cdot(\varrho\dot{M}[\eta]e_{1})=-\varrho^{\prime}\partial_{1}\mathcal{E}\eta-\varrho^{\prime}(\partial_{n}\mathcal{E}\eta e_{1}-\partial_{1}\mathcal{E}\eta e_{n})\cdot e_{n}=0.

Thus, we have the more accessible formula

(3.4.43)

\overset{w_{0}}{P^{1}}(u,\eta)=\nabla\cdot((\sigma_{q_{0},\eta_{0}}-\varrho)(u-\dot{M}[\eta]e_{1}))-\mathfrak{g}\nabla\cdot((v_{w_{0}}-\varrho^{\prime}e_{1}/\mathfrak{g})\mathcal{E}\eta)=\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{II},

and we will deal with
\mathbf{I}
and
\mathbf{II}
separately.

The term
\mathbf{I}
is handled with the Taylor expansion trick of Lemma
3.8
, i.e. we decompose
\sigma_{q_{0},\eta_{0}}-\varrho=\sigma^{(1)}_{q_{0},\eta_{0}}+\sigma^{(2)}_{\eta_{0}}
, where

(3.4.44)

\sigma^{(1)}_{q_{0},\eta_{0}}=\mathcal{N}^{(1)}_{H^{-1}}(q_{0},\eta_{0})\text{ and }\sigma^{(2)}_{\eta_{0}}=\mathcal{N}^{(2)}_{H^{-1}}(\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta).

Employing the above decomposition and recalling (
3.4.15
), we may further rewrite

(3.4.45)

\mathbf{I}=\nabla\cdot((\sigma_{q_{0},\eta_{0}}-\varrho)(u+\partial_{1}\mathcal{E}\eta e_{n})-\sigma^{(1)}_{q_{0},\eta_{0}}\partial_{n}\mathcal{E}\eta e_{1}-\sigma^{(2)}_{\eta_{0}}\partial_{n}\mathcal{E}_{0}\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{H}}\eta e_{1})-\partial_{1}(\sigma^{(2)}_{\eta_{0}}\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta)/b=\nabla\cdot\mathbf{I}_{1}+\partial_{1}\mathbf{I}_{2}.

To handle
\nabla\cdot\mathbf{I}_{1}
we will first derive a Sobolev estimate for
\mathbf{I}_{1}
. Indeed, thanks to multiple applications of Corollary
D.7
and Lemma
A.1
, we are free to bound, for any
s\in\mathbb{N}
,

(3.4.46)

\lVert\mathbf{I}_{1}\rVert_{H^{s}}\lesssim\lVert\sigma_{q_{0},\eta_{0}}^{(1)},\sigma_{\eta_{0}}^{(2)}\rVert_{H^{1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\times W^{1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor,\infty}}\lVert u,\eta\rVert_{H^{s}\times\mathcal{H}^{1/2+s}}\\
+\begin{cases}0&\text{if }s\leqslant 1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor,\\
\lVert\sigma^{(1)}_{q_{0},\eta_{0}},\sigma^{(2)}_{\eta_{0}}\rVert_{H^{s}\times W^{s,\infty}}\lVert u,\eta\rVert_{H^{1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\times\mathcal{H}^{3/2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}&\text{if }1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor<s.\end{cases}

Then we use Lemma
3.8
combined with the fundamental theorem of calculus as in the first part of the proof of Lemma
3.19
to acquire the bounds

(3.4.47)

\lVert\sigma^{(1)}_{q_{0},\eta_{0}},\sigma^{(2)}_{\eta_{0}}\rVert_{H^{s}\times W^{s,\infty}}\lesssim\begin{cases}\lVert q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}&\text{if }s\leqslant 2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor,\\
\lVert q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s-1}}&\text{if }2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor<s.\end{cases}

Upon synthesizing the bounds (
3.4.46
) and (
3.4.47
), we find that

(3.4.48)

\lVert\mathbf{I}_{1}\rVert_{H^{s}}\lesssim\rho\lVert q,u,\eta\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s-2}}+\begin{cases}0&\text{if }s\leqslant 2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor,\\
\lVert q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s-1}}\lVert q,u,\eta\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{\lfloor n/2\rfloor-1}}&\text{if }2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor<s,\end{cases}

which is the aforementioned Sobolev estimate. Then, in light of (
3.4.48
), the fact that
\mathrm{Tr}_{\partial\Omega}(\mathbf{I}_{1}\cdot e_{n})=0
, and divergence - normal trace compatibility estimates from Proposition
C.1
, we arrive at the bound

(3.4.49)

\lVert\nabla\cdot\mathbf{I}_{1}\rVert_{\hat{H}^{1+s}}\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{I}_{1}\rVert_{H^{2+s}}\lesssim\rho\lVert q,u,\eta\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}+\begin{cases}0&\text{if }s\leqslant\lfloor n/2\rfloor,\\
\lVert q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{1+s}}\lVert q,u,\eta\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{\lfloor n/2\rfloor-1}}&\text{if }\lfloor n/2\rfloor<s.\end{cases}

Having dispatched
\mathbf{I}_{1}
, we turn our attention to the
\mathbf{I}_{2}
term in (
3.4.45
). The product
\sigma^{(2)}_{\eta_{0}}\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta
is handled via Lemma
3.8
and the algebra properties of band limited members of
\mathcal{H}^{0}(\Sigma)
enumerated in Proposition
B.2
, applied to
\sigma^{(2)}_{\eta_{0}}(\cdot,y)\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta
for
y\in[0,b]
:

(3.4.50)

\lVert\partial_{1}\mathbf{I}_{2}\rVert_{\hat{H}^{1+s}}\lesssim\Big[{\partial_{1}\Big(\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta\int_{0}^{b}\sigma^{(2)}_{\eta_{0}}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y\Big)}\Big]_{\dot{H}^{-1}}+\max_{0\leqslant j\leqslant 1+\upnu}\sup_{y\in[0,b]}\lVert\partial_{1}\partial_{n}^{j}\mathbf{I}_{2}(\cdot,y)\rVert_{H^{1+s}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\\
\lesssim\Big\lVert\int_{0}^{b}\sigma^{(2)}_{\eta_{0}}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y\Big\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}\lVert\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+\max_{0\leqslant j\leqslant 1+s}\sup_{y\in[0,b]}[{\partial_{1}\partial_{n}^{j}(\sigma^{(2)}_{\eta_{0}}(\cdot,y)\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta)}]_{\dot{H}^{-1}}\\
\lesssim\max_{0\leqslant j\leqslant 1+s}\sup_{y\in[0,b]}\lVert\partial_{n}^{j}\sigma^{(2)}_{\eta_{0}}(\cdot,y)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}\lVert\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}\lesssim\rho\lVert\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{0}},

where in deducing the final inequality we again use the tame mapping properties of
\sigma^{(2)}_{\eta_{0}}
from Lemma
3.8
, combined with the fundamental theorem of calculus as before. This completes the handling of
\mathbf{I}_{2}
, and hence of
\mathbf{I}
.

Next, we consider
\mathbf{II}
from equation (
3.4.43
). Thanks to the decomposition of
v_{w_{0}}
from Lemma
3.19
,
\mathbf{II}
has the further decomposition

(3.4.51)

\mathbf{II}=-\mathfrak{g}\nabla\cdot((v^{(1)}_{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}+v^{(2)}_{\eta_{0}})\mathcal{E}_{0}\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{H}}\eta+v^{(1)}_{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}\mathcal{E}\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta)-\mathfrak{g}\partial_{1}(\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\cdot e_{n}v^{(2)}_{\eta_{0}}\cdot e_{1}\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta)/b=\nabla\cdot\mathbf{II}_{1}+\partial_{1}\mathbf{II}_{2}.

As in the proof of (
3.4.48
), we use Corollary
D.7
and the mapping properties of
v^{(1)}_{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}
and
v^{(2)}_{\eta_{0}}
from Lemma
3.19
to see that for any
s\in\mathbb{N}
,

(3.4.52)

\lVert\mathbf{II}_{1}\rVert_{H^{s}}\lesssim\lVert v^{(1)}_{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}},v^{(2)}_{\eta_{0}}\rVert_{H^{1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\times W^{1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor,\infty}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{s-1/2}}\\
+\begin{cases}0&\text{if }s\leqslant 1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor,\\
\lVert v^{(1)}_{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}},v^{(2)}_{\eta_{0}}\rVert_{H^{s}\times W^{s,\infty}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{1/2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}&\text{if }1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor<s,\end{cases}\\
\lesssim\rho\lVert q,u,\eta\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s-3}}+\begin{cases}0&\text{if }s\leqslant 2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor,\\
\lVert q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s-1}}\lVert q,u,\eta\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{\lfloor n/2\rfloor-2}}&\text{if }2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor<s.\end{cases}

In turn, (
3.4.52
), the fact that
\mathrm{Tr}_{\partial\Omega}(\mathbf{II}_{1}\cdot e_{n})=0
, and Proposition
C.1
provide the bound

(3.4.53)

\lVert\nabla\cdot\mathbf{II}_{1}\rVert_{H^{1+s}}\lesssim\lVert\mathbf{II}_{1}\rVert_{H^{2+s}}\lesssim\rho\lVert q,u,\eta\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s-1}}+\begin{cases}0&\text{if }\upnu\leqslant\lfloor n/2\rfloor,\\
\lVert q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{1+\upnu}}\lVert q,u,\eta\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{\lfloor n/2\rfloor-1}}&\text{if }\lfloor n/2\rfloor<\upnu.\end{cases}

For
\mathbf{II}_{2}
, we argue as in (
3.4.50
), estimating the product
(\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\cdot e_{n})(v^{(2)}_{\eta_{0}}\cdot e_{1})\eta_{\mathrm{L}}
by invoking the second item from Lemma
3.19
as well as the algebra properties of band-limited members of
\mathcal{H}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
from Proposition
B.2
; this results in the bound

(3.4.54)

\lVert\partial_{1}\mathbf{II}_{2}\rVert_{\hat{H}^{1+s}}\lesssim\rho\lVert\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}.

Finally, we synthesize (
3.4.43
), (
3.4.45
), (
3.4.49
), (
3.4.50
), (
3.4.51
), (
3.4.52
), and (
3.4.54
) to get the
\overset{w_{0}}{P^{1}}
estimate

(3.4.55)

\big\lVert\overset{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}{P^{1}}(u,\eta)\big\rVert_{\hat{H}^{1+s}}\lesssim\rho\lVert q,u,\eta\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}+\begin{cases}0&\text{if }s\leqslant\lfloor n/2\rfloor,\\
\lVert q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{1+s}}\lVert q,u,\eta\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{\lfloor n/2\rfloor-1}}&\text{if }\lfloor n/2\rfloor<s.\end{cases}

Step 2
: Estimates on
\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{P^{2}}
. This is simpler than the previous step, as we can use the tame calculus conclusions of Proposition
3.11
. Recall that

(3.4.56)

\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{P^{2}}(q,u,\eta,\gamma)=(D\Psi_{2}(w_{0},\gamma_{0})-D\Psi_{2}(0,0,0,\gamma_{0}))(q,u,\eta,0)+D\Psi_{2}(w_{0},\gamma_{0})(0,0,0,\gamma)=\mathbf{J}_{1}+\mathbf{J}_{2},

where
\Psi_{2}
is the nonlinear map defined in (
3.3.7
). We may use that
\Psi_{2}
is
C^{2}
paired with the fundamental theorem of calculus to write

(3.4.57)

\mathbf{J}_{1}=\int_{0}^{1}D^{2}\Psi_{2}(tw_{0},\gamma_{0})((q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0},0),(q,u,\eta,0))\;\mathrm{d}t.

Hence, by the strong tame estimates on the second derivative from Proposition
3.11
, the log-convexity of the norm of the
\mathbb{X}_{s}
-spaces (see Lemma
3.25
), and Young’s inequality we get that for
\upnu\geqslant 1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor
:

(3.4.58)

\lVert\mathbf{J}_{1}\rVert_{H^{s}}\lesssim\rho\lVert q,u,\eta\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}+\lVert q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}\lVert q,u,\eta\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}.

On the other hand, by inspection we see that
D\Psi_{2}(0,\gamma_{0})(0,0,0,\gamma)=0
. Thus by a similar fundamental theorem of calculus argument, we have that

(3.4.59)

\mathbf{J}_{2}=\int_{0}^{1}D^{2}\Psi_{2}(tw_{0},\gamma_{0})((w_{0},0),(0,0,0,\gamma))\;\mathrm{d}t.

This lets us use the strong tame estimates on the second derivative again to bound

(3.4.60)

\lVert\mathbf{J}_{2}\rVert_{H^{s}}\lesssim|\gamma|\lVert q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}.

By combining equations (
3.4.58
) and (
3.4.60
), we obtain the stated bounds for
\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{P^{2}}
.

Step 3
: Estimates on
\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{P^{3}}
. We perform the same analysis, utilizing the fundamental theorem of calculus and the tame
C^{2}
-estimates as in the second step, but this time we employ Proposition
3.12
in place of Proposition
3.11
.
∎

The remainder of this subsection is devoted to the decomposition of the
D\Phi
piece of
D\overline{\Psi}
, which is derived from equations (
3.3.9
) and (
3.3.10
), into subcomponents
Q
and
R
. The key observation here is that
\Phi:B_{\mathbb{X}_{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho_{\mathrm{WD}})\times\mathbb{W}_{3+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\to\mathbb{Y}^{0}
is linear in the second factor, and hence for
\theta_{0}
as in (
3.4.3
) we can write,

(3.4.61)

D\Phi(\theta_{0})(q,u,\eta,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F})=\overset{\theta_{0}}{Q}(q,u,\eta)+\overset{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}{R}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F})

where

(3.4.62)

\overset{\theta_{0}}{Q}(q,u,\eta)=D\Phi(\theta_{0})(q,u,\eta,0,0,0)

and

(3.4.63)

\overset{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}{R}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F})=D\Phi(q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0},0,0,0)(0,0,0,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F}).

The following result reveals the point of the
Q+R
decomposition of
D\Phi
:
Q
is small when the background is small, and
R
is independent of
(q,u,\eta)
and thus enjoys a useful decoupling. In contrast with the
A+P
decomposition (Proposition
3.21
), the next result is less delicate and only relies on the smooth-tameness results from Propositions
3.14
and
3.15
.

Proposition 3.22

(Properties of the
Q+R
decomposition of
D\Phi
)
.

Let
0<\rho\leqslant\rho_{\mathrm{WD}}
, where
\rho_{\mathrm{WD}}
is defined in Theorem
3.17
, and

(3.4.64)

\theta_{0}=(q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0},\mathcal{T}_{0},\mathcal{G}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0})\in(B_{\mathbb{X}_{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho)\times B_{\mathbb{W}_{3+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho))\cap(\mathbb{X}_{\infty}\times\mathbb{W}_{\infty}).

The following hold for
\mathbb{N}\ni s\geqslant 2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor
.

(1)

The map
\overset{\theta_{0}}{Q}:\mathbb{X}_{s}\to\mathbb{Y}^{s}
is well-defined, continuous, and obeys the estimate

(3.4.65)

\big\lVert\overset{\theta_{0}}{Q}(q,u,\eta)\big\rVert_{\mathbb{Y}^{s}}\lesssim\rho\lVert q,u,\eta\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}+\lVert\theta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}\times\mathbb{W}_{1+s}}\lVert q,u,\eta\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}.

(2)

The map
\overset{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}{R}:\mathbb{W}_{1+s}\to\mathbb{Y}^{s}
is well-defined, continuous, and obeys the estimate

(3.4.66)

\big\lVert\overset{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}{R}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F})\big\rVert_{\mathbb{Y}^{s}}\lesssim\lVert\mathcal{T},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F}\rVert_{\mathbb{W}_{1+s}}+\langle\lVert q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}\rangle\lVert\mathcal{T},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F}\rVert_{\mathbb{W}_{3+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}.

In the above the implicit constants depend on the dimension, the physical parameters,
s
, and
\rho_{\mathrm{WD}}
.

Proof.

That
\overset{\theta_{0}}{Q}
is well-defined, continuous, and, when
\rho=\rho_{\mathrm{WD}}
and
s\geqslant 2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor
, obeys a tame estimate of the form

(3.4.67)

\big\lVert\overset{\theta_{0}}{Q}(q,u,\eta)\big\rVert_{\mathbb{Y}^{s}}\lesssim\lVert q,u,\eta\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}+\langle\lVert\theta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}\times\mathbb{W}_{1+s}}\rangle\lVert q,u,\eta\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}

follows from the formula (
3.4.62
), the tame smoothness assertions of Propositions
3.14
and
3.15
, and the tame estimates on derivatives from Lemma
2.7
. We can improve the above estimate by leveraging the fact that for fixed
(q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0})
the map
(\mathcal{T}_{0},\mathcal{G}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0})\mapsto\overset{\theta_{0}}{Q}
is linear; indeed, by arguing as in the proof of Lemma
2.5
, we deduce from estimate (
3.4.67
) that actually

(3.4.68)

\big\lVert\overset{\theta_{0}}{Q}(q,u,\eta)\big\rVert_{\mathbb{Y}^{s}}\lesssim\lVert\mathcal{T}_{0},\mathcal{G}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{W}_{3+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}\lVert q,u,\eta\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}+\lVert\theta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}\times\mathbb{W}_{1+s}}\lVert q,u,\eta\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}.

The first item now follows. The assertions and estimate of the second item are a direct consequence of formula (
3.4.63
), the tame smoothness assertions of Propositions
3.14
and
3.15
,and the tame estimates on derivatives from Lemma
2.7
.
∎

3.5.
Prelude to linear analysis

Recall that
\overline{\Psi}
is the nonlinear operator associated with the PDE (
1.4.9
) and is defined in (
3.3.2
). Our goal now is to prove that this map satisfies the hypotheses of the inverse function theorem, which are enumerated in Definition
2.20
, Theorem
2.21
, and Theorem
2.24
. The previous analysis of this section, in particular Lemma
3.2
and Theorem
3.17
, shows that the ‘nonlinear hypotheses,’ other than a trivial issue with the domain and the first item of Definition
2.20
, of this inverse function theorem are satisfied.

It only remains, then, to show that the ‘linear hypotheses’ given in the third item of Definition
2.20
are satisfied. In other words, we aim to prove the following assertion about the derivative,
D\overline{\Psi}
. Given
\mathbb{N}\ni s\geqslant 2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor
and an interval
I\Subset\mathbb{R}^{+}
, there exists an
existence and estimates
parameter
\rho_{\mathrm{EE}}(s)\in(0,\rho_{\mathrm{WD}}]
(also depending on
I
), where
\rho_{\mathrm{WD}}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
is from Theorem
3.17
, with the property that whenever (recall that
\theta_{0}
is defined in (
3.4.3
))

(3.5.1)

(\theta_{0},\gamma_{0})\in(B_{\mathbb{X}^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho_{\mathrm{EE}}(s))\times B_{\mathbb{W}_{3+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho_{\mathrm{EE}}(s))\times I)\cap(\mathbb{X}^{1+s}\times\mathbb{W}_{2+s}\times\mathbb{R}^{+})

and

(3.5.2)

(g,f,k,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F},\gamma)\in\mathbb{Y}^{s}\times\mathbb{W}_{1+s}\times\mathbb{R},

there exists a unique
(q,u,\eta)\in\mathbb{X}^{s}
solving
D\overline{\Psi}(\theta_{0},\gamma_{0})(q,u,\eta,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F},\gamma)=(g,f,k,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F},\gamma)
; moreover, if we set
\Xi=(g,f,k,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F},\gamma)
and
\Theta=(q,u,\eta,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F},\gamma)
then the solution tuple obeys the tame estimate

(3.5.3)

\lVert\Theta\rVert_{\mathbb{X}^{s}\times\mathbb{W}_{1+s}\times\mathbb{R}}\lesssim\lVert\Xi\rVert_{\mathbb{Y}^{s}\times\mathbb{W}_{1+s}\times\mathbb{R}}+\langle\lVert\theta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}^{1+s}\times\mathbb{W}_{2+s}}\rangle\lVert\Xi\rVert_{\mathbb{X}^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\times\mathbb{W}_{3+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\times\mathbb{R}}

for an implicit constant depending only on the dimension, the various physical parameters,
s
,
I
, and
\rho_{\mathrm{EE}}(s)
.

Thanks to the analysis of the previous subsection, namely Propositions
3.21
and
3.22
, we have that the behavior of
D\overline{\Psi}
is governed by the principal part operator
A
defined in (
3.4.7
). Thus our above goal is essentially achieved, modulo minor supplementary analysis, as soon as we prove the following assertion. For any
s\in\mathbb{N}
and
\gamma_{0}\in I\Subset\mathbb{R}^{+}
an interval, there exists an
existence and estimates principal part
parameter
\rho_{\mathrm{EEP}}(s)\in(0,\rho_{\mathrm{WD}}]
(also depending on
I
) with the property that whenever

(3.5.4)

w_{0}=(q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0})\in B_{\mathbb{X}^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho_{\mathrm{EEP}}(s))\cap\mathbb{X}^{\infty}\text{ and }(g,f,k)\in\mathbb{Y}^{s},

there exists a unique
(q,u,\eta)\in\mathbb{X}^{s}
solving
\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{A}(q,u,\eta)=(g,f,k)
; moreover, the solution obeys the tame estimates

(3.5.5)

\lVert q,u,\eta\rVert_{\mathbb{X}^{s}}\lesssim\lVert g,f,k\rVert_{\mathbb{Y}^{s}}+\begin{cases}0&\text{if }s\leqslant\lfloor n/2\rfloor,\\
\langle\lVert q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{1+s}}\rangle\lVert q,u,\eta\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}&\text{if }\lfloor n/2\rfloor<s.\end{cases}

Again we allow for implied constants to depend on the dimension, the physical parameters,
s
,
I
, and
\rho_{\mathrm{EEP}}(s)
. We will see that a necessary condition for the estimate (
3.5.5
) to hold is that the operator
\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{A}:\overset{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}{\mathbb{X}^{s}}\to\mathbb{Y}^{s}
is a Banach isomorphism, where we recall the adapted spaces are defined in (
3.4.36
). This expresses one of the core difficulties of the linear analysis: we either have that the family of operators
\{{\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{A}}\}\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{X}^{1+s};\mathbb{Y}^{s})
are defined on the common Banach space
\mathbb{X}^{1+s}
but
not
isomorphisms, or else each individual operator
\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{A}
is defined on a larger adapted Banach space
\overset{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}{\mathbb{X}^{s}}
making it an isomorphism onto
\mathbb{Y}^{s}
, but the spaces
\{{\overset{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}{\mathbb{X}^{s}}}\}
are
inequivalent
. Consequently, we cannot port invertibility from one operator to the next via the method of continuity, even if we were to establish the estimates of (
3.5.5
) a priori.

We overcome this difficulty via an elliptic regularization procedure which we now describe. For
m\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
we define the
2m^{\mathrm{th}}
-order linear elliptic differential operator

(3.5.6)

L_{m}=(-1)^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\partial_{j}^{2m}.

We will consider a sequence of operators obtained by adding a vanishing contribution of this operator to the continuity equation component of
A
and also adding a vanishing contribution of
(-\Delta_{\|})^{m-1/4}
to the kinematic boundary condition. These new operators have the following domains: for
m,N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
and
s\in\{{-1}\}\cup\mathbb{N}
we define the space

(3.5.7)

\mathbb{X}^{s}_{m,N}=\{(q,u,\eta)\in\mathbb{X}_{s}\;:\;\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}(u)=0,\;\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma}(u\cdot e_{n})+\partial_{1}\eta=N^{-1}(-\Delta_{\|})^{m-1/4}\eta\\
(q,\eta)\in H^{1+s+2m}(\Omega)\times\mathcal{H}^{1+s+2m}(\Sigma),\;\mathrm{Tr}_{\partial\Omega}(\partial_{n}^{m}q)=\cdots=\mathrm{Tr}_{\partial\Omega}(\partial_{n}^{2m-1}q)=0\}

and endow it with the norms

(3.5.8)

\lVert q,u,\eta\rVert_{\mathbb{X}^{s}_{m,N}}=\sqrt{\lVert q,u,\eta\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}^{2}+N^{-2}\lVert q,\eta\rVert^{2}_{H^{1+s+2m}\times\mathcal{H}^{1+s+2m}}},

and

(3.5.9)

\lVert q,u,\eta\rVert_{\overset{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}{\mathbb{X}^{s}_{m,N}}}=\sqrt{\lVert q,u,\eta\rVert_{\mathbb{X}^{s}_{m,N}}^{2}+\lVert\nabla\cdot(v_{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}q)\rVert_{H^{1+s}}^{2}}.

We note that, in light of the fourth item of Lemma
3.19
,
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\overset{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}{\mathbb{X}^{s}_{m,N}}}
is a norm equivalent to
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathbb{X}^{s}_{m,N}}
, with equivalence constants depending on
s
,
m
,
N
, and
\rho_{\mathrm{WD}}
.

For
s\in\mathbb{N}
we then define the regularized principal part operator

(3.5.10)

\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{A_{m,N}}:\mathbb{X}^{s}_{m,N}\to\mathbb{Y}^{s}

via

(3.5.11)

\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{A_{m,N}}(q,u,\eta)=(N^{-1}L_{m}(q+\mathfrak{g}\eta)+\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{A^{1}}(q,u,\eta),\overset{\gamma_{0}}{A^{2}}(q,u,\eta),\overset{\gamma_{0}}{A^{3}}(q,u,\eta)),

where the
A^{i}
terms are the same as in (
3.4.7
).

The upshot is that the operators
\{{\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{A_{m,N}}}\}
are all contained in the space
\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{X}^{s}_{m,N},\mathbb{Y}^{s})
and, as we will see, obey a family of nice a priori estimates. Thus the method of continuity is available for the regularized operators’ existence theory.

Once we have existence for the regularized operators, we would like to show that we can pass to the limit as
N\to\infty
and obtain existence for
A
. This is achieved by carefully proving
N
-independent a priori estimates for
A_{m,N}
by inductively working up from the base case of a priori estimates for weak solutions. This inductive estimate procedure will also be done in tandem with the operator
A
to obtain the sought-after a priori estimates of (
3.5.5
).

As a result, we will need to set notation for weak formulations of the operators
A
and
A_{m,N}
. First we recall the definition of the space
{{}_{0}}H^{1}
from (
1.7.3
). The operator associated with the weak formulation of the momentum equation is

(3.5.12)

\overset{\gamma_{0}}{\mathscr{I}}:\mathbb{X}_{-1}\to({{}_{0}}H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n}))^{\ast}

defined by

(3.5.13)

\langle\overset{\gamma_{0}}{\mathscr{I}}(q,u,\eta),w\rangle_{({{}_{0}}H^{1})^{\ast},{{}_{0}}H^{1}}=\int_{\Omega}-\gamma_{0}^{2}\varrho\partial_{1}u\cdot w-q\nabla\cdot(\varrho w)+\mathfrak{g}\varrho\nabla\eta\cdot w+\gamma_{0}\mathbb{S}^{\varrho}u:\nabla w\\
-\varsigma\langle\Delta_{\|}\eta,\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma}(w\cdot e_{n})\rangle_{H^{-1/2},H^{1/2}}

for
(q,u,\eta)\in\mathbb{X}_{-1}
and
w\in{{}_{0}}H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
. We next define a family of operators associated with the weak formulation of the full problem by setting

(3.5.14)

\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{\mathscr{J}}:\overset{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}{\mathbb{X}^{-1}}\to\mathbb{Y}^{-1}

via

(3.5.15)

\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{\mathscr{J}}(q,u,\eta)=\big(\nabla\cdot(\varrho u)+\nabla\cdot(v_{w_{0}}(q+\mathfrak{g}\eta)),\overset{\gamma_{0}}{\mathscr{I}}(q,u,\eta)\big),

where we recall that the vector field
v_{w_{0}}
is defined in (
3.4.11
). Furthermore, given
\tau\in[0,1]
and
m,N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
with
m\geqslant 2
we define the map

(3.5.16)

\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{\mathscr{J}^{\tau}_{m,N}}:\mathbb{X}_{m,N}^{-1}\to\mathbb{Y}^{-1}

via

(3.5.17)

\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{\mathscr{J}^{\tau}_{m,N}}(q,u,\eta)=(\nabla\cdot(\varrho u)+\tau\nabla\cdot(v_{w_{0}}(q+\mathfrak{g}\eta))+N^{-1}L_{m}(q+\mathfrak{g}\eta),\overset{\gamma_{0}}{\mathscr{I}}(q,u,\eta)).

We now give a basic result on the well-definedness and continuity of these operators.

Lemma 3.23

(Well-definedness check for linear analysis)
.

Let
\rho\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and
w_{0}=(q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0})
be as in the hypotheses of Lemma
3.19
. Let
I\Subset\mathbb{R}^{+}
be an interval with
\gamma_{0}\in I
. The following hold.

(1)

The weak formulation operator of (
3.5.14
) is well-defined and bounded.

(2)

For
\mathbb{N}\ni m\geqslant 2
,
N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, and
\tau\in[0,1]
, the regularized weak formulation operator of (
3.5.16
) is well-defined and bounded.

(3)

For
\mathbb{N}\ni m\geqslant 2
and
N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
the regularization of the principal part operator defined by (
3.5.10
) is well-defined and bounded.

Proof.

For the first item we see that it suffices to check that the first component of
\overset{\gamma_{0},w_{0}}{\mathscr{J}}
is well-defined and maps boundedly into
\hat{H}^{0}(\Omega)
. For this we can argue in a manner similar to the first part of the proof of Proposition
3.21
, where we studied (
3.4.39
). The only difference is in handling the
\nabla\cdot(v_{w_{0}}q)
term. That this term maps boundedly into
L^{2}(\Omega)
is immediate from the definition of the norm on
\overset{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}{\mathbb{X}^{-1}}
. We then use Proposition
B.10
, paired with the fact that
v_{w_{0}}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
, to obtain that
q\mapsto\int_{0}^{b}\nabla\cdot(v_{w_{0}}q)(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y
maps boundedly into
\dot{H}^{-1}(\Omega)
.

The second and third items follow as soon as we check that

(3.5.18)

\mathbb{X}^{-1}_{m,N}\ni(q,u,\eta)\mapsto\nabla\cdot(\varrho u)+\tau\nabla\cdot(v_{w_{0}}(q+\mathfrak{g}\eta))+N^{-1}L_{m}(q+\mathfrak{g}\eta)\in\hat{H}^{0}(\Omega)

is a well-defined and bounded linear map. We handle the
\tau\nabla\cdot(v_{w_{0}}(q+\mathfrak{g}\eta))
term in the same way that we handled (
3.4.41
) from Proposition
3.21
. For the
N^{-1}L_{m}(q+\mathfrak{g}\eta)
term we see that it obviously maps into
L^{2}(\Omega)
. To acquire the divergence compatibility condition, we use the Neumann boundary conditions built into the domain
\mathbb{X}^{-1}_{m,N}
to compute

(3.5.19)

\int_{0}^{b}\frac{1}{N}L_{m}(q+\mathfrak{g}\eta)(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\partial_{j}^{2m}\Big(\mathfrak{g}b\eta+\int_{0}^{b}q(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y\Big),

but since
m\geqslant 1
, this provides the bound

(3.5.20)

\Big[{\int_{0}^{b}\frac{1}{N}L_{m}(q+\mathfrak{g}\eta)(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y}\Big]_{\dot{H}^{-1}}\lesssim\frac{1}{N}(\lVert\nabla_{\|}\eta\rVert_{H^{2m-2}}+\lVert q\rVert_{H^{2m-1}})\lesssim\lVert q,u,\eta\rVert_{\mathbb{X}^{-1}_{m,N}}.

Finally we handle the
\nabla\cdot(\varrho u)
term of (
3.5.18
). Evidently, this maps into
L^{2}(\Omega)
, leaving us to check the divergence compatibility condition. By integrating, we learn that

(3.5.21)

\int_{0}^{b}\nabla\cdot(\varrho u)(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y=(\nabla_{\|},0)\cdot\int_{0}^{b}(\varrho u)(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y-\varrho(b)\partial_{1}\eta+\frac{\varrho(b)}{N}(-\Delta_{\|})^{m-1/4}\eta,

and since
m\geqslant 2
this yields the estimate

(3.5.22)

\Big[{\int_{0}^{b}\nabla\cdot(\varrho u)(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y}\Big]_{\dot{H}^{-1}}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{L^{2}}+\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+\frac{1}{N}\lVert\nabla_{\|}\eta\rVert_{H^{2m-5/2}}\lesssim\lVert q,u,\eta\rVert_{\mathbb{X}^{-1}_{m,N}}.

∎

We now consider the relationship between the weak and strong operators,
\mathscr{J}
,
\mathscr{J}^{\tau}_{m,N}
and
A
,
A_{m,N}
, by introducing a functional that maps the strong form of the data to the weak formulation of the data. For
s\in\mathbb{N}
we define the strong-to-weak data map

(3.5.23)

\mathscr{K}:H^{s}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})\times H^{1/2+s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}^{n})\to({{}_{0}}H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n}))^{\ast}

given by

(3.5.24)

\langle\mathscr{K}(f,k),w\rangle_{({{}_{0}}H^{1})^{\ast},{{}_{0}}H^{1}}=\int_{\Omega}f\cdot w+\int_{\Sigma}k\cdot w,

where
w\in{{}_{0}}H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
(f,k)\in H^{s}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})\times H^{1/2+s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and
s\in\mathbb{N}
.

Lemma 3.24

(Strong and weak solutions)
.

Under the hypotheses of Lemma
3.23
, the following hold for
(g,f,k)\in\mathbb{Y}^{0}
.

(1)

If
(q,u,\eta)\in\overset{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}{\mathbb{X}^{0}}
, then
\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{A}(q,u,\eta)=(g,f,k)
if and only if
\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{\mathscr{J}}(q,u,\eta)=(g,\mathscr{K}(f,k))
.

(2)

If
m,N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
,
m\geqslant 2
, and
(q,u,\eta)\in\mathbb{X}^{0}_{m,N}
, then
\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{A_{m,N}}(q,u,\eta)=(g,f,k)
if and only if
\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{\mathscr{J}^{1}_{m,N}}(q,u,\eta)=(g,\mathscr{K}(f,k))
.

Proof.

These follow directly from integration by parts.
∎

This section is concluded with the following simple lemma on log-convexity, which we recall is defined in Section
1.7
.

Lemma 3.25

(Log-convexity of the norms)
.

The following Banach scales are log-convex for the stated norms:

\displaystyle\big(\mathbb{X}^{s},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathbb{X}^{s}}\big)_{s\in\mathbb{N}},

\displaystyle\big(\overset{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}{\mathbb{X}^{s}},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\overset{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}{\mathbb{X}^{s}}}\big)_{s\in\mathbb{N}},

\displaystyle\big(\mathbb{X}^{s}_{m,N},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathbb{X}^{s}_{m,N}}\big)_{s\in\mathbb{N}},

(3.5.25)

\displaystyle\big(\mathbb{X}^{s}_{m,N},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\overset{q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0}}{\mathbb{X}^{s}_{m,N}}}\big)_{s\in\mathbb{N}},

\displaystyle\big(\mathbb{Y}^{s},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathbb{Y}^{s}}\big)_{s\in\mathbb{N}},

where we take
m,N\in\mathbb{N}
and
(q_{0},u_{0},\eta_{0})
be as in the hypotheses of Lemma
3.19
.

Proof.

Lemma
B.5
shows that the anisotropic Sobolev spaces
\{{\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
are log-convex. The result then follows from the log-convexity of standard Sobolev spaces (see Theorem
D.4
and Corollary
D.5
) and the evident preservation of log-convexity under products.
∎

4.
Analysis of steady transport equations and their regularizations

In the previous section we introduced the regularized principal part operator
\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{A_{m,N}}
(defined in equation (
3.5.10
)), which perturbs the operator
\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{A}
(defined in equation (
3.4.7
)) by adding a multiple of the elliptic operator
L_{m}
(defined in (
3.5.6
)) to the part of
\overset{w_{0},\gamma_{0}}{A}
corresponding to the linearized continuity equation. In this section we focus our attention on solutions to such regularized steady transport equations, with the aim of deriving precise estimates that are independent of the regularization parameter. These will play an essential role in our subsequent linear analysis. Along the way, we also develop some estimates of solutions to the standard steady transport equation.

To be precise, we now study equations of the type

(4.0.1)

\alpha f+\nabla\cdot(vf)+\varepsilon L_{m}f=g\text{ in }\Omega,

where
\alpha,g:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
are given functions,
v:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a given vector field,
\varepsilon\geqslant 0
is small,
L_{m}
is the linear elliptic operator given by (
3.5.6
), and
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
is the unknown. The key results of this section are Proposition
4.4
and Theorem
4.8
.

In what follows, a key player is a vector field
X\in W^{\infty,\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
\mathrm{Tr}_{\partial\Omega}(X\cdot e_{n})=0
. Assume
X_{0}\in H^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
X_{1}\in W^{\infty,\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
\rho_{\mathrm{max}}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
is arbitrary but fixed,
0<\rho\leqslant\rho_{\mathrm{max}}
,
r\in\mathbb{N}
,

(4.0.2)

X=X_{0}+X_{1},\text{ and }(DX_{0},DX_{1})\in B_{H^{r}}(0,\rho)\times B_{W^{r,\infty}}(0,\rho).
