\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\Big\{{f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})+\bigcup_{p_{d}<p<\infty}L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\;:\;\nabla f\in H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})\text{ and }\partial_{1}f\in\dot{H}^{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\Big\},

with the equivalence of norms

(B.1.25)

\lVert f\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}\asymp\sqrt{\lVert\nabla f\rVert_{H^{s-1}}^{2}+[{\partial_{1}f}]_{\dot{H}^{-1}}^{2}},

where the implied constants depend only on
d
and
s
.

Proof.

We begin with the proof of the first item. Let
f\in\mathscr{S}^{\ast}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
satisfy
\nabla f\in H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\partial_{1}f\in\dot{H}^{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and let
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be such that
\varphi=1
on
B(0,1/2)
,
0\leqslant\varphi\leqslant 1
, and
\supp(\varphi)\subseteq B(0,1)
. Given
\delta\in(0,1)
, write
\varphi_{\delta}(\xi)=\varphi(\xi/\delta)
for
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For
\delta\in(0,1)
define
f_{\delta}\in\mathscr{S}^{\ast}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
via
\mathscr{F}[f_{\delta}]=(1-\varphi_{\delta})\mathscr{F}[f]
and note that
\supp(\mathscr{F}[f_{\delta}])\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash B(0,\delta/2)
and that
\mathscr{F}[f]=\mathscr{F}[f_{\delta}]
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash B(0,\delta)
. Since
\nabla f\in H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
we have that
\mathscr{F}[f_{\delta}]
is a locally integrable function given by

(B.1.26)

\mathscr{F}[f_{\delta}](\xi)=-(1-\varphi_{\delta}(\xi))\mathrm{i}\xi(2\pi|\xi|^{2})^{-1}\cdot\mathscr{F}[\nabla f](\xi).

This fact and the equivalence

(B.1.27)

|\xi|^{-2}\xi_{1}^{2}+|\xi|^{2}\langle\xi\rangle^{2(s-1)}\asymp|\xi|^{-2}(\xi_{1}^{2}+|\xi|^{4})\mathds{1}_{B(0,1)}(\xi)+\langle\xi\rangle^{2s}\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B(0,1)}(\xi)\text{ for }\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\{0\},

where the implicit constants depend only on
d
and
s
, justify the estimate

(B.1.28)

\left\lVert f_{\delta}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big(|\xi|^{-2}(\xi_{1}^{2}+|\xi|^{4})\mathds{1}_{B(0,1)}+\langle\xi\rangle^{2s}\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B(0,1)}\big)|\mathscr{F}[f_{\delta}](\xi)|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\xi\\
\asymp\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash B(0,\delta/2)}(|\xi|^{-2}\xi_{1}^{2}+|\xi|^{2}\langle\xi\rangle^{2(s-1)})|1-\varphi_{\delta}(\xi)|^{2}|\mathscr{F}[f](\xi)|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\xi\lesssim[{\partial_{1}f}]^{2}_{\dot{H}^{-1}}+\lVert\nabla f\rVert_{H^{s-1}}^{2},

which shows that
\{{f_{\delta}}\}_{\delta\in(0,1)}\subset\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. A similar argument shows that
\{{f_{\delta}}\}_{\delta\in(0,1)}
is Cauchy in
\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and since
\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is complete,
f_{\delta}\to g\in\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as
\delta\to 0
. On the other hand, since
\mathscr{F}[f]=\mathscr{F}[f_{\delta}]
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash B(0,\delta)
, we readily deduce that
\mathscr{F}[f]=\mathscr{F}[g]
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\{0\}
and hence that
\mathrm{supp}(\mathscr{F}[f-g])\subseteq\{{0}\}
. The tempered distributions supported at a point consist only of linear combinations of Dirac masses and their derivatives; hence, there is a polynomial
P
such that
f-g=P
. Theorem 5.6 Leoni and Tice
[
60
]
shows that the inclusion
g\in\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
implies that
\nabla g\in H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
(the proof there is stated for
s\geqslant 1
, but the argument works just as well for
0\leqslant s<1
). Consequently,
\nabla P=\nabla(f-g)\in H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, which requires that
P
is a constant. This completes the proof of the first item.

We now turn to the proof of the second item. Call the second space in (
B.1.24
)
\mathcal{H}^{s}_{\mathrm{II}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Theorems 5.5 and 5.6 of
[
60
]
show that if
f\in\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then
\nabla f\in H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\partial_{1}f\in\dot{H}^{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
; these inclusions, together with the observations of Remark
B.3
, show that
\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{s}_{\mathrm{II}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

To prove the reverse inclusion, we first note that
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})+\bigcup_{p_{d}<p<\infty}L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subseteq\mathscr{S}^{\ast}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
. Thus, the first item shows that if
f\in\mathcal{H}^{s}_{\mathrm{II}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then
f-c\in\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for some
c\in\mathbb{R}
. On the other hand, by definition we can choose
p_{d}<q<\infty
such that
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})+L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and so upon appealing again to Remark
B.3
we find that
c=f-(f-c)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})+L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, which implies that
c=0
. Thus,
f\in\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and we deduce from this that
\mathcal{H}^{s}_{\mathrm{II}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

We now know that
\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\mathcal{H}^{s}_{\mathrm{II}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, so it remains only to verify (
B.1.25
). This follows from (
B.1.27
), together with the additional equivalences

(B.1.29)

\lVert\nabla f\rVert_{H^{s-1}}\asymp\lVert|\nabla|\langle\nabla\rangle^{s-1}f\rVert_{L^{2}}\text{ and }[{\partial_{1}f}]_{\dot{H}^{-1}}\asymp\lVert|\nabla|^{-1}\partial_{1}f\rVert_{L^{2}}\text{ for }f\in\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

where again the implicit constants depend only on
s
and
d
.
∎

The next property of the specialized Sobolev spaces that we need is a simple interpolation property.

Lemma B.5

(Log-convexity of the norm in anisotropic Sobolev spaces)
.

Let
s_{0},s_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
be such that
s_{0},s_{1}\geqslant 0
, and let
\sigma\in[0,1]
. For all
f\in(\mathcal{H}^{s_{0}}\cap\mathcal{H}^{s_{1}})(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have that
f\in\mathcal{H}^{(1-\sigma)s_{0}+\sigma s_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and satisfies the bound

(B.1.30)

\lVert f\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{(1-\sigma)s_{0}+\sigma s_{1}}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert^{1-\sigma}_{\mathcal{H}^{s_{0}}}\lVert f\rVert^{\sigma}_{\mathcal{H}^{s_{1}}}.

Proof.

We define an auxiliary function

(B.1.31)

\chi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}\text{ via }\chi(\xi)=(|\xi\cdot e_{1}||\xi|^{-1}+|\xi|)\mathds{1}_{B(0,1)}(\xi)+\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B(0,1)}(\xi).

From the definition of the norm on
\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
it is clear that if
\eta
belongs to this space then
\chi(\nabla/2\pi\mathrm{i})\eta\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and we have the equivalence

(B.1.32)

\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}\asymp\lVert\chi(\nabla/2\pi\mathrm{i})\eta\rVert_{H^{s}}.

From (
B.1.32
), we see immediately that the log-convexity of the
\mathcal{H}^{s}
spaces follows from the usual log-convexity of
L^{2}
-based Sobolev spaces.
∎

We now study smoothing operators on the scales of anisotropic Sobolev spaces.

Lemma B.6

(LP-smoothability)
.

The Banach scale
\{{\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
is LP-smoothable in the sense of Definition
2.11
.

Proof.

For the smoothing operators we make the obvious choice
S_{j}\eta=\mathds{1}_{B(0,2^{j})}(\nabla/2\pi\mathrm{i})\eta
for
j\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
. The continuity of
S_{j}:\mathcal{H}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathcal{H}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is clear. Moreover, the Littlewood-Paley condition (
2.1.22
) is trivially satisfied with
A=1
. Thus it remains only to verify the smoothing inequalities from the first item of Definition (
2.11
). We again consider the auxiliary function
\chi
from (
B.1.31
). Since the smoothing operators
\{{S_{j}}\}_{j=0}^{\infty}
commute with the Fourier multiplication operator
\chi(\nabla/2\pi\mathrm{i})
, we see that the smoothing inequalities of (
2.1.17
)–(
2.1.20
) follow directly from LP-smoothability of
\{{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
(see Example
2.13
) and (
B.1.32
).
∎

B.2.
Adapted Sobolev spaces

Given
X\in L^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we define the adapted Sobolev space

(B.2.1)

H^{0}_{X}(\Omega)=\{{\varphi\in L^{2}(\Omega)\;:\;\nabla\cdot(X\varphi)\in L^{2}(\Omega)}\},

where the divergence is understood in the sense of distributions, and equip it with the obvious norm
\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{0}_{X}}=\sqrt{\lVert\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\lVert\nabla\cdot(X\varphi)\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}}
and associated inner-product.

Lemma B.7

(Basic properties of
H^{0}_{X}(\Omega)
)
.

Let
X\in L^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Then the following hold.

(1)

H^{0}_{X}(\Omega)
is a Hilbert space.

(2)

If
\psi\in C^{\infty}(\Omega)\cap W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)
and
\varphi\in H^{0}_{X}(\Omega)
, then we have the inclusion
\varphi\psi\in H^{0}_{X}(\Omega)
and the estimate
\left\lVert\varphi\psi\right\rVert_{H^{0}_{X}}\lesssim\left\langle\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\right\rangle\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert_{W^{1,\infty}}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{H^{0}_{X}}
.

(3)

The space
\{\varphi\in H^{0}_{X}(\Omega)\;:\;\supp(\varphi)\text{ is bounded}\}
is dense in
H^{0}_{X}(\Omega)
.

Proof.

Suppose
\{\varphi_{k}\}_{k=0}^{\infty}\subseteq H^{0}_{X}(\Omega)
is Cauchy. Then the sequences
\{\varphi_{k}\}_{k=0}^{\infty}
and
\{\zeta_{k}\}_{k=0}^{\infty}
are Cauchy in
L^{2}
, where
\zeta_{k}=\nabla\cdot(\varphi_{k}X)
. Thus, there exist
\varphi,\zeta\in L^{2}(\Omega)
such that
\varphi_{k}\to\varphi
and
\zeta_{k}\to\zeta
in
L^{2}
. However, for
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
,

(B.2.2)

\int_{\Omega}\zeta\chi=\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}\zeta_{k}\chi=\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}-\varphi_{k}X\cdot\nabla\chi=\int_{\Omega}-\varphi X\cdot\nabla\chi,

so
\zeta=\nabla\cdot(\varphi X)
in the sense of distributions, and we deduce that
H^{0}_{X}(\Omega)
is complete and hence a Hilbert space. The first item is proved.

Now we prove the second item. Let
\varphi\in H^{0}_{X}(\Omega)
and
\psi\in W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)\cap C^{\infty}(\Omega)
. We begin by claiming that

(B.2.3)

\nabla\cdot(X\psi\varphi)=\psi\nabla\cdot(X\varphi)+X\varphi\cdot\nabla\psi.

Once the claim is proved, the inclusion
\varphi\psi\in H^{0}_{X}(\Omega)
and the stated estimate follow immediately. To prove the claim, let
\{{\varphi_{\ell}}\}_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq H^{1}(\Omega)\cap C^{\infty}(\Omega)
be a sequence such that
\varphi_{\ell}\to\varphi
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
as
\ell\to\infty
. By the product rule, we have the identity

(B.2.4)

\nabla\cdot(X\psi\varphi_{\ell})=\psi\nabla\cdot(X\varphi_{\ell})+X\varphi_{\ell}\cdot\nabla\psi\text{ for every }\ell\in\mathbb{N},

or more precisely, for any
\chi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega)
we have that

(B.2.5)

-\int_{\Omega}X\psi\varphi_{\ell}\cdot\nabla\chi=-\int_{\Omega}\nabla(\psi\chi)\cdot X\varphi_{\ell}+\int_{\Omega}\chi X\varphi_{\ell}\cdot\nabla\psi.

Now we send
\ell\to\infty
and use the convergence in
L^{2}(\Omega)
and the inclusion
\nabla\cdot(X\varphi)\in L^{2}(\Omega)
to deduce that

(B.2.6)

-\int_{\Omega}X\psi\varphi\cdot\nabla\chi=\int_{\Omega}(\psi\nabla\cdot(X\varphi)+X\varphi\cdot\nabla\psi)\chi,

which is (
B.2.3
). This completes the proof of the claim and the second item.

Finally, we prove the density assertion. Fix
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
\psi=1
in
B(0,1)
. Define
\{{\psi_{N}}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}}\subset H^{1}(\Omega)
via
\psi_{N}=\psi(N^{-1}\cdot)
. Let
\varphi\in H^{0}_{X}(\Omega)
, and write
\varphi_{N}=\psi_{N}\varphi
. The proof is complete as soon as we show that
\varphi_{N}\to\varphi
as
N\to\infty
in the
H^{0}_{X}(\Omega)
-norm. The dominated convergence theorem shows that
\varphi_{N}\to\varphi
in
L^{2}
as
N\to\infty
. On the other hand, formula (
B.2.3
) implies that
\nabla\cdot(X\psi_{N}\varphi)=\psi_{N}\nabla\cdot(X\varphi)+N^{-1}X\varphi(\nabla\psi)(N^{-1}\cdot)
. Since
\nabla\cdot(X\varphi)\in L^{2}(\Omega)
, we have that
\psi_{N}\nabla\cdot(X\varphi)\to\nabla\cdot(X\varphi)
as
N\to\infty
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
. Since
\nabla\psi
is bounded, we have that
N^{-1}X\varphi(\nabla\psi)(N^{-1}\cdot)\to 0
as
N\to\infty
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
. Together, these convergences imply that
\nabla\cdot(X\varphi_{N})\to\nabla\cdot(X\varphi)
as
N\to\infty
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
. Hence, we have shown that
\varphi_{N}\to\varphi
as
N\to\infty
in the
H^{0}_{X}(\Omega)
-norm, and the third item is proved.
∎

Next we prove a density result.

Lemma B.8

(Density of smooth functions in adapted Sobolev spaces)
.

For
X\in W^{1,\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
, the space
C^{\infty}_{c}(\overline{\Omega})
is dense in
H^{0}_{X}(\Omega)
.

Proof.

First note that the inclusion
C^{\infty}_{c}(\overline{\Omega})\subseteq H^{0}_{X}(\Omega)
follows from the extra assumption that
X\in W^{1,\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
and Rademacher’s theorem. To prove density, we endeavor to apply Proposition III.2.8 and Theorem III.2.10 in Boyer and Fabrie
[
11
]
. These results show that, given a bounded Lipschitz domain
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
U\subseteq B(0,R)
, there exist mollification operators
\{{\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}}\}_{\varepsilon>0}
such that if
f\in L^{2}(U)
and
\alpha\in W^{1,\infty}(U;\mathbb{R}^{n})
satisfy
\nabla\cdot(\alpha f)\in L^{2}(U)
, then we have that
\{{\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}f}\}_{\varepsilon>0}\subseteq C^{\infty}_{c}(\overline{U})
with
\supp(\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}f)\subseteq R+1
,
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}f\overset{\varepsilon\to 0}{\to}f
in
L^{2}(U)
, and
\nabla\cdot(\alpha\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}f)\overset{\varepsilon\to 0}{\to}\nabla\cdot(\alpha f)
in
L^{2}(U)
.

Note that
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
does not have a compact boundary, so we cannot directly apply this result. However, thanks to Lemma
B.7
we can reduce to this case. Let
\varphi\in H^{0}_{X}(\Omega)
and let
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
. Thanks to the aforementioned lemma there exists a
\widetilde{\varphi}\in H^{0}_{X}(\Omega)
with bounded support such that
\lVert\varphi-\widetilde{\varphi}\rVert_{H^{0}_{X}}\leqslant\kappa/2
. For some
3\leqslant R\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, we then have that
\mathrm{supp}(\widetilde{\varphi})\subseteq U_{R}=B_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(0,R)\cap\Omega
. The set
U_{2R}
is a Lipschitz domain with compact boundary, and hence it admits smoothing operators
\{{\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}}\}_{\varepsilon>0}
as described above. By the support conditions on
\widetilde{\varphi}
and the properties of these smoothing operators, there exits an
\varepsilon_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that for all
0<\varepsilon\leqslant\varepsilon_{0}
we have that
\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}\widetilde{\varphi})\subseteq U_{3R/2}
and

(B.2.7)

\sqrt{\lVert\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}\widetilde{\varphi}-\widetilde{\varphi}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(U_{2R})}+\lVert\nabla\cdot(X\mathcal{S_{\varepsilon}}\widetilde{\varphi})-\nabla\cdot(X\widetilde{\varphi})\rVert_{L^{2}(U_{2R})}^{2}}\leqslant\kappa/2.

Let
\overline{\varphi}=\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon_{0}}\widetilde{\varphi}
. By support considerations on
\widetilde{\varphi}
and
\overline{\varphi}
, we have that actually
\overline{\varphi}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\overline{\Omega})
and

(B.2.8)

\begin{cases}\lVert\overline{\varphi}-\widetilde{\varphi}\rVert_{L^{2}(U_{2R})}=\lVert\overline{\varphi}-\widetilde{\varphi}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)},\\
\lVert\nabla\cdot(X\overline{\varphi})-\nabla\cdot(X\widetilde{\varphi})\rVert_{L^{2}(U_{2R})}=\lVert\nabla\cdot(X(\overline{\varphi}-\widetilde{\varphi}))\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.\end{cases}

Hence, by the previous two equations we get
\lVert\overline{\varphi}-\widetilde{\varphi}\rVert_{H^{0}_{X}}\leqslant\kappa/2
. The estimate
\lVert\varphi-\overline{\varphi}\rVert_{H^{0}_{X}}\leqslant\kappa
now follows from the triangle inequality, and density is proved.
∎

The next result records some integration by parts formulas.

Proposition B.9

(Some integration by parts)
.

Let
X\in W^{1,\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
be such that
\mathrm{Tr}_{\partial\Omega}(X\cdot e_{n})=0
. The following hold.

(1)

If
\varphi\in H^{0}_{X}(\Omega)
, then

(B.2.9)

\int_{\Omega}\frac{\nabla\cdot X}{2}\varphi^{2}=\int_{\Omega}\varphi\nabla\cdot(\varphi X).

(2)

If
\varphi\in H^{0}_{X}(\Omega)
and
\psi\in H^{1}(\Omega)
, then
\int_{\Omega}\nabla\cdot(\varphi X)\psi=-\int_{\Omega}\varphi X\cdot\nabla\psi
.

Proof.

We begin by proving the first item. Assume initially that
\varphi\in H^{1}(\Omega)
. By using the product rule in two ways, we compute that

(B.2.10)

2^{-1}\nabla\cdot(\varphi^{2}X)=\nabla\cdot(\varphi^{2}X)-2^{-1}\nabla\cdot(\varphi^{2}X)=\varphi\nabla\cdot(\varphi X)-2^{-1}(\nabla\cdot X)\varphi^{2}.

We then integrate this over
\Omega
, apply the divergence theorem, and use the condition
\mathrm{Tr}_{\partial\Omega}(X\cdot e_{n})=0
to get (
B.2.9
). The case of general
\varphi\in H^{0}_{X}(\Omega)
follows from a density argument via Lemma
B.8
. The second item is proved via a similar density argument.
∎

Next we prove a useful estimate.

Proposition B.10

(Refined divergence compatibility estimate)
.

Suppose that
X\in W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)
and
\varphi\in H^{0}_{X}(\Omega)
. Then the function
\int_{0}^{b}\nabla\cdot(X\varphi)(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y:\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\to\mathbb{R}
belongs to
\dot{H}^{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
, satisfies the equality

(B.2.11)

\int_{0}^{b}\nabla\cdot(X\varphi)(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y=(\nabla_{\|},0)\cdot\int_{0}^{b}(X\varphi)(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y,

and obeys the estimate

(B.2.12)

\Big[{\int_{0}^{b}\nabla\cdot(X\varphi)(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y}\Big]_{\dot{H}^{-1}}\lesssim\lVert X\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}}

for an implicit constant depending only on
b
and the dimension.

Proof.

Let
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
and view
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\overline{\Omega})
via the trivial extension
\psi(x,y)=\psi(x)
for
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times(0,b)
. Thanks to the second item of Proposition
B.9
and Fubini’s theorem, we have that

(B.2.13)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}\Big(\int_{0}^{b}\nabla\cdot(X\varphi)(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y\Big)\psi=\int_{\Omega}\nabla\cdot(X\varphi)\psi=-\int_{\Omega}\varphi X\cdot\nabla\psi\\
=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}\Big(\int_{0}^{b}(\varphi X)(\cdot,y);\mathrm{d}y\Big)\cdot(\nabla_{\|}\psi,0).

Since
\psi
was arbitrary, the identity (
B.2.11
) is established. The estimate (
B.2.12
) readily follows from (
B.2.11
) and the definition of the norm on
\dot{H}^{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
.
∎

Appendix C
A selection of PDE tools

C.1.
The divergence boundary value problem

Our focus in this subsection is the construction of right-inverses of the divergence operator in various spaces. We first make the following notation: for
s\in[0,\infty)
, we define the function space

(C.1.1)

\hat{H}^{s}(\Omega)=\Big\{{\phi\in H^{s}(\Omega)\;:\;\int_{0}^{b}\phi(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y\in\dot{H}^{-1}(\Sigma)}\Big\},

where the homogeneous spaces
\dot{H}^{-1}
are defined in (
1.7.4
). This space is Hilbert for the norm
\lVert\phi\rVert_{\hat{H}^{s}}=\big(\lVert\phi\rVert^{2}_{H^{s}}+\big[{\int_{0}^{b}\phi(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y}\big]_{\dot{H}^{-1}}^{2}\big)^{1/2}
.

We now provide a useful compatibility estimate related to the divergence and the normal trace.

Proposition C.1

(Divergence-normal trace compatibility condition)
.

If
u\in H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then

(C.1.2)

\Big[{\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma}u\cdot e_{n}-\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}u\cdot e_{n}-\int_{0}^{b}(\nabla\cdot u)(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y}\Big]_{\dot{H}^{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{L^{2}}.

Proof.

Thanks to the fundamental theorem of calculus, we have

(C.1.3)

\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma}u\cdot e_{n}-\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}u\cdot e_{n}-\int_{0}^{b}(\nabla\cdot u)(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y=-(\nabla_{\|},0)\cdot\int_{0}^{b}u(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y.

Estimate (
C.1.2
) now follows by definition of the norm on
\dot{H}^{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
(see (
1.7.4
)).
∎

The rest of this subsection is devoted building solution operators to divergence boundary value problems.

Proposition C.2

(Solution operators to divergence boundary value problems)
.

There exist bounded linear operators
\mathcal{B}_{0}
and
\mathcal{B}_{1}
such that for
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

(C.1.4)

\mathcal{B}_{0}:\hat{H}^{k}(\Omega)\to H^{1+k}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap H^{1}_{0}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})

and

(C.1.5)

\mathcal{B}_{1}:\{{\varphi\in H^{1/2+k}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}^{n})\;:\;\varphi\cdot e_{n}\in\dot{H}^{-1}(\Sigma)}\}\to H^{1+k}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap{{}_{0}}H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n}),

with the properties

(C.1.6)

\begin{cases}\nabla\cdot\mathcal{B}_{0}\psi=\psi&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\mathcal{B}_{0}\psi=0&\text{on }\partial\Omega,\end{cases}\quad\text{and}\quad\begin{cases}\nabla\cdot\mathcal{B}_{1}\varphi=0&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\mathcal{B}_{1}\varphi=\varphi&\text{on }\Sigma,\\
\mathcal{B}_{1}\varphi=0&\text{on }\Sigma_{0}.\end{cases}

Proof.

We begin with the construction of
\mathcal{B}_{0}
. Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\psi\in\hat{H}^{k}(\Omega)
. According to the Tonelli and Parseval theorems, we have that

(C.1.7)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}\sum_{j=0}^{k}\int_{0}^{b}\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{2(k-j)}\char 12\relax{\partial_{n}^{j}\mathscr{F}[\psi](\xi,y)}^{2}\;\mathrm{d}y\;\mathrm{d}\xi\asymp\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert_{H^{k}}^{2},

and in particular this implies that for almost every
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
we have that

(C.1.8)

\sum_{j=0}^{k}\int_{0}^{b}\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{2(k-j)}\char 12\relax{\partial_{n}^{j}\mathscr{F}[\psi](\xi,y)}^{2}\;\mathrm{d}y<\infty.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that, in fact, this quantity is finite for every
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
. We then define the measurable function
\zeta:\Omega\to\mathbb{C}
via

(C.1.9)

\zeta(\xi,y)=\frac{\cosh(2\pi\left|\xi\right|y)}{2\pi\left|\xi\right|\sinh(2\pi\left|\xi\right|b)}\int_{0}^{b}\mathscr{F}[\psi](\xi,t)\cosh(2\pi\left|\xi\right|(b-t))\;\mathrm{d}t\\
-\int_{0}^{y}\mathscr{F}[\psi](\xi,t)\frac{\sinh(2\pi\left|\xi\right|(y-t))}{2\pi\left|\xi\right|}\;\mathrm{d}t.

It is a simple matter to verify that for each
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\backslash\{0\}
we have the inclusion
\zeta(\xi,\cdot)\in H^{2}((0,b);\mathbb{C})
and that

(C.1.10)

\begin{cases}-\partial_{n}^{2}\zeta(\xi,\cdot)+4\pi^{2}\left|\xi\right|^{2}\zeta(\xi,\cdot)=\mathscr{F}[\psi](\xi,\cdot)&\text{in }(0,b)\\
\partial_{n}\zeta(\xi,t)=0&\text{for }t\in\{0,b\}.\end{cases}

Multiplying the ODE satisfied by
\zeta
by
\overline{\zeta}
and integrating by parts over
(0,b)
, we see that

(C.1.11)

\int_{0}^{b}(\left|\partial_{n}\zeta(\xi,y)\right|^{2}+4\pi^{2}\left|\xi\right|^{2}\left|\zeta(\xi,y)\right|^{2})\;\mathrm{d}y=\int_{0}^{b}\mathscr{F}[\psi](\xi,y)\overline{\zeta(\xi,y)}\;\mathrm{d}y\\
=\int_{0}^{b}\mathscr{F}[\psi](\xi,y)(\overline{\zeta(\xi,y)}-\langle\overline{\zeta(\xi,\cdot)}\rangle_{(0,b)})\;\mathrm{d}y+\langle\overline{\zeta(\xi,\cdot)}\rangle_{(0,b)}\int_{0}^{b}\mathscr{F}[\psi](\xi)\;\mathrm{d}y.

In the above we have written
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{(0,b)}
to be the integral average over
(0,b)
. By the Poincaré, Cauchy-Schwarz, and Cauchy inequalities, we may then bound

(C.1.12)

\Big|\int_{0}^{b}\mathscr{F}[\psi](\xi,y)\overline{\zeta(\xi,y)}\;\mathrm{d}y\Big|\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{b}(\left|\partial_{n}\zeta(\xi,y)\right|^{2}+4\pi^{2}\left|\xi\right|^{2}\left|\zeta(\xi,y)\right|^{2})\;\mathrm{d}y\\
+C\int_{0}^{b}\char 12\relax{\mathscr{F}[\psi](\xi,y)}^{2}\;\mathrm{d}y+C\Big|\int_{0}^{b}\frac{\mathscr{F}[\psi](\xi,y)}{\left|\xi\right|}\;\mathrm{d}y\Big|^{2}

for a constant
C>0
depending only on
b
. On the other hand, a similar argument shows that

(C.1.13)

\int_{0}^{b}(\left|\xi\right|^{2(k+1)}\left|\partial_{n}\zeta(\xi,y)\right|^{2}+4\pi^{2}\left|\xi\right|^{2(k+2)}\left|\zeta(\xi,y)\right|^{2})\;\mathrm{d}y=\int_{0}^{b}\left|\xi\right|^{k}\mathscr{F}[\psi](\xi,y)\left|\xi\right|^{k+2}\overline{\zeta(\xi,y)}\;\mathrm{d}y\\
\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{b}4\pi^{2}\left|\xi\right|^{2(k+2)}\left|\zeta(\xi,y)\right|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}y+C\int_{0}^{b}\left|\xi\right|^{2k}\char 12\relax{\mathscr{F}[\psi](\xi,y)}^{2}\;\mathrm{d}y

for a constant
C>0
depending only on
b
. Upon combining these bounds, we deduce that
\zeta(\xi,\cdot)
obeys the estimate

(C.1.14)

\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{2(k+1)}\int_{0}^{b}(\left|\partial_{n}\zeta(\xi,y)\right|^{2}+4\pi^{2}\left|\xi\right|^{2}\char 12\relax{\zeta(\xi,y)}^{2})\;\mathrm{d}y\lesssim\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{2k}\int_{0}^{b}\char 12\relax{\mathscr{F}[\psi](\xi,y)}^{2}\;\mathrm{d}y+\Big|\int_{0}^{b}\frac{\mathscr{F}[\psi](\xi,y)}{\left|\xi\right|}\;\mathrm{d}y\Big|^{2}.

Returning to (
C.1.10
) and exploiting (
C.1.8
) and a simple finite induction argument, we deduce that
\zeta(\xi,\cdot)\in H^{2+k}((0,b);\mathbb{C})
and that for
0\leqslant j\leqslant k
we have the bound

(C.1.15)

\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{2(k-j)}\int_{0}^{b}\char 12\relax{\partial_{n}^{2+j}\zeta(\xi,y)}^{2}\;\mathrm{d}y\lesssim\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{2(k-j)}\int_{0}^{b}\char 12\relax{\partial_{n}^{j}\mathscr{F}[\psi](\xi,y)}^{2}\;\mathrm{d}y\\
+\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{2(k-j+1)}4\pi^{2}\char 12\relax{\xi}^{2}\int_{0}^{b}\char 12\relax{\partial_{n}^{j}\zeta(\xi,y)}^{2}\;\mathrm{d}y.

We may then take appropriate linear combinations of these estimates, for
0\leqslant j\leqslant k
, and (
C.1.14
), in order to deduce the bound

(C.1.16)

\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{2(k+1)}\int_{0}^{b}4\pi^{2}\left|\xi\right|^{2}\char 12\relax{\zeta(\xi,y)}^{2}\;\mathrm{d}y+\sum_{j=1}^{2+k}\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{2(k+2-j)}\int_{0}^{b}\char 12\relax{\partial_{n}^{j}\zeta(\xi,y)}^{2}\;\mathrm{d}y\\
\lesssim\sum_{j=0}^{k}\int_{0}^{b}\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{2(k-j)}\char 12\relax{\partial_{n}^{j}\mathscr{F}[\psi](\xi,y)}^{2}\;\mathrm{d}y+\Big|\int_{0}^{b}\frac{\mathscr{F}[\psi](\xi,y)}{\left|\xi\right|}\;\mathrm{d}y\Big|^{2}.

Next, we define the vector field
\Xi:\Omega\to\mathbb{C}^{n}
via
\Xi(\xi,y)=(-2\pi i\xi\zeta(\xi,y),-\partial_{n}\zeta(\xi,y))
. Since
\psi
is real-valued we know that
\overline{\mathscr{F}[\psi](\xi,y)}=\mathscr{F}[\psi](-\xi,y)
for
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
and
y\in(0,b)
, and this readily implies that
\overline{\Xi(\xi,y)}=\Xi(-\xi,y)
as well. The bound (
C.1.16
), integrated over
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
, implies that
\Xi\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{n})
, and so we may define
X\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
via
X=\mathscr{F}^{-1}[\Xi]
. In turn, (
C.1.16
) and (
C.1.7
) further imply that
X\in H^{1+k}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
and

(C.1.17)

\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{H^{1+k}}\lesssim\Big(\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert_{H^{k}}^{2}+\Big[{\int_{0}^{b}\psi(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y}\Big]_{\dot{H}^{-1}}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}=\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert_{\hat{H}^{k}}.

We use (
C.1.10
) to compute
\mathscr{F}[\nabla\cdot X](\xi,y)=(2\pi i\xi,0)\cdot\Xi(\xi,y)+\partial_{n}\Xi\cdot e_{n}(\xi,y)=-\partial_{n}^{2}\zeta(\xi,y)+4\pi^{2}\left|\xi\right|^{2}\zeta(\xi,y)=\mathscr{F}[\psi](\xi,y)
and
\mathscr{F}[X](\xi,t)=\Xi\cdot e_{n}(\xi,t)=0\text{ for }t\in\{0,b\}
. Thus,
X
satisfies

(C.1.18)

\begin{cases}\nabla\cdot X=\psi&\text{in }\Omega,\\
X\cdot e_{n}=0&\text{on }\partial\Omega.\end{cases}

We next recall that standard Sobolev trace theory (see for instance Chapter 7 in Adams and Fournier
[
2
]
or Chapter 18 in Leoni
[
59
]
) shows that the trace map

(C.1.19)

H^{2+k}(\Omega)\ni f\mapsto(\mathrm{Tr}_{\partial\Omega}[f],\mathrm{Tr}_{\partial\Omega}[\partial_{n}f])\in H^{3/2+k}(\partial\Omega)\times H^{1/2+k}(\partial\Omega)

is bounded and linear and admits a bounded right inverse,
\mathcal{R}
. Consequently, we may define
\omega\in H^{k+2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
via
\omega=(\mathcal{R}(0,-X\cdot e_{1}),\dots,\mathcal{R}(0,-X\cdot e_{n-1}))
. Finally, this allows us to define
\mathcal{B}_{0}\psi\in H^{1+k}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
via
\mathcal{B}_{0}\psi=X+(\partial_{n}\omega,\nabla_{\|}\cdot\omega)
. Then from (
C.1.18
) and the construction of
\omega
we have that
\nabla\cdot\mathcal{B}_{0}\psi=\nabla\cdot X+\nabla_{\|}\cdot\partial_{n}\omega-\partial_{n}\nabla_{\|}\cdot\omega=\psi
in
\Omega,
while on
\partial\Omega
we have for
j\in\{{1,\dots,n-1}\}

(\mathcal{B}_{0}\psi)\cdot e_{j}=X\cdot e_{j}+\partial_{n}\omega\cdot e_{j}=X\cdot e_{j}-X\cdot e_{j}=0
.
Additionally we have
(\mathcal{B}_{0}\psi)\cdot e_{n}=X\cdot e_{n}-\nabla_{\|}\cdot\omega=X\cdot e_{n}=0
. Thus,
\mathcal{B}_{0}\psi
satisfies the properties stated in (
C.1.6
). Moreover, the construction of
\mathcal{B}_{0}\psi
shows that the resulting map
\psi\mapsto\mathcal{B}_{0}\psi
is linear and satisfies
\left\lVert\mathcal{B}_{0}\psi\right\rVert_{H^{k+1}}\lesssim\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert_{\hat{H}^{k}}
. This completes the construction of the bounded linear map
\mathcal{B}_{0}
.

We now build
\mathcal{B}_{1}
. To this end, we write
L
for a bounded right inverse of the trace map

(C.1.20)

H^{1+k}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap{{}_{0}}H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})\ni u\mapsto\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma}(u)\in H^{1/2+k}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}^{n}),

which again exists by standard trace theory. Note that if
\varphi\in H^{1/2+k}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\varphi\cdot e_{n}\in\dot{H}^{-1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then

(C.1.21)

\int_{0}^{b}\nabla\cdot(L\varphi)(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y=(\nabla_{\|},0)\cdot\int_{0}^{b}(L\varphi)(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y+\varphi\cdot e_{n}(\cdot),

and we readily deduce from this that

(C.1.22)

\Big[{\int_{0}^{b}\nabla\cdot(L\varphi)(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y}\Big]_{\dot{H}^{-1}}\lesssim\lVert L\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}}+[{\varphi\cdot e_{n}}]_{\dot{H}^{-1}}\lesssim\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{H^{1/2+k}}+[{\varphi\cdot e_{n}}]_{\dot{H}^{-1}}.

We may thus define the linear map
\mathcal{B}_{1}
via
\mathcal{B}_{1}\varphi=L\varphi-\mathcal{B}_{0}\nabla\cdot L\varphi
. It is then a simple matter to verify that
\mathcal{B}_{1}
is bounded and
\mathcal{B}_{1}\varphi
satisfies the conditions stated in (
C.1.6
). This completes the construction of
\mathcal{B}_{1}
.
∎

We next record a couple corollaries. The first constructs an operator on
H^{k}(\Omega)
.

Corollary C.3

(Right inverse to the divergence)
.

There exists a bounded linear operator such that for
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{B}:H^{k}(\Omega)\to H^{1+k}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap{{}_{0}}H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})

with
\nabla\cdot\mathcal{B}\psi=\psi
for all
\psi\in H^{k}(\Omega)
.

Proof.

If for
p\in\mathbb{N}
we define
A_{p}\psi:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
via
A_{p}\psi(x,y)=\frac{y^{p}}{b}\int_{0}^{b}\psi(x,t)\;\mathrm{d}t
, then
A_{p}\psi\in H^{k}(\Omega)
and
\left\lVert A_{p}\psi\right\rVert_{H^{k}}\lesssim_{p}\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert_{H^{k}}
. In other words,
A_{p}\in\mathcal{L}(H^{k}(\Omega))
.

Note that for
\psi\in H^{k}(\Omega)
we have that
\int_{0}^{b}(\psi(\cdot,y)-A_{0}\psi(\cdot,y))\;\mathrm{d}y=\int_{0}^{b}\psi(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y-\int_{0}^{b}\psi(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y=0
. Consequently, we may construct
\mathcal{B}_{0}(\psi-A_{0}\psi)\in H^{1+k}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap H^{1}_{0}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
by using the operator
\mathcal{B}_{0}
from Proposition
C.2
. We then define
\mathcal{B}:H^{k}(\Omega)\to H^{1+k}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap{{}_{0}}H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
via
\mathcal{B}\psi=\mathcal{B}_{0}(\psi-A_{0}\psi)+(A_{1}\psi)e_{n}
. It is then a simple matter to check that this is bounded and linear and satisfies
\nabla\cdot\mathcal{B}\psi=\psi
for all
\psi\in H^{k}(\Omega)
.
∎

The second corollary to Proposition
C.2
constructs an operator on
H^{1/2+k}(\Sigma)\cap\dot{H}^{-1}(\Sigma)
.

Corollary C.4

(Solenoidal extension operator)
.

There exists a bounded linear operator such that for
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{B}_{2}:H^{1/2+k}(\Sigma)\cap\dot{H}^{-1}(\Sigma)\to{{}_{0}}H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
with
\nabla\cdot\mathcal{B}_{2}\chi=0
,
\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{B}_{2}\chi\cdot e_{n})=\chi
, and
(I-e_{n}\otimes e_{n})\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{B}_{2}\chi)=0
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{B}_{1}
be as in Proposition
C.2
, and define
\mathcal{B}_{2}
via
\mathcal{B}_{2}\chi=\mathcal{B}_{1}(\chi e_{n})
.
∎

C.2.
Elliptic theory tools

We begin this subsection by recording some results about the bilinear form
B_{m}
from (
4.1.1
). While
B_{m}
is not coercive, the next result shows that it satisfies Gårding’s inequality.

Lemma C.5

(
B_{m}
Gårding inequalities)
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
\Lambda\in W^{\infty,\infty}(\Omega)
with
\Lambda>0
and
1/\Lambda\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
. There exist constants
c,C\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, depending only on
\Lambda
, the dimension,
\Omega
, and
m
, such that
c\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{m}}^{2}\leqslant B_{m}(\Lambda\varphi,\varphi)+C\lVert\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}
for all
\varphi\in H^{m}(\Omega)
.

Proof.

Consider first the case that
\Lambda=1
. We decompose
B_{m}=B_{m,\|}+B_{m,n}
where

(C.2.1)

B_{m,\|}(\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1})=\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\int_{\Omega}\partial_{j}^{m}\varphi_{0}\partial_{j}^{m}\varphi_{1}\text{ and }B_{m,n}(\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1})=\int_{\Omega}\partial_{n}^{m}\varphi_{0}\partial_{n}^{m}\varphi_{1}.

For
B_{m,\|}
, we use Fubini’s theorem followed by Plancherel’s theorem to estimate

(C.2.2)

B_{m,\|}(\varphi,\varphi)=\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\int_{0}^{b}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}|(2\pi\mathrm{i}\xi_{j})^{m}\mathscr{F}[\varphi(\cdot,y)](\xi)|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\xi\;\mathrm{d}y\\
=\int_{0}^{b}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}|(2\pi\mathrm{i}\xi_{j})^{m}|^{2}}{\sum_{|\alpha|=m}|(2\pi\mathrm{i}\xi)^{\alpha}|^{2}}\sum_{|\alpha|=m}|(2\pi\mathrm{i}\xi)^{\alpha}\mathscr{F}[\varphi(\cdot,y)](\xi)|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\xi\;\mathrm{d}y\\
\gtrsim_{m}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{n-1}\\
|\beta|=m\end{subarray}}\lVert\partial^{(\beta,0)}\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}=\lVert\varphi\rVert_{\dot{H}^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1};L^{2}(0,b))}^{2}.

Hence, by the fact that
\dot{H}^{m}\cap L^{2}=H^{m}
, we get that

(C.2.3)

B_{m,\|}(\varphi,\varphi)+\lVert\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\gtrsim_{m}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1};L^{2}(0,b))}^{2}.

On the other hand, for the
B_{m,n}
piece, we see that

(C.2.4)

B_{m,n}(\varphi,\varphi)=\lVert\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1};\dot{H}^{m}(0,b))}^{2}.

Thus, by again using that
\dot{H}^{m}\cap L^{2}=H^{m}
, we get the bound

(C.2.5)

B_{m,n}(\varphi,\varphi)+\lVert\varphi\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}\gtrsim_{\Omega,m}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1};H^{m}(0,b))}^{2}.

Upon combining (
C.2.3
) and (
C.2.5
), we arrive at the estimate

(C.2.6)

B_{m}(\varphi,\varphi)+\lVert\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\gtrsim_{m,\Omega}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{m}L^{2}\cap L^{2}H^{m}}^{2}.

The proof in the case that
\Lambda=1
is then complete as soon as we note the slicing characterization

(C.2.7)

H^{m}(\Omega)=H^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1};L^{2}(0,b))\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1};H^{m}(0,b)),

with norm equivalence, which can be proved as follows. The slicing characterization for
\mathbb{R}^{n}
in place of
\Omega
is proved in Lemma A.6 from Leoni and Tice
[
60
]
. We then reduce (
C.2.7
) to this case with the help of a Stein extension operator
\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}
(see Definition
A.2
), which continuously maps both
H^{m}(\Omega)\to H^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
H^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1};L^{2}(0,b))\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};H^{m}(0,b))\to H^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1};L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};H^{m}(\mathbb{R}))
.

We next consider the case of general
\Lambda
satisfying the stated hypotheses. We introduce commutators as follows:

(C.2.8)

B_{m}(\Lambda\varphi,\varphi)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{\Omega}\Lambda(\partial_{j}^{m}\varphi)^{2}+[\Lambda,\partial_{j}^{m}]\varphi\partial_{j}^{m}\varphi.

Hence, we have the estimate

(C.2.9)

cB_{m}(\varphi,\varphi)\leqslant B_{m}(\Lambda\varphi,\varphi)+\sqrt{B_{m}(\varphi,\varphi)}\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lVert[\Lambda,\partial_{j}^{m}]\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}}.

By Cauchy’s inequality and the boundedness of the commutator operator (see Corollary
D.8
),

(C.2.10)

cB_{m}(\varphi,\varphi)\leqslant B_{m}(\Lambda\varphi,\varphi)+C\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{m-1}}^{2}.

Invoking the special case
\Lambda=1
then shows that

(C.2.11)

c\lVert\varphi\rVert^{2}_{H^{m}}\leqslant C\lVert\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+B_{m}(\Lambda\varphi,\varphi)+C\lVert\varphi\rVert^{2}_{H^{m-1}}.

For the final term above, we use the log-convexity of the norm and Young’s inequality: for
\kappa\in(0,1)
we estimate
\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{m-1}}\lesssim\kappa^{-m}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}}+\kappa\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{m}}
and then choose
\kappa
sufficiently small to absorb the right hand side’s
\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{m}}
-contribution by the left.
∎

Our next lemma involves integration by parts.

Lemma C.6

(
B_{m}
integration by parts)
.

Suppose that
\psi\in H^{2m}(\Omega)
and
\phi\in H^{m}(\Omega)
. Then

(C.2.12)

B_{m}(\phi,\psi)=\int_{\Omega}\phi L_{m}\psi+\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}\Big(\int_{\Sigma}-\int_{\Sigma_{0}}\Big)(-1)^{m-1-j}\partial_{n}^{j}\phi\partial_{n}^{2m-j-1}\psi,

where
L_{m}
is the linear elliptic operator defined in (
3.5.6
).

Proof.

This is a simple exercise in integration by parts.
∎

Now we prove a priori estimates for the natural Neumann problem associated with the operator
L_{m}
.

Lemma C.7

(A priori estimates for
L_{m}
)
.

Suppose that
m\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\psi\in H^{k}(\Omega)
, and
\varphi\in H^{k+2m}(\Omega)
are related via the equations

(C.2.13)

\begin{cases}L_{m}\varphi=\psi&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\partial_{n}^{m}\varphi=\cdots=\partial_{n}^{2m-1}\varphi=0&\text{on }\partial\Omega.\end{cases}

Then we have the a priori estimate

(C.2.14)

\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{k+2m}}\lesssim\lVert\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}}+\lVert\psi\rVert_{H^{k}},

where the implicit constant depends only on
k
,
m
, and
\Omega
.

Proof.

We begin by proving the case
k=0
. By taking the
L^{2}
-inner product of the equation with
\varphi
and utilizing Lemma
C.6
, we are left with

(C.2.15)

B_{m}(\varphi,\varphi)=\int_{\Omega}\psi\varphi.

For the right hand side we use the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, while for the left hand side we use the Gårding inequality from Lemma
C.5
. This yields the inequality

(C.2.16)

\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{2m}}\lesssim\lVert\varphi,\psi\rVert_{L^{2}\times L^{2}}.

We now induct on
k\in\mathbb{N}
. We have already established the base case,
k=0
. Suppose that
k\in\mathbb{N}
and that whenever
\varphi\in H^{k+2m}(\Omega)
and
\psi\in H^{k}(\Omega)
are related via the equations (
C.2.13
), we have the a priori estimate

(C.2.17)

\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{k+2m}}\lesssim\lVert\varphi,\psi\rVert_{H^{k}\times H^{k}}.

We now prove the above necessarily holds for
k+1
as well. Assume
\varphi\in H^{1+k+2m}(\Omega)
and
\psi\in H^{1+k}(\Omega)
satisfy (
C.2.13
). Let
j\in\{{1,\dots,n-1}\}
and apply
\partial_{j}
to these equations. We then see that

(C.2.18)

\begin{cases}L_{m}\partial_{j}\varphi=\partial_{j}\psi&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\partial_{n}^{m}\partial_{j}\varphi=\cdots=\partial_{n}^{2m-1}\partial_{j}\varphi=0&\text{on }\partial\Omega.\end{cases}

Hence, we can apply the inductive hypothesis and sum over
j
to arrive at the bounds

(C.2.19)

\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\lVert\partial_{j}\varphi\rVert_{H^{k+2m}}\lesssim\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\lVert\partial_{j}\varphi,\partial_{j}\psi\rVert_{H^{k}\times H^{k}}\leqslant\lVert\varphi,\psi\rVert_{H^{1+k}\times H^{1+k}}.

On the other hand, we can rearrange the PDE satisfied by
\varphi
and
\psi
to see that
(-1)^{m}\partial_{n}^{2m}\varphi=\psi-(-1)^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\partial_{j}^{2m}\varphi
. Thus,

(C.2.20)

\lVert\partial_{n}^{2m}\varphi\rVert_{H^{1+k}}\leqslant\lVert\psi\rVert_{H^{1+k}}+\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\lVert\partial_{j}^{2m}\varphi\rVert_{H^{1+k}}\leqslant\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{1+k}}+\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\lVert\partial_{j}\varphi\rVert_{H^{k+2m}}.

Combining (
C.2.19
) and (
C.2.20
) then shows that

(C.2.21)

\sqrt{B_{1+k+2m}(\varphi,\varphi)}\leqslant\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\lVert\partial_{j}\varphi\rVert_{H^{k+2m}}+\lVert\partial_{n}^{2m}\varphi\rVert_{H^{1+k}}\lesssim\lVert\varphi,\psi\rVert_{H^{1+k}\times H^{1+k}}.

Finally, we use Gårding’s inequality, Lemma
C.5
, to see that

(C.2.22)

\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{1+k+2m}}\lesssim\lVert\varphi,\psi\rVert_{H^{1+k}\times H^{1+k}}.

This completes the induction argument.

From the induction, we know that a weaker version of (
C.2.14
) holds, namely the same estimate but with
\lVert\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}}
replaced by
\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{k}}
. However, we can readily derive the desired bound from this weaker one by using interpolation, Young’s inequality, and absorption: for
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
we have that

(C.2.23)

\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{k}}\lesssim\lVert\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}}^{\frac{2m}{k+2m}}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{k+2m}}^{\frac{k}{k+2m}}\lesssim\kappa\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{k+2m}}+\kappa^{-\frac{k}{2m}}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}}.

∎

If we add a
0^{\mathrm{th}}
term to
L_{m}
, we obtain an existence theory.

Lemma C.8

(Existence theory for
L_{m}
)
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
and
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
. The operator

(C.2.24)

\kappa+L_{m}:\{{\varphi\in H^{2m}(\Omega)\;:\;\mathrm{Tr}_{\partial\Omega}(\partial_{n}^{m}\varphi)=\cdots=\mathrm{Tr}_{\partial\Omega}(\partial_{n}^{2m-1}\varphi)=0}\}\to L^{2}(\Omega)

is a Banach isomorphism.

Proof.

The proof of Lemma
C.5
, paired with the semi-definiteness of
B_{m}
, shows that the symmetric bilinear map

(C.2.25)

H^{m}(\Omega)\times H^{m}(\Omega)\ni(\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1})\mapsto\int_{\Omega}\kappa\varphi_{0}\varphi_{1}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{\Omega}\partial_{j}^{m}\varphi_{0}\partial_{j}^{m}\varphi_{1}\in\mathbb{R}

is bounded and coercive, and thus defines an inner-product on
H^{m}(\Omega)
. The Riesz representation theorem then provides for the existence of unique weak solutions, but then standard elliptic regularity arguments (which are elementary in
\Omega
since we can use horizontal difference quotients without cutoffs) allow us to promote the regularity of weak solutions to
H^{2m}(\Omega)
, provided the data lie in
L^{2}(\Omega)
. In turn, by integrating by parts, we verify that such weak solutions satisfy the boundary conditions. From this we readily deduce that the operator is an isomorphism between the stated spaces.
∎

We next record a result about inverting a particular pseudodifferential operator.

Lemma C.9

(Simple symbol inversion)
.

Fix
s,\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
with
\sigma\geqslant 0
and
M,N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
. Suppose that
\psi\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. There exists a unique
\varphi\in H^{s+\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

(C.2.26)

(N^{-1}(M^{-1}+(-\Delta)^{\sigma/2})-\partial_{1})\varphi=\psi.

Moreover we have the estimate

(C.2.27)

\lVert(NM)^{-1}\varphi,N^{-1}|\nabla|^{\sigma}\varphi\rVert_{H^{s}\times H^{s}}\lesssim\lVert\psi\rVert_{H^{s}}

for implicit constants depending only on
\sigma
.

Proof.

The symbol of the pseudodifferential operator in (
C.2.26
) is

(C.2.28)

\mathbb{R}^{d}\ni\xi\mapsto a(\xi)=(MN)^{-1}\langle 2\pi\sqrt{M|\xi|^{\sigma}}\rangle^{2}-2\pi\mathrm{i}\xi\cdot e_{1}\in\mathbb{C}.

The stated estimate then follows readily from the elementary estimate

(C.2.29)

\frac{1}{|a(\xi)|}\leqslant\frac{MN}{\langle 2\pi\sqrt{M|\xi|^{\sigma}}\rangle^{2}}\leqslant\frac{N}{2\pi}\min\big\{{M,|\xi|^{-\sigma}}\big\}\text{ for }\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

∎

C.3.
Dissipation calculation for traveling compressible Navier-Stokes

This subsection explores the role of forcing in the traveling wave problem (
1.4.9
). We will require the following density result.

Lemma C.10

(Density of smooth functions with bounded support)
.

For any
s\in\{-1,0\}\cup\mathbb{R}^{+}
, the subspace

(C.3.1)

\{(q,u,\eta)\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega})\times C^{\infty}_{c}(\overline{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{n})\times C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})\;:\;\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}u=0,\text{ and }\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma}u\cdot e_{n}+\partial_{1}\eta=0\}

is dense in
\mathbb{X}^{s}
, as defined by (
3.1.3
).

Proof.

First, we note that Theorem 5.2 and the second item of Theorem 5.6 in Leoni and Tice
[
60
]
imply that
H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})\subset\mathcal{H}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
is a continuous and dense inclusion for
r\geqslant 0
. On the other hand,
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
is dense in
H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
. These facts combine to give the density of
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
in
\mathcal{H}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
. In turn, we deduce that

(C.3.2)

C^{\infty}_{c}(\overline{\Omega})\times C^{\infty}_{c}(\overline{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{n})\times C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})\subset\mathbb{X}_{s}

is a dense inclusion, where the latter space is defined by (
3.1.1
).

It remains to handle the boundary conditions that define the subspace
\mathbb{X}^{s}\subset\mathbb{X}_{s}
. For this we will modify the map
\rho_{\mathbb{X}}
from Lemma
3.2
. Let
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
be such that
0\leqslant\psi\leqslant 1
,
\supp(\psi)\subseteq(-2,2)
, and
\psi=1
on
(-1,1)
. For
1\leqslant\nu\in\mathbb{R}
we define
\psi_{\nu}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\overline{\Omega})
via
\psi_{\nu}(x,y)=\psi(|x/\nu|^{2})
for
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times(0,b)
. Next, we define
\Pi_{\nu}:\mathbb{X}_{s}\to\mathbb{X}_{s}
via

(C.3.3)

\Pi_{\nu}(q,u,\eta)=(q,u-\psi_{\nu}\mathcal{E}_{1}(\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}u,\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma}(u\cdot e_{n})+\partial_{1}\eta),\eta),

where
\mathcal{E}_{1}
is the map defined in the proof of Lemma
3.2
. In addition to being linear and bounded (with
\sup_{\nu\geqslant 1}\left\lVert\Pi_{\nu}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{X}_{s})}<\infty
),
\Pi_{\nu}
has the property that if
(q,u,\eta)\in\mathbb{X}_{s}
is smooth and supported in a ball of radius
\nu
(appropriately interpreted for each element of the tuple), then
\Pi_{\nu}(q,u,\eta)
belongs to
\mathbb{X}^{s}
and remains smooth and supported in a ball of radius
2\nu
.

Fix
(q,u,\eta)\in\mathbb{X}^{s}
. From the dense inclusion (
C.3.2
), we are assured of the existence of a sequence
\{{(\widetilde{q}_{N},\widetilde{u}_{N},\widetilde{\eta}_{N})}\}_{N=1}^{\infty}\subset C^{\infty}_{c}(\overline{\Omega})\times C^{\infty}_{c}(\overline{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{n})\times C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
with the
N^{\mathrm{th}}
element of the sequence consisting of functions supported in a ball of radius
1\leqslant R_{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and with the additional property that
(\widetilde{q}_{N},\widetilde{u}_{N},\widetilde{\eta}_{N})\to(q,u,\eta)
in
\mathbb{X}_{s}
as
N\to\infty
. Set
(q_{N},u_{N},\eta_{N})=\Pi_{R_{N}}(\widetilde{q}_{N},\widetilde{u}_{N},\widetilde{\eta}_{N})
. Thanks to the aforementioned properties of
\Pi_{R_{N}}
, we know that
\{{(q_{N},u_{N},\eta_{N})}\}_{N=1}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{X}^{s}
and that this sequence consists of smooth functions with compact support.

By using the boundary conditions implied by the inclusion
(q,u,\eta)\in\mathbb{X}^{s}
, we now check that

(C.3.4)

(q,u,\eta)-(q_{N},u_{N},\eta_{N})=(q-\widetilde{q}_{N},u-\widetilde{u}_{N},\eta-\widetilde{\eta}_{N})\\
-(0,\psi_{R_{N}}\mathcal{E}_{1}(\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}(\widetilde{u}_{N}-u),\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma}(\widetilde{u}_{N}-u)\cdot e_{n}+\partial_{1}(\widetilde{\eta}_{N}-\eta)),0).

Then, in light of the convergence of
\{{(\widetilde{q}_{N},\widetilde{u}_{N},\widetilde{\eta}_{N})}\}_{N=1}^{\infty}
to
(q,u,\eta)
, we conclude from (
C.3.4
) that
(q_{N},u_{N},\eta_{N})\to(q,u,\eta)
in
\mathbb{X}^{s}
as
N\to\infty
.
∎

We now record an important calculation.

Theorem C.11

(Dissipation-power balance for traveling compressible Navier-Stokes)
.

Suppose that
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
\mathbb{N}\ni s\geqslant 1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor
,
(g,f,k)\in\mathbb{Y}^{s}
, and
(q,u,\eta)\in B_{\mathbb{X}^{1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho_{\mathrm{prin}})\cap\mathbb{X}^{1+s}
, where
\rho_{\mathrm{prin}}
is defined in Theorem
3.13
. Further suppose that

(C.3.5)

\begin{cases}\nabla\cdot(\sigma_{q,\eta}(u-M_{\eta}e_{1}))=g&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\gamma^{2}\sigma_{q,\eta}M_{\eta}^{-\mathrm{t}}(((u-M_{\eta}e_{1})\cdot\nabla)(M_{\eta}^{-1}u))+\sigma_{q,\eta}\nabla(q+\mathfrak{g}\eta)&\\
\quad-\gamma M_{\eta}^{-\mathrm{t}}\nabla\cdot(\mathbb{S}^{\sigma_{q,\eta}}_{\mathcal{A}_{\eta}}(M_{\eta}^{-1}u)M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}})=f&\text{in }\Omega,\\
-((P-P_{\mathrm{ext}})\circ\sigma_{q,\eta}-\gamma\mathbb{S}^{\sigma_{q,\eta}}_{\mathcal{A}_{\eta}}(M_{\eta}^{-1}u))M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}}e_{n}-\varsigma\mathscr{H}(\eta)M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}}e_{n}=k&\text{in }\Sigma,\\
u\cdot e_{n}+\partial_{1}\eta=0&\text{on }\Sigma,\\
u=0&\text{on }\Sigma_{0}.\end{cases}

In other words,
\Psi(q,u,\eta,\gamma)=(g,f,k)
, where
\Psi
is the operator given in (
3.3.4
). Then

(C.3.6)

\int_{\Omega}\frac{\gamma}{J_{\eta}}\Big(\frac{\upmu(\sigma_{q,\eta})}{2}|\mathbb{D}^{0}_{M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}}}(M_{\eta}^{-1}u)|^{2}+\uplambda(\sigma_{q,\eta})|\nabla\cdot u|^{2}\Big)=\int_{\Omega}f\cdot u+g(\gamma^{2}|M_{\eta}^{-1}u|^{2}/2+q)\\
+\mathfrak{g}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}\Big(|\nabla_{\|}|^{-1}\int_{0}^{b}g(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y\Big)|\nabla_{\|}|\eta+\int_{\Sigma}k\cdot M_{\eta}^{-1}u,

where for
w:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
differentiable and
M:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
we write

(C.3.7)

\mathbb{D}^{0}_{M}w=\nabla wM^{\mathrm{t}}+M\nabla w^{\mathrm{t}}-\frac{2}{n}(M\nabla)\cdot wI.

Proof.

First, we claim that it suffices to prove (
C.3.6
) under the additional assumption that
(q,u,\eta)
belongs to the space in (
C.3.1
). Indeed, assume that the identity holds in this special case, and let
(q,u,\eta)\in B_{\mathbb{X}^{1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho_{\mathrm{prin}})\cap\mathbb{X}^{1+s}
be generic. Thanks to Lemma
C.10
, there exists a sequence
\{{(q_{N},u_{N},\eta_{N})}\}_{N=1}^{\infty}
, belonging to the space in (
C.3.1
), such that
(q_{N},u_{N},\eta_{N})\to(q,u,\eta)
in
\mathbb{X}^{1+s}
as
N\to\infty
. Due to this convergence, we may assume without loss of generality that the sequence is contained in
B_{\mathbb{X}^{1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho_{\mathrm{prin}})
. We then rewrite the identity
\Psi(q,u,\eta,\gamma)=(g,f,k)
as
\Psi(q_{N},u_{N},\eta_{N},\gamma)=(g_{N},f_{N},k_{N})
, where
g_{N}=g+(\Psi_{1}(q_{N},u_{N},\eta_{N})-\Psi_{1}(q,u,\eta))
,
f_{N}=f+(\Psi_{2}(q_{N},u_{N},\eta_{N},\gamma)-\Psi_{2}(q,u,\eta,\gamma))
, and
k_{N}=k+(\Psi_{3}(q_{N},u_{N},\eta_{N},\gamma)-\Psi_{3}(q,u,\eta,\gamma))
. Thanks to the continuity of the map
\Psi
established in Theorem
3.13
, we have that
(g_{N},f_{N},k_{N})\to(g,f,k)
in the space
\mathbb{Y}^{s}
as
N\to\infty
. Using the special case, we have the identity

(C.3.8)

\int_{\Omega}\frac{\gamma}{J_{\eta_{N}}}\Big(\frac{\upmu(\sigma_{q_{N},\eta_{N}})}{2}|\mathbb{D}^{0}_{M_{\eta_{N}}^{\mathrm{t}}}(M_{\eta_{N}}^{-1}u_{N})|^{2}+\uplambda(\sigma_{q_{N},\eta_{N}})|\nabla\cdot u_{N}|^{2}\Big)=\int_{\Omega}f_{N}\cdot u_{N}+g_{N}(\gamma^{2}|M_{\eta_{N}}^{-1}u_{N}|^{2}/2+q_{N})\\
+\int_{\Sigma}k_{N}\cdot M_{\eta_{N}}^{-1}u_{N}+\mathfrak{g}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}\Big(|\nabla_{\|}|^{-1}\int_{0}^{b}g_{N}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y\Big)|\nabla_{\|}|\eta_{N}

for every
N
. Since
(q_{N},u_{N},\eta_{N})\to(q,u,\eta)
in
\mathbb{X}^{1+s}
and
(g_{N},f_{N},k_{N})\to(g,f,k)
in
\mathbb{Y}^{s}
as
N\to\infty
and
s\geqslant 1+\lfloor n/2\rfloor
, it is a simple matter to send
N\to\infty
in (
C.3.8
) to obtain the desired equality (
C.3.6
). This completes the proof of the claim.

We now establish (
C.3.6
) in the special case. We begin by taking the inner product of the second equation in (
C.3.5
) with
u
in
L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Since the vector field
\sigma_{q,\eta}(u-M_{\eta}e_{1})
has divergence
g
and vanishing normal trace, the contribution of the advective derivative is

(C.3.9)

\int_{\Omega}\sigma_{q,\eta}M_{\eta}^{-\mathrm{t}}(((u-M_{\eta}e_{1})\cdot\nabla)(M_{\eta}^{-1}u))\cdot u=-\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{2}\nabla\cdot(\sigma_{q,\eta}(u-M_{\eta}e_{1}))|M_{\eta}^{-1}u|^{2}\\
+\int_{\Sigma}\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{q,\eta}(u-M_{\eta}e_{1})\cdot e_{n}|M_{\eta}^{-1}u|^{2}=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}g|M_{\eta}^{-1}u|^{2}.

The contribution of the viscous stress term is

(C.3.10)

\int_{\Omega}M_{\eta}^{-\mathrm{t}}\nabla\cdot(\mathbb{S}^{\sigma_{q,\eta}}_{\mathcal{A}_{\eta}}(M_{\eta}^{-1}u)M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}})\cdot u=\int_{\Sigma}\mathbb{S}^{\sigma_{q,\eta}}_{\mathcal{A}_{\eta}}(M_{\eta}^{-1}u)M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}}e_{n}\cdot M_{\eta}^{-1}u\\
-\int_{\Omega}\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{A}_{\eta}}^{\sigma_{q,\eta}}(M_{\eta}^{-1}u)M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}}:\nabla(M_{\eta}^{-1}u)=\int_{\Sigma}\mathbb{S}^{\sigma_{q,\eta}}_{\mathcal{A}_{\eta}}(M_{\eta}^{-1}u)M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}}e_{n}\cdot M_{\eta}^{-1}u\\
-\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{J_{\eta}}\Big(\frac{\upmu(\sigma_{q,\eta})}{2}|\mathbb{D}^{0}_{M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}}}(M_{\eta}^{-1}u)|^{2}+\uplambda(\sigma_{q,\eta})|\nabla\cdot u|^{2}\Big),

where in the final identity we have used the fact that
\nabla\cdot(J_{\eta}\mathcal{A}_{\eta})=0
. For the pressure contribution, we first write
\sigma_{q,\eta}u=\sigma_{q,\eta}(u-M_{\eta}e_{1})+\sigma_{q,\eta}M_{\eta}e_{1}
, and then integrate by parts to see that

(C.3.11)

\int_{\Omega}\sigma_{q,\eta}\nabla(q+\mathfrak{g}\eta)\cdot u=-\int_{\Omega}g(q+\mathfrak{g}\eta)+\int_{\Omega}\sigma_{q,\eta}M_{\eta}e_{1}\cdot\nabla(q+\mathfrak{g}\eta).

To handle the final term above, we use the definition of
\sigma_{q,\eta}
, which appears in (
1.4.11
), to express

(C.3.12)

q+\mathfrak{g}\eta=H\circ\sigma_{q,\eta}-H\circ\varrho(\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}\cdot e_{n}).

Since
\nabla(\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}\cdot e_{n})=J_{\eta}M_{\eta}^{-\mathrm{t}}e_{n}
, it follows that
M_{\eta}e_{1}\cdot\nabla(H\circ\varrho(\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}\cdot e_{n}))=M_{\eta}e_{1}\cdot\nabla(P\circ\varrho(\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}\cdot e_{n}))=0
. We may then rewrite

(C.3.13)

\int_{\Omega}\sigma_{q,\eta}M_{\eta}e_{1}\cdot\nabla(q+\mathfrak{g}\eta)=\int_{\Omega}M_{\eta}e_{1}\cdot\nabla(P(\sigma_{q,\eta})-P\circ\varrho(\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}\cdot e_{n})).

Since
\nabla\cdot(M_{\eta}e_{1})=0
and
P(\sigma_{q,\eta})-P\circ\varrho(\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}\cdot e_{n})\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega})
, we may integrate by parts once again to see that

(C.3.14)

\int_{\Omega}M_{\eta}e_{1}\cdot\nabla(P(\sigma_{q,\eta})-P\circ\varrho(\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}\cdot e_{n}))=-\int_{\Sigma}(P-P_{\mathrm{ext}})(\sigma_{q,\eta})\partial_{1}\eta+\int_{\Sigma}(P\circ\varrho(\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}\cdot e_{n})-P_{\mathrm{ext}})\partial_{1}\eta.

The final term above vanishes since

(C.3.15)

\int_{\Sigma}(P\circ\varrho(\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}\cdot e_{n})-P_{\mathrm{ext}})\partial_{1}\eta=\int_{\Sigma}\partial_{1}\Big(\int_{0}^{\eta}(P\circ\varrho(b+s))-P_{\mathrm{ext}})\;\mathrm{d}s\Big)=0.

Combining these, we deduce that the contribution of the pressure term in the momentum equation is

(C.3.16)

\int_{\Omega}\sigma_{q,\eta}\nabla(q+\mathfrak{g}\eta)\cdot u=\int_{\Sigma}(P-P_{\mathrm{ext}})(\sigma_{q,\eta})u\cdot e_{n}-\int_{\Omega}g(q+\mathfrak{g}\eta).

Upon synthesizing these calculations, we conclude that

(C.3.17)

\int_{\Omega}f\cdot u+g(\gamma^{2}|M_{\eta}^{-1}u|^{2}/2+q+\mathfrak{g}\eta)\\
-\int_{\Sigma}((P-P_{\mathrm{ext}})(\sigma_{q,\eta})-\gamma\mathbb{S}^{\sigma_{q,\eta}}_{\mathcal{A}_{\eta}}(M_{\eta}^{-1}u))M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}}e_{n}\cdot M_{\eta}^{-1}u\\
=\int_{\Omega}\frac{\gamma}{J_{\eta}}\Big(\frac{\upmu(\sigma_{q,\eta})}{2}|\mathbb{D}^{0}_{M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}}}(M_{\eta}^{-1}u)|^{2}+\uplambda(\sigma_{q,\eta})|\nabla\cdot u|^{2}\Big).

We appeal to the dynamic boundary condition to rewrite

(C.3.18)

-\int_{\Sigma}((P-P_{\mathrm{ext}})(\sigma_{q,\eta})-\gamma\mathbb{S}^{\sigma_{q,\eta}}_{\mathcal{A}_{\eta}}(M_{\eta}^{-1}u))M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}}e_{n}\cdot M_{\eta}^{-1}u\\
=\int_{\Sigma}(k+\varsigma\mathscr{H}(\eta)M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}}e_{n})\cdot M_{\eta}^{-1}u=\int_{\Sigma}k\cdot M_{\eta}^{-1}u+\varsigma\int_{\Sigma}\partial_{1}(\langle\nabla_{\|}\eta\rangle-1)=\int_{\Sigma}k\cdot M_{\eta}^{-1}u.

Finally, from Parseval’s theorem we have the identity

(C.3.19)

\int_{\Omega}g\eta=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}\Big(|\nabla_{\|}|^{-1}\int_{0}^{b}g(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y\Big)|\nabla_{\|}|\eta.

Then (
C.3.6
) follows by combining (
C.3.17
), (
C.3.18
), and (
C.3.19
).
∎

Theorem
C.11
immediately leads to the next result.

Corollary C.12
.

Suppose that
\mathbb{N}\ni s\geqslant 2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor
,
(q,u,\eta)\in\mathbb{X}^{1+s}\cap B_{\mathbb{X}^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho_{\mathrm{WD}})
, and
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F})\in\mathbb{W}_{s}
satisfy (
1.4.9
). Then

(C.3.20)

\int_{\Omega}\frac{\gamma}{J_{\eta}}\Big(\frac{\upmu(\sigma_{q,\eta})}{2}|\mathbb{D}^{0}_{M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}}}(M_{\eta}^{-1}u)|^{2}+\uplambda(\sigma_{q,\eta})|\nabla\cdot u|^{2}\Big)=\int_{\Sigma}\mathcal{T}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}}e_{n}\cdot M_{\eta}^{-1}u\\
+\int_{\Omega}J_{\eta}(\sigma_{q,\eta}\mathcal{G}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}+\mathcal{F}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\eta})\cdot M_{\eta}^{-1}u.

We now combine this result with Korn-type bounds to deduce a useful uniqueness result. Recall that
\rho_{\mathrm{WD}}
is from Theorem
3.17
.

Corollary C.13
.

There exists a
\rho\in(0,\rho_{\mathrm{WD}}]
, depending on
b
,
\mathfrak{g}
,
P
,
\upmu
,
\uplambda
, and
n
, such that if
(q,u,\eta)\in\mathbb{X}^{3+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\cap B_{\mathbb{X}^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho)
satisfies (
1.4.9
) with
\mathcal{T}=0
,
\mathcal{G}=0
, and
\mathcal{F}=0
, then
q=0
,
u=0
, and
\eta=0
.

Proof.

We begin with some general considerations. Propositions
3.10
and
3.11
show that if

(C.3.21)

(q,u,\eta)\in B_{\mathbb{X}^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho_{\mathrm{WD}}),

then
c\leqslant\sigma_{q,\eta}\leqslant C
for some constants
c,C\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, and
1/2\leqslant J_{\eta}\leqslant 3/2
, which in particular implies that
M_{\eta}
is invertible. Assume (
C.3.21
) holds. We may then define

(C.3.22)

\mathfrak{D}_{\eta}=\int_{\Omega}\Big(\frac{\upmu(\varrho)}{2}|\mathbb{D}^{0}_{M_{\eta}^{\mathrm{t}}}(M_{\eta}^{-1}u)|^{2}+\uplambda(\varrho)|\nabla\cdot u|^{2}\Big).

It is a simple matter to check that

(C.3.23)

\mathfrak{D}_{0}\lesssim\mathfrak{D}_{\eta}+g(\left\lVert\eta\right\rVert_{H^{9/2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}})\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{1}}^{2}

for an continuous and increasing function
g:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
such that
g(0)=0
. In light of the properties of
g
and the Korn inequalities from Propositions
A.3
and
A.4
, we may choose
0<\rho\leqslant\rho_{\mathrm{WD}}
such that if
\left\lVert\eta\right\rVert_{H^{9/2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}<\rho
, then the term
g(\left\lVert\eta\right\rVert_{H^{9/2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}})\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{1}}^{2}
may be absorbed onto the left side of (
C.3.23
), resulting in the bound

(C.3.24)

\mathfrak{D}_{0}\lesssim\mathfrak{D}_{\eta}.

Now assume that
(q,u,\eta)\in\mathbb{X}^{3+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\cap B_{\mathbb{X}^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho)
satisfies (
1.4.9
) with
\mathcal{T}=0
and
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{F}=0
. Corollary
C.12
and the above bounds on
\sigma_{q,\eta}
and
J_{\eta}
then imply that
\mathfrak{D}_{\eta}=0
. Then (
C.3.24
) implies that
\mathfrak{D}_{0}=0
, and so we may again appeal to the Korn inequalities to see that
u=0
. The fourth equation in (
1.4.9
) then implies that
\eta=0
, but then the second and third require that
q=0
.
∎

Appendix D
Fine tools for nonlinear analysis

D.1.
Smoothness of superposition nonlinearities

This subsection is concerned with operators between Sobolev spaces involving composition nonlinearities.

Lemma D.1
.

Let
k=2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor
. Suppose that
\Phi:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a bi-Lipschitz homeomorphism and a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism. Then there exists a
\delta>0
, depending on
n
and the Lipschitz seminorm
[\Phi]_{C^{0,1}}
, such that if
g\in W^{k,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
h\in H^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
satisfy

(D.1.1)

\max\{\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{W^{k,\infty}},\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{H^{k}}\}<\delta,

then
\Phi+g+h
is also a bi-Lipschitz homeomorphism and a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism, satisfying the bounds

(D.1.2)

\lVert D(\Phi+g+h)\rVert_{C^{0}_{b}}<3\cdot 2^{-1}\lVert D\Phi\rVert_{C^{0}_{b}}\text{ and }\lVert D(\Phi+g+h)^{-1}\rVert_{C^{0}_{b}}<2\lVert D\Phi^{-1}\rVert_{C^{0}_{b}}.

Proof.

First note that the Banach fixed point theorem implies that if
\Xi:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a Lipschitz map satisfying the bound
[\Xi]_{C^{0,1}}<[\Phi^{-1}]_{C^{0,1}}^{-1}
, then
\Phi+\Xi
is also a bi-Lipschitz homeomorphism. The standard Sobolev embeddings provide a constant
C>0
, depending on
n
, such that
[g+h]_{C^{0,1}}\leqslant\left\lVert g+h\right\rVert_{C^{1}_{b}}\leqslant C(\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{W^{k,\infty}}+\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{H^{k}})
.
Thus, if we set
\delta=(4C[\Phi^{-1}]_{C^{0,1}})^{-1}
,
then the bound (
D.1.1
) implies that
[g+h]_{C^{0,1}}<\frac{1}{2}[\Phi^{-1}]_{C^{0,1}}^{-1}
,
and so
\Phi+g+h
is a bi-Lipschitz homeomorphism. On the other hand,
[\Phi^{-1}]_{C^{0,1}}=\lVert D\Phi^{-1}\rVert_{C^{0}_{b}}\text{ and }[g+h]_{C^{0,1}}=\lVert Dg+Dh\rVert_{C^{0}_{b}}
,
so the continuous map
D(\Phi+g+h)
is everywhere invertible, and so
\Phi+g+h
is then a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism by the inverse function theorem.

It remains to prove the bound (
D.1.2
). To this end first note that
\lVert D(g+h)(D\Phi)^{-1}\rVert_{C^{0}_{b}}\leqslant[{\Phi^{-1}}]_{C^{0,1}}[{g+h}]_{C^{0,1}}<2^{-1}

From this we deduce that

(D.1.3)

\lVert D(\Phi+g+h)\rVert_{C^{0}_{b}}\leqslant\left\lVert D\Phi\right\rVert_{C^{0}_{b}}\lVert 1+D(g+h)(D\Phi)^{-1}\rVert_{C^{0}_{b}}\leqslant 3\cdot 2^{-1}\left\lVert D\Phi\right\rVert_{C^{0}_{b}}

and

(D.1.4)

\lVert D(\Phi+g+h)^{-1}\rVert_{C^{0}_{b}}=\lVert(D\Phi)^{-1}(1+D(g+h)(D\Phi)^{-1})^{-1}\rVert_{C^{0}_{b}}\\
\leqslant\lVert D\Phi^{-1}\rVert_{C^{0}_{b}}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\lVert((D(g+h)(D\Phi)^{-1})^{-1})^{m}\rVert_{C^{0}_{b}}<\lVert D\Phi^{-1}\rVert_{C^{0}_{b}}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{m}}=2\lVert D\Phi^{-1}\rVert_{C^{0}_{b}}.

These prove (
D.1.2
).
∎

The following is a modification of the main argument presented in Inci, Kappeler, and Topalov
[
46
]
.

Theorem D.2
.

Let
\mathbb{N}\ni k\geqslant 2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Suppose that
\Phi:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a bi-Lipschitz homeomorphism and a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism such that
D\Phi\in W^{k-1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
. Let
\delta>0
be determined by
n
and
\Phi
as in Lemma
D.1
. Define the map

(D.1.5)

\Lambda:H^{m+k}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{d})\times(B_{W^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor,\infty}}(0,\delta)\cap W^{k,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n}))\times(B_{H^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\delta)\cap H^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n}))\\
\to H^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{d})

via
\Lambda(f,g,h)=f(\Phi+g+h)
. Then the following hold.

(1)

\Lambda
is well-defined and continuous.

(2)

If
m=1
, then
\Lambda
is
C^{1}
and satisfies

(D.1.6)

D\Lambda(f,g,h)(f_{1},g_{1},h_{1})=Df(\Phi+g+h)(g_{1}+h_{1})+f_{1}(\Phi+g+h).

(3)

If
m\geqslant 2
, then
\Lambda
is
C^{m}
and satisfies

(D.1.7)

D^{m}\Lambda(f,g,h)[(f_{1},g_{1},h_{1}),\dots,(f_{m},g_{m},h_{m})]=D^{m}f(\Phi+g+h)(g_{i}+h_{i})_{i=1}^{m}\\
+\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}D^{m-1}f_{\ell}(\Phi+g+h)(g_{i}+h_{i})_{i\neq\ell}.

Proof.

We divide the proof into steps.

Step 1
: Preliminary observations. For any
f
,
g
, and
h
in the domain of
\Lambda
we have that
\max\{\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{W^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor,\infty}},\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{H^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}\}<\delta
. Consequently, Lemma
D.1
implies that
\Phi+g+h
is a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism and that

(D.1.8)

\lVert D(\Phi+g+h)\rVert_{C^{0}_{b}}+\lVert D(\Phi+g+h)^{-1}\rVert_{C^{0}_{b}}\leqslant A_{0},

where
A_{0}>0
is a constant depending on
\Phi
. Throughout the rest of the proof, when we write
\lesssim
we allow for the implicit constant to depend on
A_{0}
, and thus on
\Phi
.

Step 2
: Well-definedness and continuity. We now aim to prove that
\Lambda
is actually well-defined, i.e. takes values in
H^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and is a continuous map. It suffices to prove this when
m=0
, as the cases
m\in\{1,2\}
follow from this case. To prove this, we proceed by finite induction on
0\leqslant j\leqslant k
. For such
j
let
\mathbb{P}_{j}
denote the proposition that

(D.1.9)

\Lambda:H^{m+j}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{d})\times(B_{W^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor,\infty}}(0,\delta)\cap W^{k,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n}))\times(B_{H^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}(0,\delta)\cap H^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n}))\\
\to H^{j}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{d})

is well-defined and continuous and obeys the estimate

(D.1.10)

\left\lVert\Lambda(f,g,h)\right\rVert_{H^{j}}\lesssim\langle\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{W^{k,\infty}},\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{H^{k}}\rangle^{j}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{H^{j}}.

Consider the base case,
j=0
. A change of variables and the bound (
D.1.8
) allow us to estimate

(D.1.11)

\left\lVert\Lambda(f,g,h)\right\rVert_{H^{0}}=\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left|f\circ(\Phi+g+h)\right|^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\leqslant\lVert\det\nabla(\Phi+g+h)^{-1}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}^{1/2}\lVert f\rVert_{H^{0}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{H^{0}}.

This shows that
\Lambda
is well-defined and establishes (
D.1.10
) when
j=0
. Next we bound

(D.1.12)

\lVert\Lambda(f,g,h)-\Lambda(\widetilde{f},\widetilde{g},\widetilde{h})\rVert_{H^{0}}\leqslant\lVert\Lambda(f,g,h)-\Lambda(f,\widetilde{g},\widetilde{h})\rVert_{H^{0}}+\lVert\Lambda(f-\widetilde{f},\widetilde{g},\widetilde{h})\rVert_{H^{0}}.

For the latter term we use (
D.1.11
) to see that
\lim_{(\widetilde{f},\widetilde{g},\widetilde{h})\to(f,g,h)}\lVert\Lambda(f-\widetilde{f},\widetilde{g},\widetilde{h})\rVert_{H^{0}}=0
,
while for the former we use the density of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{d})
in
H^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{d})
together with the fact that if
(\widetilde{g},\widetilde{h})\to(g,h)
then
\Phi+\widetilde{g}+\widetilde{h}\to\Phi+g+h
uniformly to deduce that
\lim_{(\widetilde{f},\widetilde{g},\widetilde{h})\to(f,g,h)}\lVert\Lambda(f,g,h)-\Lambda(f,\widetilde{g},\widetilde{h})\rVert_{H^{0}}=0
.
Thus,
\Lambda
is continuous when
j=0
, and we find that
\mathbb{P}_{0}
holds.

Proceeding inductively, we now suppose that
\mathbb{P}_{\ell}
holds for all
0\leqslant\ell\leqslant j\leqslant k-1
and consider the case
j+1\leqslant k
. Let
(f,g,h)
be in the domain of
\Lambda
in this case. For any
1\leqslant a\leqslant n
we compute

(D.1.13)

\partial_{a}\Lambda(f,g,h)=\sum_{b=1}^{n}\partial_{b}f\circ(\Phi+g+h)\partial_{a}(\Phi+g+h)_{b}=\sum_{b=1}^{n}\Lambda(\partial_{b}f,g,h)\partial_{a}(\Phi+g+h)_{b}

in order to exploit the induction hypothesis and a product estimate (see Corollary
D.7
) to bound

(D.1.14)

\left\lVert\Lambda(f,g,h)\right\rVert_{H^{j+1}}\lesssim\lVert\Lambda(f,g,h)\rVert_{H^{0}}+\sum_{a=1}^{n}\lVert\partial_{a}\Lambda(f,g,h)\rVert_{H^{j}}\\
\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{H^{0}}+\sum_{a,b=1}^{n}\lVert\Lambda(\partial_{b}f,g,h)\partial_{a}(\Phi+g+h)_{b}\rVert_{H^{j}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{H^{0}}+\langle\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{W^{k,\infty}},\lVert h\rVert_{H^{k}}\rangle\sum_{b=1}^{n}\lVert\Lambda(\partial_{b}f,g,h)\rVert_{H^{j}}\\
\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{H^{0}}+\langle\lVert g\rVert_{W^{k,\infty}},\lVert h\rVert_{H^{k}}\rangle\langle\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{W^{k,\infty}},\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{H^{k}}\rangle^{j}\lVert f\rVert_{H^{j+1}}\lesssim\langle\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{W^{k,\infty}},\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{H^{k}}\rangle^{j+1}\lVert f\rVert_{H^{j+1}},

which shows well-definedness and the bound (
D.1.10
) for
j+1
.

Next, we establish the continuity assertion of
\mathbb{P}_{j+1}
. We initially compute

(D.1.15)

\partial_{a}(\Lambda(f,g,h)-\Lambda(\widetilde{f},\widetilde{g},\widetilde{h}))=\sum_{b=1}^{n}(\Lambda(\partial_{b}f,g,h)-\Lambda(\partial_{b}\widetilde{f},\widetilde{g},\widetilde{h}))\partial_{a}(\Phi+g+h)_{b}\\
+\sum_{b=1}^{n}\Lambda(\partial_{b}\widetilde{f},\widetilde{g},\widetilde{h})\partial_{a}(g-\widetilde{g}+h-\widetilde{h})_{b}.

Again using basic product estimates (see Corollary
D.7
), we may deduce from this that

(D.1.16)

\lVert\Lambda(f,g,h)-\Lambda(\widetilde{f},\widetilde{g},\widetilde{h})\rVert_{H^{j+1}}\lesssim\lVert\Lambda(f,g,h)-\Lambda(\widetilde{f},\widetilde{g},\widetilde{h})\rVert_{H^{0}}+\sum_{a=1}^{n}\lVert\partial_{a}\Lambda(f,g,h)-\partial_{a}\Lambda(\widetilde{f},\widetilde{g},\widetilde{h})\rVert_{H^{j}}\lesssim\\
\langle\left\lVert g,h\right\rVert_{W^{k,\infty}\times H^{k}}\rangle\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|\leqslant 1}\lVert\Lambda(\partial^{\alpha}f,g,h)-\Lambda(\partial^{\alpha}\widetilde{f},\widetilde{g},\widetilde{h})\rVert_{H^{j}}+\lVert g-\widetilde{g},h-\widetilde{h}\rVert_{W^{k,\infty}\times H^{k}}\sum_{b=1}^{n}\lVert\Lambda(\partial_{b}\widetilde{f},\widetilde{g},\widetilde{h})\rVert_{H^{j}},

and this bound and the induction hypothesis readily imply the continuity assertion of
\mathbb{P}_{j+1}.
Thus
\mathbb{P}_{j+1}
holds, and the induction argument is complete.

Step 3
: Continuous differentiability when
m=1
. Next we aim to prove that
\Lambda
is
C^{1}
when
m=1
. To this end fix
(f,g,h)
in the domain of
\Lambda
and pick
r>0
such that
r+\max\{\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{W^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor,\infty}},\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{H^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}\}<\delta
.
Then
(f,g,h)+t(f_{1},g_{1},h_{1})
belongs to the domain of
\Lambda
for every
t\in[-1,1]
and
f_{1}\in H^{2+k}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
g_{1}\in W^{k,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and
h_{1}\in H^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
\max\{\left\lVert g_{1}\right\rVert_{W^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor,\infty}},\left\lVert h_{1}\right\rVert_{H^{2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}\}<r
. By using the fundamental theorem of calculus, we may compute

(D.1.17)

\Lambda(f+f_{1},g+g_{1},h+h_{1})-\Lambda(f,g,h)-\Lambda(f_{1},g,h)-\sum_{b=1}^{n}\Lambda(\partial_{b}f,g,h)(g_{1}+h_{1})_{b}=\mathcal{R}_{1}+\mathcal{R}_{2}

for remainder terms

(D.1.18)

\mathcal{R}_{1}=\sum_{b=1}^{n}(g_{1}+h_{1})_{b}\int_{0}^{1}\left(\Lambda(\partial_{b}f,g+tg_{1},h+th_{1})-\Lambda(\partial_{b}f,g,h)\right)dt

and

(D.1.19)

\mathcal{R}_{2}=\sum_{b=1}^{n}(g_{1}+h_{1})_{b}\int_{0}^{1}\Lambda(\partial_{b}f_{1},g+tg_{1},h+th_{1})dt.

Using the results of the previous step, we readily deduce from these expressions that

(D.1.20)

\frac{\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{1}+\mathcal{R}_{2}\right\rVert_{H^{k}}}{\left\lVert f_{1}\right\rVert_{H^{1+k}}+\left\lVert g_{1}\right\rVert_{W^{k,\infty}}+\left\lVert h_{1}\right\rVert_{H^{k}}}\to 0\text{ as }(f_{1},g_{1},h_{1})\to 0.

Hence,
\Lambda
is differentiable on its domain, and

(D.1.21)

D\Lambda(f,g,h)(f_{1},g_{1},h_{1})=\Lambda(f_{1},g,h)+\sum_{b=1}^{n}\Lambda(\partial_{b}f,g,h)(g_{1}+h_{1})_{b},

which may be rewritten as (
D.1.6
). On the other hand, the expression for
D\Lambda(f,g,h)
in terms of
\Lambda
shows that
D\Lambda
is continuous on its domain when
m=1
, and so
\Lambda
is actually
C^{1}
in this case.

Step 4
:
m^{\mathrm{th}}
-order continuous differentiability when
m\geqslant 2
. Now assume that
m\geqslant 2
. In this case, the result of the third step still shows that
\Lambda
is
C^{1}
on its domain with the same expression for
D\Lambda
given in (
D.1.21
). However, since
D\Lambda
can be expressed in terms of standard products and
\Lambda
, we iteratively deduce that
\Lambda
is actually
C^{2}
when
m=2
,
C^{3}
when
m=3
, etc., and that the formula (
D.1.7
) holds for any such
m
.
∎

We also need a variant of Theorem
D.2
in which the outer composition map is a fixed element of a
C^{k}_{b}-
type space, and we are considering smoothness as a mapping into the space of Sobolev multipliers. This latter space is defined as follows. For
W\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
an open set and
s\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the space of Sobolev multipliers
\mathcal{M}(H^{s}(W))
to be the set of bounded linear maps
L\in\mathcal{L}(H^{s}(W))
for which there exists
m\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(W)
such that
Lf=mf
for all
f\in H^{s}(W)
. We endow this space with the natural operator norm:
\lVert m\rVert_{\mathcal{M}(H^{s})}=\sup_{\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s}}\leqslant 1}\lVert mf\rVert_{H^{s}}
. It turns out that
\mathcal{M}(H^{s}(W))
is a closed subspace of
\mathcal{L}(H^{s}(W))
, and is thus complete, and that
\mathcal{M}(H^{s}(W))\subset H^{s}_{\mathrm{loc}}(W)
. For the proofs of these facts and more information on the spaces of Sobolev multipliers, we refer the reader to the book by Maz’ya and Shaposhnikova
[
77
]
.

Theorem D.3
.

Let
\mathbb{N}\ni k\geqslant 2+\lfloor n/2\rfloor
,
\varnothing\neq V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be open, and
\varnothing\neq O\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a Stein-extension domain (see Definition
A.2
). Suppose that
\varnothing\neq U\subseteq W^{k,\infty}(O;\mathbb{R}^{d})\times H^{k}(O;\mathbb{R}^{d})
is an open set with the property that if
(g,h)\in U
, then
(g+h)(O)\subseteq V
. For any
f\in C^{k}_{b}(V)
define the function
\Theta(f;g,h):O\to\mathbb{R}
via
\Theta(f;g,h)=f(g+h)
. Then the following hold for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
f\in C^{m+k}_{b}(V)
.

(1)

For each
(g,h)\in U
, the function
\Theta(f;g,h)
defines an element of
\mathcal{M}(H^{k}(O))
. Moreover, the induced map
\Theta(f;\cdot):U\to\mathcal{M}(H^{k}(O))
is
C^{m}
.

(2)

If
m\geqslant 1
, then the induced map
\Theta(f;\cdot):U\to\mathcal{M}(H^{k}(O))
satisfies

(D.1.22)

D^{m}\Theta(f;g,h)[(g_{1},h_{1}),\dotsc,(g_{m},h_{m})]=D^{m}f(g+h)[(g_{1},h_{1}),\dotsc,(g_{m},h_{m})].

Proof.

We divide the proof into three steps.

Step 1
: Well-definedness and continuity when
m=0
. Suppose that
m=0
. For
0\leqslant j\leqslant k
let
\mathbb{P}_{j}
denote the proposition that if
f\in C^{j}_{b}(V)
, then
\Theta(f;\cdot):U\to\mathcal{M}(H^{j}(O))
is well-defined, continuous, and obeys the estimate

(D.1.23)

\left\lVert\Theta(f;g,h)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{M}(H^{j})}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{C^{j}_{b}}\langle\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{W^{k,\infty}},\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{H^{k}}\rangle^{j-1}.

We will employ a finite induction to show that
\mathbb{P}_{j}
holds for all
0\leqslant j\leqslant k
, which then proves the first item when
m=0
.

Consider the base case,
j=0
. Then for
\varphi\in H^{0}(O)
we can trivially bound
\left\lVert\Theta(f;g,h)\varphi\right\rVert_{H^{0}}\leqslant\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{C^{0}_{b}}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{H^{0}}
in order to see that
\Theta(f;g,h)\in\mathcal{M}(H^{0}(O))
with
\left\lVert\Theta(f;g,h)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{M}(H^{0})}\leqslant\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{C^{0}_{b}}
. Thus,
\Theta(f;\cdot)
is well-defined and obeys the bound (
D.1.23
) when
j=0
. To prove continuity, we note that for
(g,h),(\overline{g},\overline{h})\in U
we may argue as above to bound
\lVert\Theta(f;g,h)-\Theta(f;\overline{g},\overline{h})\rVert_{\mathcal{M}(H^{0})}\leqslant\lVert f(g+h)-f(\overline{g}+\overline{h})\rVert_{C^{0}_{b}}
, but since
k>n/2
, convergence in
W^{k,\infty}\times H^{k}
implies uniform convergence, and we deduce that
\lim_{(\overline{g},\overline{h})\to(g,h)}\lVert\Theta(f;g,h)-\Theta(f;\overline{g},\overline{h})\rVert_{\mathcal{M}(H^{0})}=0
. This establishes the continuity of
\Theta(f;\cdot)
in
U
, and so
\mathbb{P}_{0}
is proved.

Now suppose that
\mathbb{P}_{\ell}
holds for all
0\leqslant\ell\leqslant j\leqslant k-1
and suppose that
f\in C^{j+1}_{b}(V)
. Then for
\varphi\in H^{j+1}(O)
and
(g,h)\in U
we have that

(D.1.24)

\left\lVert\Theta(f;g,h)\varphi\right\rVert_{H^{j+1}}\lesssim\left\lVert\Theta(f;g,h)\varphi\right\rVert_{H^{0}}+\sum_{a=1}^{n}\left\lVert\partial_{a}[\Theta(f;g,h)\varphi]\right\rVert_{H^{j+1}}\\
\lesssim\left\lVert\Theta(f;g,h)\varphi\right\rVert_{H^{0}}+\sum_{a=1}^{n}\left\lVert\partial_{a}[\Theta(f;g,h)]\varphi\right\rVert_{H^{j}}+\sum_{a=1}^{n}\left\lVert\Theta(f;g,h)\partial_{a}\varphi\right\rVert_{H^{j}}.

By the induction hypothesis, we have the bounds
\left\lVert\Theta(f;g,h)\varphi\right\rVert_{H^{0}}\leqslant\left\lVert\Theta(f;g,h)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{M}(H^{0})}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{H^{0}}
and
\sum_{a=1}^{n}\left\lVert\Theta(f;g,h)\partial_{a}\varphi\right\rVert_{H^{j}}\lesssim\left\lVert\Theta(f;g,h)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{M}(H^{j})}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{H^{j+1}}
. On the other hand,

(D.1.25)

\partial_{a}[\Theta(f;g,h)]=\sum_{b=1}^{n}\partial_{b}f(g+h)\partial_{a}(g+h)_{b}=\sum_{b=1}^{n}\Theta(\partial_{b}f,g,h)\partial_{a}(g+h)_{b},

so we may again use the induction hypothesis to bound

(D.1.26)

\sum_{a=1}^{n}\left\lVert\partial_{a}[\Theta(f;g,h)]\varphi\right\rVert_{H^{j}}\lesssim\sum_{a,b=1}^{n}\left\lVert\Theta(\partial_{b}f,g,h)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{M}(H^{j})}\left\lVert\partial_{a}(g+h)_{b}\varphi\right\rVert_{H^{j}}\\
\lesssim\sum_{b=1}^{n}\left\lVert\Theta(\partial_{b}f,g,h)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{M}(H^{j})}\left(\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{W^{k,\infty}}+\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{H^{k}}\right)\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{H^{j}}.

Upon combining these bounds and again using the induction hypothesis, we find that
\Theta(f;g,h)\in\mathcal{M}(H^{j+1}(O))
with

(D.1.27)

\left\lVert\Theta(f;g,h)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{M}(H^{j})}\lesssim\left\lVert\Theta(f;g,h)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{M}(H^{0})}+\left\lVert\Theta(f;g,h)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{M}(H^{j})}\\
+\sum_{b=1}^{n}\left\lVert\Theta(\partial_{b}f,g,h)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{M}(H^{j})}\left(\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{W^{k,\infty}}+\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{H^{k}}\right)\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{C^{j}_{b}}\left\langle\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{W^{k,\infty}},\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{H^{k}}\right\rangle^{j-1}\\
+\sum_{b=1}^{n}\left\lVert\partial_{b}f\right\rVert_{C^{j}_{b}}\left\langle\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{W^{k,\infty}}+\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{H^{k}}\right\rangle^{j}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{C^{j+1}_{b}}\left\langle\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{W^{k,\infty}},\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{H^{k}}\right\rangle^{(j+1)-1}.

This shows that
\Theta(f;\cdot)
is well-defined on
U
and obeys (
D.1.23
) in the case
j+1
.

To prove
\mathbb{P}_{j+1},
it remains only to establish continuity. For
(g,h),(\overline{g},\overline{h})\in U
and
\varphi\in H^{j+1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
we argue as above to bound

(D.1.28)

\lVert[\Theta(f;g,h)-\Theta(f;\overline{g},\overline{h})]\varphi\rVert_{H^{j+1}}\lesssim\lVert\Theta(f;g,h)-\Theta(f;\overline{g},\overline{h})\rVert_{\mathcal{M}(H^{0})}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{0}}\\
+\sum_{a=1}^{n}\lVert\Theta(f;g,h)-\Theta(f;\overline{g},\overline{h})\rVert_{\mathcal{M}(H^{j})}\lVert\partial_{a}\varphi\rVert_{H^{j}}+\sum_{a=1}^{n}\lVert\partial_{a}[\Theta(f;g,h)-\Theta(f;\overline{g},\overline{h})]\varphi\rVert_{H^{j}}.

The first two terms here are easy to deal with, but we must rewrite the third:
\partial_{a}[\Theta(f;g,h)-\Theta(f;\overline{g},\overline{h})]=\sum_{b=1}^{n}[\Theta(\partial_{b}f;g,h)-\Theta(\partial_{b}f;\overline{g},\overline{h})]\partial_{a}(g+h)_{b}+\sum_{b=1}^{n}\Theta(\partial_{b}f,\overline{g},\overline{h})\partial_{a}(g+h-\overline{g}-\overline{h})_{b}
,
which then allows us to bound

(D.1.29)

\sum_{a=1}^{n}\lVert\partial_{a}[\Theta(f;g,h)-\Theta(f;\overline{g},\overline{h})]\varphi\rVert_{H^{j}}\lesssim\sum_{a,b=1}^{n}\lVert\Theta(\partial_{b}f;g,h)-\Theta(\partial_{b}f;\overline{g},\overline{h})\rVert_{\mathcal{M}(H^{j})}\lVert\partial_{a}(g+h)_{b}\varphi\rVert_{H^{j}}\\
+\sum_{a,b=1}^{n}\lVert\Theta(\partial_{b}f;\overline{g},\overline{h})\rVert_{\mathcal{M}(H^{j})}\lVert\partial_{a}(g+h-\overline{g}-\overline{h})_{b}\varphi\rVert_{H^{j}}\\
\lesssim\sum_{b=1}^{n}\lVert\Theta(\partial_{b}f;g,h)-\Theta(\partial_{b}f;\overline{g},\overline{h})\rVert_{\mathcal{M}(H^{j})}\left(\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{W^{k,\infty}}+\lVert h\rVert_{H^{k}}\right)\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{H^{j}}\\
+\sum_{b=1}^{n}\left\lVert\Theta(\partial_{b}f;\overline{g},\overline{h})\right\rVert_{\mathcal{M}(H^{j})}\left(\left\lVert g-\overline{g}\right\rVert_{W^{k,\infty}}+\left\lVert h-\overline{h}\right\rVert_{H^{k}}\right)\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{H^{j}}.

Hence,

(D.1.30)

\left\lVert[\Theta(f;g,h)-\Theta(f;\overline{g},\overline{h})]\right\rVert_{\mathcal{M}(H^{j+1})}\lesssim\left\lVert\Theta(f;g,h)-\Theta(f;\overline{g},\overline{h})\right\rVert_{\mathcal{M}(H^{0})}\\
+\left\lVert\Theta(f;g,h)-\Theta(f;\overline{g},\overline{h})\right\rVert_{\mathcal{M}(H^{j})}+\left(\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{W^{k,\infty}}+\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{H^{k}}\right)\sum_{b=1}^{n}\left\lVert\Theta(\partial_{b}f;g,h)-\Theta(\partial_{b}f;\overline{g},\overline{h})\right\rVert_{\mathcal{M}(H^{j})}\\
+\left(\left\lVert g-\overline{g}\right\rVert_{W^{k,\infty}}+\left\lVert h-\overline{h}\right\rVert_{H^{k}}\right)\sum_{b=1}^{n}\left\lVert\Theta(\partial_{b}f;\overline{g},\overline{h})\right\rVert_{\mathcal{M}(H^{j})},

and we readily deduce from this bound and the induction hypothesis that
\Theta(f;\cdot)
is continuous on
U
when
f\in C^{j+1}_{b}(V)
. Thus,
\mathbb{P}_{j+1}
holds, and the finite induction argument is complete.

Step 2
:
C^{1}
when
f\in C^{1+k}_{b}(V)
